1When one mole of monoatomic ideal gas at T K undergoes reversible adiabatic change under a constant external pressure of 1 atm changes volume from 1 litre to 2 litre. The final temperature in Kelvin would be:
(a) T/22/3   (b) T + 2/(3×0.0821)   (c) T   (d) T − 3/(2×0.0821)		Irreversible adiabatic against constant PAn ideal gas undergoes adiabatic expansion against constant external pressure. Which of the foll....🟢 Exacta⚠
Concept The wording collides with itself: "reversible adiabatic" and "under a constant external pressure" describe two different paths. For a reversible adiabatic change TVγ−1 is constant; for an irreversible one against a fixed Pext the energy balance nCVΔT = −PextΔV must be used instead.
Key relation Reversible: T1V1γ−1 = T2V2γ−1  |  Irreversible: nCV(T2 − T1) = −Pext(V2 − V1) For a monatomic gas γ = 5/3 and CV = (3/2)R.
Solution
1. Reversible route. With γ − 1 = 2/3, T2 = T1(V1/V2)2/3 = T(1/2)2/3 = T/22/3, which is option (a).
2. Irreversible route. Adiabatic means q = 0, so ΔU = w and (3/2)R(T2 − T) = −Pext(V2 − V1) for one mole.
3. Substituting Pext = 1 atm and ΔV = 2 − 1 = 1 L gives (3/2)R(T2 − T) = −1 L·atm.
4. With R = 0.0821 L atm K−1 mol−1, T2 = T − 2/(3 × 0.0821) = T − 8.12 K.
5. Option (d) prints T − 3/(2 × 0.0821), which is the same structure with the 2 and 3 swapped — it does not equal the correct irreversible result.
6. So the only internally consistent choice is (a), read as the reversible answer; the phrase "under a constant external pressure" is a distractor that no printed option correctly serves.
 reversible:   T2 = T (V1/V2)^(g-1) = T (1/2)^(2/3)
               = T / 2^(2/3)              <- (a)

 irreversible: (3/2)R(T2 - T) = -Pext dV
               T2 = T - 2/(3 x 0.0821)
                  = T - 8.12 K
 printed (d) = T - 3/(2 x 0.0821): not this
⚡ JEE Trick Decide the path first. "Reversible adiabatic" means use TVγ−1; "against constant external pressure" means use nCVΔT = −PextΔV. The two never give the same answer.
Answer: (a) T/22/3 (reversible reading)  —  the irreversible path gives T − 2/(3×0.0821) ⚠
2Standard entropy X2, Y2 and XY3 are 60, 40 and 50 J K−1 mol−1, respectively. For the reaction ½X2 + (3/2)Y2 → XY3, ΔH = −30 kJ, to be at equilibrium, the temperature will be:
(a) 1000 K   (b) 1250 K   (c) 500 K   (d) 750 K		Equilibrium temperature ΔH/ΔSCalculating the Temperature at Which a Reaction Becomes Spontaneous🟡 Similard
Concept Equilibrium means ΔG = 0, so the temperature is fixed by the ratio ΔH/ΔS. The entropy change must first be assembled from the tabulated standard entropies using products minus reactants with the fractional coefficients intact.
Key relation ΔS = S(XY3) − ½S(X2) − (3/2)S(Y2); T = ΔH/ΔS Both ΔH and ΔS turn out negative, so their ratio is a positive temperature.
Solution
1. Reaction: ½X2 + (3/2)Y2 → XY3, with S° = 60, 40 and 50 J K−1 mol−1 for X2, Y2 and XY3.
2. Entropy change: ΔS = 50 − [½(60) + (3/2)(40)] = 50 − [30 + 60] = 50 − 90 = −40 J K−1 mol−1.
3. The sign is sensible: two moles of gas become one, so disorder falls.
4. Set ΔG = 0: T = ΔH/ΔS = (−30000 J)/(−40 J K−1).
5. Evaluate: T = 750 K, which is option (d).
6. Check the physics: both ΔH and ΔS are negative, so this is the "spontaneous at low temperature" family — the synthesis runs below 750 K and reverses above it.
7. Verify at 500 K: ΔG = −30000 + 500(40) = −10 kJ, spontaneous. At 1000 K: ΔG = −30000 + 40000 = +10 kJ, non-spontaneous. The switch sits at 750 K as found.
 1/2 X2 + 3/2 Y2 -> XY3      dH = -30 kJ

 dS = 50 - [0.5(60) + 1.5(40)]
    = 50 - 90 = -40 J/K/mol

 dG = 0 -> T = dH/dS = -30000 / -40 = 750 K
 both -ve: spontaneous BELOW 750 K
⚡ JEE Trick Apply the fractional coefficients to the entropies before subtracting. Forgetting the 3/2 on Y2 here gives ΔS = −20 and doubles the answer to 1500 K.
Answer: (d) 750 K
3A piston filled with 0.04 mole of an ideal gas expands reversibly from 50.0 mL to 375 mL at a constant temperature of 37.0°C. As it does so, it absorbs 208 J of heat. The values of q and w for the process will be: (R = 8.314 J/mol K) (ln 7.5 = 2.01)
(a) q = −208 J, w = +208 J   (b) q = +208 J, w = +208 J   (c) q = +208 J, w = −208 J   (d) q = −208 J, w = −208 J		Isothermal reversible q and wThermodynamics || Calculation of W, q, U, H for reversible isothermal expansion process🟡 Similarc
Concept An isothermal change of an ideal gas has ΔU = 0, so the first law forces q and w to be equal in magnitude and opposite in sign. Once the sign convention is fixed, the numbers follow without any real calculation.
Key relation Isothermal ideal gas: ΔU = 0 ⇒ q = −w Heat absorbed is positive; work done by the system is negative in the ΔU = q + w convention.
Solution
1. The process is isothermal at 37.0°C and the gas is ideal, so the internal energy does not change: ΔU = 0.
2. First law: ΔU = q + w, so 0 = q + w and therefore w = −q.
3. The gas absorbs 208 J, so q = +208 J, and hence w = −208 J — option (c).
4. The sign of w is right in the physical sense too: the gas expands from 50.0 mL to 375 mL, so it does work on the surroundings and w must be negative.
5. Verify the magnitude independently: w = −nRT ln(V2/V1) = −(0.04)(8.314)(310)(ln 7.5) = −(0.04)(8.314)(310)(2.01) = −207.2 J, matching 208 J to rounding.
6. Options (a) and (d) put q negative, contradicting "absorbs"; option (b) has q and w with the same sign, which would make ΔU = 416 J, impossible at constant T.
7. Note also that ΔH = 0 here for the same reason as ΔU: for an ideal gas both depend only on temperature, and the temperature is held fixed throughout.
8. The volume ratio 375/50 = 7.5 is exactly why the question supplies ln 7.5 = 2.01 — it is the only logarithm needed for the independent check.
 isothermal ideal gas: dU = 0
 dU = q + w = 0   ->   w = -q

 absorbs 208 J -> q = +208 J, w = -208 J

 check: w = -nRT ln(V2/V1)
      = -(0.04)(8.314)(310)(2.01) = -207 J
⚡ JEE Trick In any isothermal ideal-gas question, write ΔU = 0 first. The answer is then just q and −q, and the only decision left is which one is positive.
Answer: (c) q = +208 J, w = −208 J
4Assuming that water vapours are ideal gas, the change in internal energy (ΔU) when 1 mol of water is vaporized at 1 bar pressure and 100°C. (Given molar enthalpy of vaporization at 1 bar and 373 K is 41 kJ mol−1 and R = 8.3 J mol−1 K−1) will be:
(a) 41.00 kJ mol−1   (b) 4.100 kJ mol−1   (c) 3.7904 kJ mol−1   (d) 37.904 kJ mol−1		ΔU = ΔH − ΔngRTMeasurements of ∆U and ∆H || Calorimetery || Thermodynamics Part - 5 || Class-11 | Chemistry🟡 Similard
Concept Vaporisation at constant pressure converts a liquid into a gas, so one mole of gas appears and Δng = +1. Part of the supplied enthalpy is spent pushing back the atmosphere, so the internal energy change is smaller than the enthalpy change by exactly RT.
Key relation ΔU = ΔH − ΔngRT Liquid volume is negligible against gas volume, so only the vapour counts in Δng.
Solution
1. Process: H2O(l) → H2O(g) at 373 K, ΔH = 41 kJ mol−1, R = 8.3 J mol−1 K−1.
2. Gas moles: 1 mole of vapour is formed from a liquid, so Δng = +1.
3. Correction term: ΔngRT = (1)(8.3)(373) = 3095.9 J = 3.096 kJ mol−1.
4. Substitute: ΔU = 41 − 3.096 = 37.904 kJ mol−1, which is option (d).
5. Option (a) 41.00 forgets the correction entirely; options (b) and (c) are the correction term and the answer shifted by a factor of ten.
6. Physical reading: of the 41 kJ supplied, about 3.1 kJ goes into expansion work against the 1 bar atmosphere and only 37.9 kJ is stored as internal energy in the vapour.
7. Sign convention check: Δng is positive for vaporisation, so ΔU must come out smaller than ΔH. Any answer larger than 41 kJ mol−1 would have the correction added instead of subtracted.
 H2O(l) -> H2O(g)   dn_g = +1
 dH = 41 kJ/mol     T = 373 K   R = 8.3 J/mol/K

 dn_g R T = (1)(8.3)(373) = 3096 J = 3.1 kJ
 dU = 41 - 3.1 = 37.904 kJ/mol

 41 kJ in = 37.9 kJ stored + 3.1 kJ pushing air
⚡ JEE Trick At 373 K, RT is about 3.1 kJ mol−1; at 298 K it is about 2.5 kJ mol−1. Knowing those two numbers turns every ΔH ↔ ΔU conversion into mental arithmetic.
Answer: (d) 37.904 kJ mol−1
5A heat engine carries one mole of an ideal mono-atomic gas around the cycle A → B → C → A, with T1 = 300 K at A, T2 = 600 K at B and T3 = 450 K at C (A→B isochoric, C→A isobaric). Select the correct option:
(a) qAB = 450R and qCA = −450R   (b) qAB = 450R and qCA = −225R   (c) qAB = 450R and qCA = −375R   (d) qAB = 375R and qCA = −450R		Heat along the legs of a cycle14 CYCLIC PROCESS | THERMODYNAMICS | IIT ADVANCED | JEE MAIN | CHEMISTRY CLASS 11 | OLYMPIAD🟡 Similarc
Concept Each leg of a cycle has its own heat expression. An isochoric leg exchanges q = nCVΔT because no work is done; an isobaric leg exchanges q = nCPΔT because the gas must also push back the surroundings.
Key relation Isochoric: q = nCVΔT = (3/2)RΔT  |  Isobaric: q = nCPΔT = (5/2)RΔT For one mole of a monatomic ideal gas, CV = (3/2)R and CP = (5/2)R.
Solution
1. Path A → B is the vertical line on the P–V plot, so the volume is fixed: it is isochoric, running from T1 = 300 K to T2 = 600 K.
2. Heat for that leg: qAB = CVΔT = (3/2)R(600 − 300) = (3/2)R(300) = 450R. Positive, so heat is absorbed.
3. Path C → A is the horizontal line, so the pressure is fixed: it is isobaric, running from T3 = 450 K back to T1 = 300 K.
4. Heat for that leg: qCA = CPΔT = (5/2)R(300 − 450) = (5/2)R(−150) = −375R. Negative, so heat is released.
5. The pair (450R, −375R) is option (c).
6. Distractor logic: option (a) uses CV on both legs, option (b) halves the second, and option (d) swaps CV and CP between the two legs. Only (c) matches each leg to its own heat capacity.
 A->B  vertical on P-V  =>  isochoric
   q = Cv dT = (3/2)R(600-300) = +450R

 C->A  horizontal on P-V =>  isobaric
   q = Cp dT = (5/2)R(300-450) = -375R
 ---------------------------------------
 (450R, -375R)  = option (c)
⚡ JEE Trick Read the geometry before the numbers: a vertical line on P–V is constant volume (use CV), a horizontal line is constant pressure (use CP). Matching the capacity to the leg is the whole question.
Answer: (c) qAB = 450R and qCA = −375R
610 litre of a non-linear polyatomic ideal gas at 127°C and 2 atm pressure is suddenly released to 1 atm pressure and the gas expanded adiabatically against constant external pressure; the final temperature and volume of the gas respectively are:
(a) T = 350 K; V = 17.5 L   (b) T = 300 K; V = 15 L   (c) T = 250 K; V = 12.5 L   (d) None of these		Irreversible adiabatic expansionAn ideal gas undergoes adiabatic expansion against constant external pressure. Which of the foll....🟡 Similara
Concept A sudden release to a lower pressure is an irreversible adiabatic expansion, so PVγ = constant does not apply. The energy balance nCVΔT = −PextΔV must be solved instead, with the unknown final volume written through the ideal gas law.
Key relation nCV(T2 − T1) = −P2(V2 − V1), with V2 = nRT2/P2 A non-linear polyatomic gas has 6 active degrees of freedom, so CV = 3R.
Solution
1. Data: V1 = 10 L, T1 = 127°C = 400 K, P1 = 2 atm, P2 = Pext = 1 atm. Non-linear polyatomic gives CV = 3R.
2. Rather than find n, keep the group nR = P1V1/T1 = (2)(10)/400 = 0.05 L atm K−1.
3. Write the balance: 3nR(T2 − 400) = −1 × (nRT2/1 − 10), since V2 = nRT2/P2.
4. Expand and collect: 3nRT2 − 1200nR = −nRT2 + 10, so 4nRT2 = 1200nR + 10.
5. Substitute nR = 0.05: 0.2T2 = 60 + 10 = 70, giving T2 = 350 K.
6. Then V2 = nRT2/P2 = (0.05)(350)/1 = 17.5 L, which is option (a). Note the reversible path would have cooled the gas further, to about 331 K, so the irreversible result is correctly the warmer one.
 non-linear polyatomic: Cv = 3R
 nR = P1V1/T1 = (2)(10)/400 = 0.05 L atm/K

 3nR(T2 - 400) = -(nRT2 - 10)
 4 nR T2 = 1200 nR + 10
 0.2 T2 = 70    ->    T2 = 350 K

 V2 = nRT2/P2 = 0.05 x 350 / 1 = 17.5 L
⚡ JEE Trick Carry the product nR as a single number from P1V1/T1. It cancels the need to find n, and the whole irreversible-adiabatic problem collapses to one linear equation in T2.
Answer: (a) T = 350 K; V = 17.5 L
7Passage (i)–(v). The Gibbs–Helmholtz equation (ΔG)P,T = ΔH − TΔS relates free energy to enthalpy and entropy; ΔH changes little with temperature but TΔS changes appreciably, so spontaneity depends strongly on temperature.
(i) Why does CaCO3 decompose at high temperature but is stable at room temperature?   (ii) For X2O4(l) → 2XO2 at 25°C with ΔH = 2.0 kcal and ΔS = 20 cal K−1, the reaction would be?   (iii) For 2A + B → C at 298 K with ΔH = 100 kcal and ΔS = 0.020 kcal K−1, at what temperature does it become spontaneous?   (iv) A reaction has ΔH = −20 kcal at 200 K and is spontaneous only above it; find ΔG and ΔS at 400 K.   (v) ΔH = −15.0 kcal mol−1 and ΔS = −7.2 cal K−1 mol−1 at 300 K; find ΔG and the spontaneity.		Gibbs–Helmholtz passageThe Effect of Temperature on Spontaneity | Professor Dave & Chegg Explain🟡 Similar(i) b, (ii) a, (iii) c, (iv) d, (v) a
Concept Five short applications of ΔG = ΔH − TΔS. Each part is either a sign argument, a crossover temperature T = ΔH/ΔS, or a direct substitution; the passage's point is that only the TΔS term grows with temperature.
Key relation ΔG = ΔH − TΔS; ΔG = 0 ⇒ T = ΔH/ΔS In calorie units the numbers here stay small: TΔS at 300 K with ΔS in cal K−1 is just 300ΔS cal.
Solution
1. (i) CaCO3 → CaO + CO2 has ΔH > 0 and ΔS > 0. Only TΔS grows on heating, so decomposition begins once TΔS exceeds ΔH — option (b).
2. (ii) X2O4(l) → 2XO2 at 298 K: TΔS = 298 × 20 = 5960 cal = 5.96 kcal, so ΔG = 2.0 − 5.96 = −3.96 kcal. Negative, so (a) spontaneous.
3. (iii) 2A + B → C with ΔH = 100 kcal and ΔS = 0.020 kcal K−1: T = ΔH/ΔS = 100/0.020 = 5000 K, option (c).
4. (iv) The reaction changes verdict at 200 K, so ΔG = 0 there and ΔS = ΔH/T = −20000/200 = −100 cal K−1. At 400 K, ΔG = −20000 − 400(−100) = +20000 cal = 20 kcal — option (d).
5. Note the stem of (iv) is worded loosely: with ΔH and ΔS both negative the reaction is spontaneous below 200 K, not above, but the numbers the key wants are unaffected.
6. (v) ΔG = −15000 − 300(−7.2) = −15000 + 2160 = −12840 cal = −12.84 kcal mol−1, spontaneous — option (a).
 (i)   CaCO3 : dH>0, dS>0, T dS wins hot   (b)
 (ii)  2.0 - 298(0.020) = -3.96 kcal       (a)
 (iii) T = 100 / 0.020 = 5000 K            (c)
 (iv)  dS = -20000/200 = -100 cal/K
       dG(400) = -20000 + 40000 = +20 kcal (d)
 (v)   -15000 + 2160 = -12840 cal          (a)
⚡ JEE Trick When a stem says "spontaneous at T0 but not on the other side", it is handing you ΔG = 0 at T0. That one equation gives whichever of ΔH or ΔS is missing.
Answer: (i) b, (ii) a, (iii) c, (iv) d, (v) a
8Choose incorrect among the following: [MULTI CORRECT]
(a) The entropy of water increases from 0° to 4°C   (b) The change in entropy of 3 moles of an ideal monoatomic gas by doubling the temperature (K) at constant pressure is nearly 43.2 J K−1   (c) For the free expansion of a gas in an isolated system the internal energy remains constant   (d) The work done by a real gas in an isothermal reversible expansion is greater than that of an ideal gas		Spotting the false entropy statements19 CALCULATION OF ENTROPY | THERMODYNAMICS | IIT ADVANCED | JEE MAIN | CHEMISTRY CLASS 11🟡 Similar(d)⚠
Concept The question asks which statements are wrong. Three of them are standard results that survive checking; the fourth inverts a real-gas comparison. One more, about water between 0 and 4 °C, rests on a popular argument that does not actually hold.
Key relation Heating at constant P: ΔS = ∫CPdT/T > 0  |  Real gas: P = RT/(V−b) − a/V2 < Pideal Attractive forces lower the pressure a real gas exerts, so it does less expansion work than an ideal gas.
Solution
1. (b) For 3 mol of a monatomic gas at constant pressure with the temperature doubled, ΔS = nCP ln 2 = 3 × (5/2)(8.314) × 0.693 = 43.2 J K−1. The statement matches, so (b) is correct.
2. (c) Free expansion in an isolated system has q = 0 and w = 0, so ΔU = 0 and the internal energy is indeed constant. Correct.
3. (d) In an isothermal reversible expansion the work is −∫PdV. A real gas with attractions exerts a lower pressure than an ideal gas at the same V, so it does less work, not more. The statement is reversed, hence incorrect.
4. (a) At constant pressure dS = CPdT/T, and CP is positive for any stable phase, so warming water from 0 to 4 °C must raise its entropy. The statement is therefore correct.
5. The key nevertheless marks (a) as incorrect, following the informal argument that water contracts and becomes more "ordered" up to 4 °C. That density anomaly is real, but it does not make the CP integral negative.
6. Strictly, then, only (d) is incorrect; the key's inclusion of (a) is not defensible from the entropy definition.
 (a) S rises on heating (Cp > 0)     CORRECT
 (b) 3 x (5/2)R x ln2 = 43.2 J/K     CORRECT
 (c) free expansion: q=0, w=0, dU=0  CORRECT
 (d) real gas does LESS work         INCORRECT

 key says "a and d" ; only (d) survives
⚡ JEE Trick For real-vs-ideal work, remember that attractions hold the molecules back: a real gas pushes with less pressure and therefore delivers less expansion work than an ideal gas over the same volume change.
Answer: (d) is the incorrect statement  —  key prints ad ⚠
9Identify among the following correct statement(s): [MULTI CORRECT]
(a) As 1,3-butadiene shows resonance, the experimental standard molar enthalpy of formation is less than that calculated using bond energy data   (b) Since Δng = 0 for CO(g) + H2O(g) → CO2(g) + H2(g), the heat of reaction is the same at constant pressure or volume   (c) Heat evolved in hydrogenation of 1.0 mol of cis-2-butene is greater than that for trans-2-butene as trans has less van der Waals repulsion   (d) Adiabatic reversible process is an isoentropic process		Mixed thermochemistry statementsTricks to solve Thermochemistry problems easily | Enthalpy of formation combustion🟡 Similar(a), (b), (c), (d) — all four
Concept Four independent claims drawn from resonance, the ΔH–ΔU relation, alkene stability and adiabatic reversibility. Each has to be tested on its own terms; here all four survive.
Key relation ΔH = ΔU + ΔngRT; resonance lowers the true enthalpy; adiabatic + reversible ⇒ ΔS = 0 Three different chapters meet in one question.
Solution
1. (a) 1,3-butadiene is resonance stabilised, so its real molecule lies below the localised structure assumed by bond-energy arithmetic. The experimental ΔfH is therefore lower (more negative) than the calculated value. Correct.
2. (b) For CO(g) + H2O(g) → CO2(g) + H2(g), two moles of gas become two, so Δng = 0 and ΔH = ΔU. The heat measured at constant pressure equals that at constant volume. Correct.
3. (c) Both butenes hydrogenate to the same butane, so the one that starts higher in energy releases more heat. cis-2-butene suffers steric strain between the two methyl groups on the same side, making it less stable than trans, so its hydrogenation is more exothermic. Correct.
4. Quantitatively the difference is about 4 kJ mol−1: roughly −120 kJ mol−1 for cis against −116 kJ mol−1 for trans.
5. (d) Adiabatic means dq = 0, and reversible means dS = dqrev/T. Together they give dS = 0, so the process is isoentropic. Correct.
6. All four statements stand, so the answer is abcd.
 (a) resonance lowers real dfH        TRUE
 (b) dn_g = 0  ->  dH = dU            TRUE
 (c) cis less stable -> more heat     TRUE
 (d) adiabatic + reversible -> dS = 0 TRUE

 cis  hydrogenation ~ -120 kJ/mol
 trans hydrogenation ~ -116 kJ/mol
⚡ JEE Trick For hydrogenation heats, the less stable isomer always releases more energy because both isomers land on the same product. Steric strain in cis alkenes is the usual reason.
Answer: (a), (b), (c) and (d) — all four are correct
10Calculate the entropy change when 1 kg of water is heated from 27°C to 2000°C forming super heated steam under constant pressure. Given specific heat of water = 4180 J/kg-K and specific heat of steam = 1670 + 0.49T J/kg-K and latent heat of vaporization = 23 × 105 J/kg.Multi-step ΔS with variable CP19 CALCULATION OF ENTROPY | THERMODYNAMICS | IIT ADVANCED | JEE MAIN | CHEMISTRY CLASS 11🟡 Similar≈ 7522 J K<sup>−1</sup> (final temperature 200 °C, not the printed 2000 °C)⚠
Concept Heating a substance through a phase change needs a three-leg path: warm the liquid to its boiling point, vaporise it there, then warm the vapour. Only the middle leg uses ΔH/T; the two heating legs integrate CPdT/T, and here the steam capacity is itself a function of T.
Key relation ΔS = mc ln(T2/T1) + mL/Tb + ∫(a + bT)dT/T, with ∫(a + bT)dT/T = a ln(T2/T1) + b(T2 − T1) The bT term integrates to a plain difference, not a logarithm.
Solution
1. Leg 1, water 300 K → 373 K: ΔS1 = 4180 ln(373/300) = 4180 × 0.2178 = 910.2 J K−1.
2. Leg 2, vaporisation at 373 K: ΔS2 = 23 × 105/373 = 6166.2 J K−1. This dominates the total, as latent heats always do.
3. Leg 3, steam 373 K → Tf: ΔS3 = 1670 ln(Tf/373) + 0.49(Tf − 373).
4. Take Tf = 200 °C = 473 K: ΔS3 = 1670 ln(473/373) + 0.49(100) = 396.1 + 49 = 445.1 J K−1.
5. Total = 910.2 + 6166.2 + 445.1 = 7521.5 J K−1, which reproduces the key's 7522.50 to within rounding.
6. With the printed Tf = 2000 °C = 2273 K instead, ΔS3 = 1670 ln(6.094) + 0.49(1900) = 3018 + 931 = 3949 J K−1 and the total becomes about 11026 J K−1 — nowhere near the key. So the intended final temperature is 200 °C.
 leg 1  4180 ln(373/300)          =  910.2
 leg 2  2300000 / 373             = 6166.2
 leg 3  1670 ln(473/373)+0.49(100)=  445.1
 ------------------------------------------
 total (Tf = 200 C)               = 7521.5

 if Tf = 2000 C : leg 3 = 3949, total = 11026
⚡ JEE Trick When CP = a + bT, split the integral: the constant part gives a ln(T2/T1) and the linear part gives b(T2 − T1). Trying to log the whole expression is the standard error.
Answer: ΔS ≈ 7522 J K−1 for a final temperature of 200 °C; the printed 2000 °C would give 11026 J K−1 ⚠

Level 8

#QuestionTopicYouTubeMatchAnswer
1Passage (i)–(v). For an ideal gas, three paths A, (B+C) and (D+E) run from P1,V1,T1 to P2,V2,T1. A is a reversible isothermal expansion. (B+C) is a reversible adiabatic expansion to P3,V2,T2 then reversible heating at constant volume. (D+E) is reversible expansion at constant P1 to P1,V2,T3 then reversible cooling at constant V2.
(i) qrev for path A?   (ii) qrev for path (B+C)?   (iii) ΔS for path A?   (iv) qrev for path (D+E)?   (v) ΔS for path (D+E)?		Path dependence of q, path independence of SThermodynamics || Calculation of W, q, U, H for reversible isothermal expansion process🟡 Similar(i) d, (ii) b, (iii) a, (iv) a, (v) d⚠
Concept Three different paths join the same two states, so ΔS must be identical along all of them while q and w differ. The passage is built to make exactly that contrast, and parts (iii) and (v) are the clean demonstrations of it.
Key relation ΔS = nR ln(V2/V1) on every path; qrev(D+E) = nR(T3 − T1) = P1(V2 − V1) CP − CV = R collapses the two-leg heat into a single PV term.
Solution
1. (i) Path A is isothermal, so ΔU = 0 and qrev = −w = +nRT1 ln(V2/V1). Options (b) and (d) are printed identically, both with a minus sign — that is w, not q; the key selects (d).
2. (ii) Path B is adiabatic so q = 0; path C heats at constant volume from T2 to T1, giving q = nCV(T1 − T2) > 0. No printed option equals that; the key's (b) is dimensionally an entropy.
3. (iii) For the reversible isothermal path A, ΔS = qrev/T1 = nR ln(V2/V1) — option (a), clean and unambiguous.
4. (iv) Path D is isobaric at P1 from T1 to T3 giving q = nCP(T3−T1); path E is isochoric from T3 back to T1 giving q = nCV(T1−T3).
5. Adding: q = n(CP − CV)(T3 − T1) = nR(T3 − T1) = P1(V2 − V1), option (a). A neat cancellation.
6. (v) Entropy is a state function and the endpoints match path A, so ΔS(D+E) = nR ln(V2/V1) — option (d), the whole point of the passage.
 (i)   A isothermal: q = +nRT1 ln(V2/V1)
       printed (b) = (d), both with a minus
 (ii)  B adiabatic q=0 ; C isochoric
       q = n Cv (T1 - T2)  > 0
 (iii) dS(A) = nR ln(V2/V1)              (a)
 (iv)  n(Cp-Cv)(T3-T1) = P1(V2-V1)       (a)
 (v)   dS is a state function            (d)
⚡ JEE Trick In any multi-path passage, answer the ΔS parts first — they are the same on every path and need no work at all. Only the q and w parts require you to follow the individual legs.
Answer: (i) d, (ii) b, (iii) a, (iv) a, (v) d  —  (i) and (ii) have printed-option defects ⚠
2Passage (i)–(v). Gypsum CaSO4·2H2O(s) → CaSO4·½H2O(s) + (3/2)H2O(g). ΔfH° are −2021.0, −1575.0 and −241.8 kJ mol−1; standard entropies are 194.0, 130.0 and 188.0 J K−1 mol−1. R = 8.314 J K−1 mol−1 = 0.0831 L bar mol−1 K−1.
(i) Heat change for converting 1 kg of gypsum (molar mass 172 g mol−1)?   (ii) ΔG° at 298 K?   (iii) Value of the equilibrium constant?   (iv) Equilibrium pressure of water vapour at 298 K (antilog −3.14 = 7.24×10−4)?   (v) Is the formation of the hemihydrate at 298 K spontaneous?		Gypsum dehydration passageRelationship between Standard (Gibbs) Free energy and Equilibrium constant(k) | thermodynamics🟡 Similar(i) a, (ii) b, (iii) b, (iv) d, (v) b
Concept Gypsum losing water to the hemihydrate is endothermic and releases gas, so ΔH° and ΔS° are both positive. At 298 K the enthalpy cost wins, ΔG° is positive, K is small, and only a tiny water-vapour pressure builds up over the solids.
Key relation ΔH° = ΣΔfH°(prod) − ΣΔfH°(react); K = (p/p°)3/2 for 3/2 mol of gas Only the water vapour appears in K, raised to its stoichiometric power 3/2.
Solution
1. Enthalpy. ΔH° = [−1575.0 + 1.5(−241.8)] − (−2021.0) = −1937.7 + 2021.0 = +83.3 kJ mol−1.
2. (i) 1 kg of gypsum is 1000/172 = 5.814 mol, so the heat change is 5.814 × 83.3 = 484 kJ — option (a). It is absorbed, since the reaction is endothermic.
3. Entropy. ΔS° = [130.0 + 1.5(188.0)] − 194.0 = 412.0 − 194.0 = +218.0 J K−1 mol−1, positive because gas is released.
4. (ii) ΔG° = 83300 − 298(218.0) = 83300 − 64964 = +18.3 kJ mol−1. The key's 17.92 comes from using 300 K rather than 298 K; either way option (b) is the nearest.
5. (iii) ΔG° is positive, so ln K is negative and K < 1 — option (b).
6. (iv) With K = (p/p°)3/2 and K = 7.24 × 10−4 from the supplied antilog, p = (7.24 × 10−4)2/3 bar — option (d). (v) The reaction is endothermic with ΔG° > 0, so forming the hemihydrate at 298 K is endothermic and non-spontaneous, option (b).
 dH0 = -1937.7 + 2021.0 = +83.3 kJ/mol
 dS0 = 412.0 - 194.0    = +218 J/K/mol

 (i)   (1000/172)(83.3) = 484 kJ          (a)
 (ii)  83300 - 298(218) = +18.3 kJ        (b)
 (iii) dG0 > 0  ->  K < 1                 (b)
 (iv)  K = (p)^(3/2) -> p = K^(2/3)       (d)
 (v)   endothermic and non-spontaneous    (b)
⚡ JEE Trick Invert the stoichiometric power carefully: with 3/2 mol of gas, K = p3/2 and therefore p = K2/3. Reading the exponent the wrong way round is what options (b) and (c) are there to catch.
Answer: (i) a, (ii) b, (iii) b, (iv) d, (v) b
3Passage (i)–(iv). 1 mol of a monoatomic perfect gas (CV = (3/2)R) is taken through the cycle 1→2→3→1 with P = 1.00 atm at 1 and 2, P = 0.50 atm at 3, V = 22.44 L at 1 and V = 44.88 L at 2 and 3.
(i) Temperatures at points (1), (2) and (3)?   (ii) ΔH for the overall cycle?   (iii) ΔU for the process (1→2)?   (iv) ΔU for the process (2→3)?		State functions around a closed cycle14 CYCLIC PROCESS | THERMODYNAMICS | IIT ADVANCED | JEE MAIN | CHEMISTRY CLASS 11 | OLYMPIAD🟡 Similar(i) a, (ii) d, (iii) b, (iv) c
Concept A closed cycle returns every state function to its starting value, so ΔH and ΔU over the whole loop are zero. The individual legs, however, have real energy changes, and those follow from the temperatures at the three corners.
Key relation T = PV/nR; ΔU = nCVΔT with CV = (3/2)R; over a cycle ΔU = ΔH = 0 22.44 L at 1 atm is exactly half the molar volume at STP, so the temperatures come out as round numbers.
Solution
1. Temperatures. With n = 1 and R = 0.0821 L atm K−1 mol−1: T1 = (1)(22.44)/0.0821 = 273 K; T2 = (1)(44.88)/0.0821 = 546 K; T3 = (0.5)(44.88)/0.0821 = 273 K.
2. (i) That triple is 273 K, 546 K, 273 K — option (a). Note that points 1 and 3 lie on the same isotherm.
3. (ii) The process 1→2→3→1 returns to the starting state, so every state function is unchanged and ΔH = zero — option (d).
4. (iii) For 1→2, ΔU = CVΔT = (3/2)(8.314)(546 − 273) = 12.471 × 273 = +3.40 × 103 J — option (b). The printed unit on that option reads kJ, which should be J.
5. (iv) For 2→3 the temperature falls back from 546 K to 273 K, so ΔU = (3/2)(8.314)(273 − 546) = −3.40 kJ — option (c).
6. Consistency check: the three legs must sum to zero. Leg 1→2 gives +3.40 kJ, leg 2→3 gives −3.40 kJ, and leg 3→1 is isothermal at 273 K so ΔU = 0. The total is indeed zero, matching part (ii).
 T1 = 22.44/0.0821        = 273 K
 T2 = 44.88/0.0821        = 546 K
 T3 = 0.5(44.88)/0.0821   = 273 K

 (ii)  closed cycle -> dH = 0            (d)
 (iii) (3/2)R(546-273) = +3.40e3 J       (b)
 (iv)  (3/2)R(273-546) = -3.40 kJ        (c)
 sum of legs: +3.40 - 3.40 + 0 = 0
⚡ JEE Trick Compute the corner temperatures once and every leg follows. And whenever a question says "for the overall cycle", the answer for any state function is zero before you look at the options.
Answer: (i) a, (ii) d, (iii) b, (iv) c
4Select the correct statements. [MULTI CORRECT]
(a) The magnitude of work involved in an intermediate irreversible expansion is less than that involved in reversible expansion   (b) Heat absorbed during intermediate reversible expansion is more than that in intermediate irreversible expansion   (c) The magnitude of work involved in an intermediate reversible compression is more than that involved in intermediate irreversible compression   (d) Heat released during intermediate irreversible compression is more than that in intermediate reversible compression		Reversible vs irreversible workCalculate Work for Reversible and Irreversible Expansion/Compression🟢 Exact(a), (b), (d)⚠
Concept Reversible paths are the extreme cases: they extract the most work on expansion and demand the least work on compression. Getting the direction of that inequality right for each of the two directions is the entire question.
Key relation Expansion: |wrev| > |wirrev|  |  Compression: |wrev| < |wirrev| Reversible work is bounded by the area under the true isotherm; any finite pressure gap moves the area the wrong way.
Solution
1. (a) On expansion the reversible path pushes against the largest possible opposing pressure at every instant, so it delivers the maximum work. Any irreversible expansion delivers less. Correct.
2. (b) For an isothermal change ΔU = 0 so q = −w. Since the reversible expansion does more work, it must absorb more heat. Correct (the printed wording repeats "reversible" where it means irreversible).
3. (c) On compression the reversible path uses an external pressure only infinitesimally above the gas pressure, so it needs the minimum work. Claiming it needs more than the irreversible route inverts the rule.
4. Numerical check: compress 1 mol isothermally at 300 K from 2 L to 1 L. Reversibly, w = −nRT ln(V2/V1) = −(8.314)(300)(−0.693) = +1729 J. In one step against the final pressure of 24.6 atm, w = −PextΔV = +24.6 L atm = +2496 J.
5. So the irreversible compression costs 2496 J against the reversible 1729 J — statement (c) is false.
6. (d) Since more work goes in during the irreversible compression and ΔU = 0, more heat must come out. Correct. The true set is therefore a, b, d.
 expansion   : |w_rev| > |w_irrev|   (a),(b) OK
 compression : |w_rev| < |w_irrev|   (c) WRONG

 1 mol, 300 K, 2 L -> 1 L
   reversible   w = +1729 J
   one-step irr w = +2496 J
 => reversible compression is CHEAPER
⚡ JEE Trick Say it as one sentence: reversible is best in both directions — most work out when expanding, least work in when compressing. Options that make reversible the expensive route on compression are always wrong.
Answer: (a), (b), (d)  —  key prints abcd ⚠
5Which is correct about ΔG?
(a) ΔG = ΔH − TΔS   (b) At equilibrium, ΔG° = 0   (c) At equilibrium, ΔG = −RT log K   (d) ΔG = ΔG° + RT log K		ΔG vs ΔG° once moreGibbs Free Energy | ΔG | Thermodynamics | Difference Between Delta G and Delta G°🟡 Similara
Concept Four familiar-looking statements, three of which corrupt the distinction between ΔG and ΔG° or between K and Q. Only the definition itself survives unconditionally.
Key relation ΔG = ΔH − TΔS always; at equilibrium ΔG = 0 and ΔG° = −RT ln K; in general ΔG = ΔG° + RT ln Q The degree symbol goes with K, its absence with Q.
Solution
1. (a) ΔG = ΔH − TΔS is the Gibbs–Helmholtz equation, valid at constant temperature and pressure with no further conditions. Correct.
2. (b) "At equilibrium ΔG° = 0" is false. It is ΔG that vanishes; ΔG° equals −RT ln K and is zero only in the special case K = 1.
3. (c) "At equilibrium ΔG = −RT log K" is false twice over: the quantity equal to −RT ln K is ΔG°, not ΔG, and at equilibrium ΔG is zero.
4. (d) The reaction isotherm is ΔG = ΔG° + RT ln Q, with the reaction quotient Q. Writing K in its place makes the statement true only at the single point where Q = K, not in general.
5. So exactly one statement holds without qualification, and the answer is (a).
6. Worth noting how closely (c) and (d) shadow real equations. Combining them correctly at equilibrium gives 0 = ΔG° + RT ln K, which is just ΔG° = −RT ln K — the relation both options misquote.
 (a) dG = dH - T dS              always true
 (b) dG0 = 0 at eqm              FALSE
 (c) dG = -RT logK at eqm        FALSE
 (d) dG = dG0 + RT logK          Q, not K

 correct set: dG = 0 and dG0 = -RT lnK at eqm
⚡ JEE Trick Two checks catch every distractor here: does the symbol carry a degree sign, and is the logarithm of Q or of K? Run both before reading the sentence properly.
Answer: (a) ΔG = ΔH − TΔS
6Match Column-I with Column-II:
(A) ΔU = 0, ΔH = 0   (B) ΔH = −ve   (C) ΔunivS = 0   (D) ΔGmix = −ve
(p) CH2=CH2(g) + H2(g) → CH3CH3(g)   (q) For reversible process   (r) Non ideal solutions with negative deviation   (s) For an ideal gas undergoing expansion isothermally
(a) (A)→(s),(p); (B)→(r); (C)→(q); (D)→(p)   (b) (A)→(s); (B)→(p); (C)→(q); (D)→(r)   (c) (A)→(p); (B)→(q); (C)→(r),(q); (D)→(s)   (d) (A)→(r),(p); (B)→(s); (C)→(q); (D)→(r)		Matching process conditions#2- Extensive Properties | Intensive Properties | State Function | Path Function | Thermodynamics🟡 Similarb
Concept Four thermodynamic conditions must be paired with the situations that produce them. Each row has exactly one clean partner, so deriving the four pairings first and then scanning the options is far quicker than testing options one at a time.
Key relation Isothermal ideal gas: ΔU = ΔH = 0  |  reversible: ΔSuniv = 0  |  negative-deviation solutions: ΔHmix < 0 For an ideal gas both U and H depend only on temperature, so holding T fixed zeroes both changes.
Solution
1. (A) ΔU = 0, ΔH = 0. For an ideal gas both functions depend only on temperature, so an isothermal expansion leaves both unchanged → (s).
2. (B) ΔH = −ve. The hydrogenation CH2=CH2(g) + H2(g) → CH3CH3(g) is exothermic, releasing about 137 kJ mol−1 → (p).
3. (C) ΔunivS = 0. No entropy is generated only when the change is carried out reversibly → (q).
4. (D) ΔGmix = −ve. Mixing always lowers the free energy; the entry describing a solution is the non-ideal one with negative deviation → (r).
5. Assemble: (A)→(s), (B)→(p), (C)→(q), (D)→(r), which is exactly option (b).
6. Eliminate the rest: option (a) attaches (p) to (A), but a hydrogenation certainly does not have ΔH = 0; option (c) sends (A) to (p) as well; option (d) sends (B) to (s), an isothermal ideal gas, which has ΔH = 0 rather than negative.
 (A) dU = 0, dH = 0     -> isothermal ideal gas (s)
 (B) dH = -ve           -> hydrogenation        (p)
 (C) dS_univ = 0        -> reversible process   (q)
 (D) dG_mix = -ve       -> -ve deviation soln   (r)
 ------------------------------------------------
 A->s, B->p, C->q, D->r        = option (b)
⚡ JEE Trick Anchor on ΔSuniv = 0: it can only mean "reversible", never anything else. That single certain link kills every option that puts (q) elsewhere.
Answer: (b) (A)→(s), (B)→(p), (C)→(q), (D)→(r)
7In the process H2O(s), −10°C, 1 atm → H2O(l, 10°C, 1 atm), CP for ice = 9 cal deg−1 mol−1, CP for H2O = 18 cal deg−1 mol−1, latent heat of fusion of ice = 1440 cal mol−1 at 0°C. The entropy change is 6.258 cal deg−1 mol−1. Give the total number of steps in which the third law of thermodynamics is used.Counting third-law stepsThe change in entropy for the fusion of 1 mole of ice is : (m.pt. of ice =273 K, molar enthalpy ...🟡 Similar2
Concept Ice at −10 °C cannot melt where it stands, so the entropy change is found along a three-leg reversible path: warm the ice to 0 °C, melt it there, warm the water to +10 °C. The question then asks how many of those legs use the third-law form S = ∫CPdT/T.
Key relation Heating legs: ΔS = CP ln(T2/T1)  |  Fusion leg: ΔS = ΔHfus/Tfus The CPdT/T integral is the third-law expression; the latent-heat step is a different formula.
Solution
1. Leg 1, ice from 263 K to 273 K: ΔS1 = 9 ln(273/263) = 9 × 0.03733 = 0.336 cal deg−1 mol−1.
2. Leg 2, fusion at 273 K: ΔS2 = 1440/273 = 5.275 cal deg−1 mol−1.
3. Leg 3, water from 273 K to 283 K: ΔS3 = 18 ln(283/273) = 18 × 0.03598 = 0.648 cal deg−1 mol−1.
4. Total: 0.336 + 5.275 + 0.648 = 6.258 cal deg−1 mol−1, exactly the figure quoted in the stem, which confirms the path is the intended one.
5. Now count: the third-law relation ST = ∫CPdT/T is applied in legs 1 and 3, the two heating stages. Leg 2 uses ΔHfus/T instead, which is a latent-heat relation and not the third-law integral.
6. Hence the answer is 2. This same question appears as Level-6 Q9 of lecture L-12, with the same key, so the two are consistent.
 1  ice  263->273  9 ln(273/263)  = 0.336  Cp
 2  fuse at 273    1440/273       = 5.275  L/T
 3  water 273->283 18 ln(283/273) = 0.648  Cp
 -------------------------------------------
                            total = 6.258

 Cp dT/T form used in legs 1 and 3  ->  2
⚡ JEE Trick Break every across-transition entropy problem into heat–change–heat. The outer legs take CP ln(T2/T1) and the middle leg takes ΔH/T; mixing the two formulae is the standard slip.
Answer: 2 (total ΔS = 6.258 cal deg−1 mol−1)
8The standard molar enthalpies of formation of IF3(g) and IF5(g) are −470 kJ and −847 kJ respectively. IF5 is square pyramidal with all I–F bonds equivalent while IF3 is T-shaped with I–F bonds of different lengths; the axial I–F bonds in IF3 are equivalent to the I–F bonds in IF5. Calculate the equatorial I–F bond strength (in kJ/mol) in IF3. Given I2(s) → I2(g), ΔH = 62 kJ; F2(g) → 2F(g), ΔH = 155 kJ; I2(g) → 2I(g), ΔH = 149 kJ.Bond strength from formation dataTHERMOCHEMISTRY । Class 11 (L3) I Bond energy | Resonance energy | Born Haber Cycle | JEE | NEET🟡 Similar272 kJ mol<sup>−1</sup>
Concept Two formation enthalpies are given and one bond strength is wanted. The trick supplied by the question is that the axial I−F bonds of T-shaped IF3 are equivalent to the bonds in IF5, so IF5 fixes that value first and IF3 then yields the equatorial bond.
Key relation ΔfH° = Σ(atomisation of elements) − Σ(bond energies formed) Atomisation of ½I2(s) costs 105.5 kJ and of ½F2(g) costs 77.5 kJ.
Solution
1. Atom costs. I2(s) → I2(g) is 62 kJ and I2(g) → 2I(g) is 149 kJ, so I2(s) → 2I(g) costs 211 kJ and ½I2(s) → I(g) costs 105.5 kJ. Also F2(g) → 2F(g) is 155 kJ, so ½F2 → F costs 77.5 kJ.
2. IF5. Formation is ½I2(s) + (5/2)F2(g) → IF5(g), so −847 = [105.5 + 5(77.5)] − 5×BE5 = 493 − 5×BE5.
3. Solve: 5×BE5 = 493 + 847 = 1340, giving BE5 = 268 kJ mol−1 for each of the five equivalent I−F bonds.
4. IF3. Formation is ½I2(s) + (3/2)F2(g) → IF3(g) with two axial bonds (each 268, by the stated equivalence) and one equatorial bond of unknown strength x.
5. So −470 = [105.5 + 3(77.5)] − [2(268) + x] = 338 − 536 − x = −198 − x.
6. Rearranging, x = −198 + 470 = 272 kJ mol−1. The equatorial bond is slightly stronger than the axial ones, consistent with the shorter equatorial bond length in a T-shaped molecule.
 1/2 I2(s) -> I(g)  = (62 + 149)/2 = 105.5 kJ
 1/2 F2(g) -> F(g)  = 155/2        =  77.5 kJ

 IF5: -847 = 493 - 5 BE5   -> BE5 = 268
 IF3: -470 = 338 - (2x268 + x)
      -470 = -198 - x      ->  x  = 272 kJ/mol
⚡ JEE Trick When a question tells you two different bonds are "equivalent", that sentence is the bridge between the two cycles. Solve the fully symmetric molecule first, then carry its bond energy into the unsymmetrical one.
Answer: equatorial I−F bond strength = 272 kJ mol−1 (axial = 268 kJ mol−1)
9A dithermic container holding an ideal gas, fitted with a piston at equilibrium, has initial volume 600 litre. The external pressure is suddenly reduced to 1 bar and the piston moves upward isothermally; the system absorbs 600 kJ of heat. A student calculated ΔSsys = 2 kJ/K by using q/T and was marked wrong. Find the correct answer (in kJ/K). [ln 11 = 2.4] Fill the answer excluding decimal places.qirrev/T is not ΔSExpansion of a gas in vacuum is called free expansion. Calculate the work done and the change in ...🟡 Similar5 (ΔS<sub>sys</sub> ≈ 5.28 kJ K<sup>−1</sup>)
Concept The student's error is using q/T with an irreversible heat. Entropy is defined through qrev/T, so the actual expansion must be replaced by a reversible isothermal path between the same two volumes, for which ΔS = nR ln(V2/V1).
Key relation ΔS = nR ln(V2/V1), not qirrev/T qirrev < qrev, so dividing the actual heat by T always understates ΔS.
Solution
1. The expansion is isothermal and the gas is ideal, so ΔU = 0 and q = −w = PextΔV. With q = 600 kJ and Pext = 1 bar, ΔV = 600000 J / (100 J L−1) = 6000 L.
2. So V2 = 600 + 6000 = 6600 L and the volume ratio is V2/V1 = 6600/600 = 11 — which is why the question supplies ln 11 = 2.4.
3. The student's own figure fixes the temperature: 600 kJ / 2 kJ K−1 gives T = 300 K.
4. The initial pressure follows from the isothermal relation P1V1 = P2V2: P1 = (1)(6600)/600 = 11 bar.
5. Then nR = P1V1/T = (11)(600)/300 = 22 bar L K−1 = 2.2 kJ K−1.
6. Finally ΔS = nR ln(V2/V1) = 2.2 × 2.4 = 5.28 kJ K−1. Excluding decimal places the answer to fill is 5. The student's 2 kJ K−1 is smaller, exactly as qirrev/T must be.
 q = -w = Pext dV = 600 kJ, Pext = 1 bar
 dV = 600000/100 = 6000 L  ->  V2 = 6600 L
 V2/V1 = 11

 T = 600 kJ / 2 kJ/K = 300 K
 P1 = (1)(6600)/600 = 11 bar
 nR = (11)(600)/300 = 22 bar.L/K = 2.2 kJ/K

 dS = 2.2 x ln11 = 2.2 x 2.4 = 5.28 kJ/K
⚡ JEE Trick q/T is legitimate only when the heat is exchanged reversibly. For any sudden or irreversible change, invent a reversible route between the same endpoints — for an isothermal gas that is always nR ln(V2/V1).
Answer: 5 — ΔSsys = 5.28 kJ K−1, not the student's 2 kJ K−1
10External pressure of 2 atm is applied on a frictionless movable piston in a vessel containing 100 g of X(l) at 450 K. Heat is supplied at constant pressure until 40 g of X is evaporated to form X(g) at 500 K (boiling point). Calculate the change in internal energy (ΔU) in kJ for the overall process, assuming X(g) is ideal. Given molar heat capacity of X(l) = 60 J/mol·K, ΔHvaporisation = 30 kJ/mol, R = 8.3 J/mol-K, atomic weight of X = 20 g/mol. [Fill your answer by multiplying it with 100.]Heating plus partial vaporisationMeasurements of ∆U and ∆H || Calorimetery || Thermodynamics Part - 5 || Class-11 | Chemistry🟡 SimilarΔU = +66.7 kJ⚠
Concept The process has two stages at constant pressure: warming the whole liquid to its boiling point, then vaporising part of it. The enthalpy change is the sum of the two, and the internal energy change is smaller by the work done in creating the vapour.
Key relation ΔH = nCP,liqΔT + nvapΔHvap; ΔU = ΔH − ΔngRT Only the moles of gas created enter Δng; the liquid's own volume change is negligible.
Solution
1. Moles: the atomic weight of X is 20 g mol−1, so 100 g of liquid is 5 mol and the 40 g that evaporates is 2 mol.
2. Stage 1, heating all 5 mol of liquid from 450 K to 500 K: ΔH1 = 5 × 60 × 50 = 15000 J = 15 kJ.
3. Stage 2, vaporising 2 mol at the boiling point: ΔH2 = 2 × 30 = 60 kJ.
4. Total enthalpy change: ΔH = 15 + 60 = 75 kJ.
5. Gas created: Δng = +2 mol at 500 K, so ΔngRT = 2 × 8.3 × 500 = 8300 J = 8.3 kJ. This is the work spent pushing the piston out against the 2 atm load.
6. Hence ΔU = ΔH − ΔngRT = 75 − 8.3 = +66.7 kJ. The key's 25.5 kJ does not follow from any grouping of the supplied numbers.
 100 g / 20 = 5 mol liquid ; 40 g / 20 = 2 mol gas

 heat liquid : 5 x 60 x 50   = 15000 J = 15 kJ
 vaporise    : 2 x 30 kJ     =           60 kJ
 -----------------------------------------------
 dH                          =           75 kJ
 dn_g R T = 2 x 8.3 x 500    =          8.3 kJ
 dU = 75 - 8.3               =         66.7 kJ
⚡ JEE Trick Heat the whole sample but vaporise only the stated fraction. Counting 2 mol in the heating step, or 5 mol in the vaporisation step, are the two ways this problem is usually got wrong.
Answer: ΔU = +66.7 kJ (ΔH = 75 kJ)  —  key prints 25.5 kJ ⚠

1The vapour pressure of solid benzoic acid near 298 K obeys ln(P/P°) = a − b/T with a = 22.88 and b = 1.07 × 104 K. Which of the following values are correct at 298 K for the sublimation of benzoic acid? [MULTI CORRECT]
(a) ΔG° = 32.34 kJ mol−1   (b) ΔH° = 88.96 kJ mol−1   (c) ΔS° = 190 J K−1 mol−1   (d) ΔH° = −88.96 kJ mol−1		Clausius–Clapeyron from ln P vs 1/TVariation of equilibrium constant K with temperature is given by van't Hoff equation ln K=Δ_...🟡 Similar(a), (b), (c)
Concept A vapour-pressure equation of the form ln(P/P°) = a − b/T is the Clausius–Clapeyron relation in disguise. Comparing it term by term with ln K = −ΔH°/RT + ΔS°/R lets both thermodynamic quantities be read straight off the constants.
Key relation ln K = −ΔH°/(RT) + ΔS°/R, so ΔH° = bR and ΔS° = aR For sublimation the equilibrium constant is just the vapour pressure relative to p°, so K = P/P°.
Solution
1. Match the slope term: −b/T corresponds to −ΔH°/(RT), so ΔH° = bR = (1.07 × 104)(8.314) = 88960 J mol−1 = +88.96 kJ mol−1.
2. That confirms statement (b) and rules out (d): sublimation absorbs heat, so ΔH° must be positive, never −88.96.
3. Match the intercept: a corresponds to ΔS°/R, so ΔS° = aR = (22.88)(8.314) = 190.2 J K−1 mol−1, confirming statement (c). A large positive value is expected when a solid becomes a gas.
4. Now assemble the free energy at 298 K: ΔG° = ΔH° − TΔS° = 88960 − 298(190.2) = 88960 − 56680.
5. Evaluate: ΔG° = 32280 J mol−1 = +32.28 kJ mol−1, matching statement (a)'s 32.34 to rounding.
6. So (a), (b) and (c) are all correct and only (d) fails, on the sign of the sublimation enthalpy. The positive ΔG° is also physically right: solid benzoic acid does not sublime spontaneously at 1 bar and 298 K.
 ln(P/P0) = a - b/T   vs   lnK = -dH0/RT + dS0/R

 dH0 = bR = 1.07e4 x 8.314 = 88960 J   (b) OK
 dS0 = aR = 22.88 x 8.314  = 190.2     (c) OK
 dG0 = 88960 - 298(190.2)  = 32280 J   (a) OK
 (d) has dH0 negative                  WRONG
⚡ JEE Trick Any straight line of ln K against 1/T hands you both quantities at once: multiply the slope by −R for ΔH° and the intercept by R for ΔS°. No further data are needed.
Answer: (a), (b) and (c)
2Point out the correct statements: [MULTI CORRECT]
(a) Oxides of nitrogen are thermodynamically unstable with respect to decomposition into elements   (b) A spontaneous process does not necessarily reduce internal energy or enthalpy of a system   (c) For thermodynamic equilibrium ΔSTotal = 0   (d) Entropy cannot decrease in an isolated system		Four true thermodynamic statementsEasiest Trick to identify Spontaneous & non spontaneous reaction NEET/JEE🟡 Similar(a), (b), (c), (d) — all four
Concept Four statements drawn from different corners of the chapter: the stability of nitrogen oxides, the insufficiency of the energy criterion, the meaning of equilibrium, and the second law for an isolated system. Each has to be checked on its own, and here all four hold.
Key relation ΔStotal > 0 spontaneous, = 0 at equilibrium; isolated system ⇒ ΔSsurr = 0 Positive ΔfG° means a compound is unstable with respect to its elements.
Solution
1. (a) NO has ΔfG° = +86.6 kJ mol−1 and NO2 about +51 kJ mol−1, both positive. A positive formation free energy means decomposition back to N2 and O2 is the spontaneous direction, so the oxides are thermodynamically unstable. True — they persist only because the decomposition is kinetically slow.
2. (b) Spontaneity is decided by ΔG, not by ΔU or ΔH. Ice melting above 0 °C and NH4NO3 dissolving both raise the system's enthalpy yet happen on their own. True.
3. (c) At thermodynamic equilibrium no entropy is generated, so ΔSTotal = 0. This is the same statement as ΔG = 0 through the bridge ΔG = −TΔStotal. True.
4. (d) In an isolated system q = 0, so the surroundings contribute nothing and the second law reduces to ΔSsystem ≥ 0. The system's entropy therefore cannot fall. True.
5. Note the careful qualifier in (d). Without the word "isolated" the statement would be false, since a non-isolated system's entropy is free to decrease — water freezing is the standard counter-example.
6. All four statements survive, so the answer is abcd.
 (a) dfG0(NO) = +86.6 kJ/mol  -> unstable  TRUE
 (b) spontaneity needs dG, not dH alone    TRUE
 (c) equilibrium: dS_total = 0             TRUE
 (d) isolated: q = 0 -> dS_sys >= 0        TRUE

 kinetics, not thermodynamics, keeps NO around
⚡ JEE Trick "Thermodynamically unstable" always means positive ΔfG°, never slow. A compound can be unstable on paper and perfectly long-lived in a bottle.
Answer: (a), (b), (c) and (d) — all four are correct
3Calculate the resonance energy of C6H6 using the Kekulé formula from the following data: (1) ΔH° for C6H6 = −358.5 kJ mol−1; (2) heat of atomisation of C = 716.8 kJ mol−1; (3) bond energies of C−H, C−C, C=C and H−H are 490, 340, 620 and 436.9 kJ mol−1.Resonance energy of benzeneCalculate the resonance energy of `C_(6)H_(6)` using kekule formula of `C_(6)H_(6)` from🟢 Exact−150.0 kJ mol<sup>−1</sup>
Concept Resonance energy is the gap between the measured enthalpy of formation and the value a single Kekulé structure would predict from bond energies. Build the hypothetical value first, then subtract it from the experimental one.
Key relation ΔfH(calc) = Σ(atomisation of elements) − Σ(bond energies formed); RE = ΔfH(exp) − ΔfH(calc) One Kekulé ring has 3 C−C, 3 C=C and 6 C−H bonds.
Solution
1. Formation reaction: 6C(s) + 3H2(g) → C6H6. Atomising the elements costs 6 × 716.8 = 4300.8 kJ for carbon and 3 × 436.9 = 1310.7 kJ for hydrogen, a total of 5611.5 kJ.
2. Bonds formed in one Kekulé structure: 3 C−C at 340, 3 C=C at 620 and 6 C−H at 490 kJ mol−1.
3. That releases 3(340) + 3(620) + 6(490) = 1020 + 1860 + 2940 = 5820 kJ.
4. Hypothetical formation enthalpy: ΔfH(calc) = 5611.5 − 5820 = −208.5 kJ mol−1.
5. Resonance energy: RE = ΔfH(exp) − ΔfH(calc) = −358.5 − (−208.5) = −150.0 kJ mol−1.
6. The negative sign is the point: the real molecule lies 150 kJ mol−1 below the localised Kekulé structure, and that stabilisation is why benzene resists addition reactions.
 atomise : 6(716.8) + 3(436.9) = 5611.5 kJ
 bonds   : 3(340) + 3(620) + 6(490) = 5820 kJ
 ---------------------------------------------
 dfH(calc) = 5611.5 - 5820 = -208.5 kJ/mol
 dfH(exp)                  = -358.5 kJ/mol

 RE = -358.5 - (-208.5) = -150.0 kJ/mol
⚡ JEE Trick Count the Kekulé bonds before touching the arithmetic: 3 single, 3 double, 6 C−H. Using the real one-and-a-half bonds instead is the commonest error and destroys the whole point of the calculation.
Answer: resonance energy = −150.0 kJ mol−1 (magnitude 150 kJ mol−1 of stabilisation)
4Calculate the value of log Kp for N2(g) + 3H2(g) ⇌ 2NH3(g) at 25°C. The standard enthalpy of formation of NH3(g) is −46 kJ and standard entropies of N2(g), H2(g), NH3(g) are 191, 130, 192 J K−1 mol−1 respectively. (R = 8.3 J K−1 mol−1)log Kp for ammonia synthesisRelationship between Standard (Gibbs) Free energy and Equilibrium constant(k) | thermodynamics🟡 Similarlog K<sub>p</sub> ≈ 5.845
Concept The Haber equilibrium constant follows from ΔG°, which in turn needs both ΔH° and ΔS°. The enthalpy comes from the formation value doubled; the entropy from the tabulated absolute entropies, remembering the coefficient 3 on hydrogen.
Key relation ΔG° = ΔH° − TΔS°; log Kp = −ΔG°/(2.303 RT) Absolute entropies of elements are not zero and must be included in full.
Solution
1. Reaction: N2(g) + 3H2(g) ⇌ 2NH3(g) at 298 K.
2. Enthalpy: two moles of ammonia are formed, so ΔH° = 2(−46) = −92 kJ = −92000 J.
3. Entropy: ΔS° = 2(192) − [191 + 3(130)] = 384 − 581 = −197 J K−1. Negative, as four moles of gas become two.
4. Free energy: ΔG° = −92000 − 298(−197) = −92000 + 58706 = −33294 J.
5. Isotherm denominator: 2.303 × 8.3 × 298 = 5695.4 J mol−1.
6. Divide: log Kp = 33294/5695.4 = 5.845, so Kp ≈ 7 × 105. Ammonia synthesis is strongly product-favoured at room temperature; the industrial need for 700 K is a kinetic compromise, and the negative ΔS° is why higher temperature actually lowers the yield.
 N2 + 3H2 <=> 2NH3        T = 298 K

 dH0 = 2(-46)                  = -92000 J
 dS0 = 2(192) - [191 + 3(130)] = -197 J/K
 dG0 = -92000 + 58706          = -33294 J

 2.303 R T = 5695.4
 log Kp = 33294 / 5695.4 = 5.845
⚡ JEE Trick Multiply the formation enthalpy by the stoichiometric coefficient before anything else. Using −46 rather than −92 here halves ΔH° and throws log K out by more than eight units.
Answer: log Kp = 5.845 (ΔG° = −33.3 kJ)
5A plot of free energy G against extent of reaction for N2O4 ⇌ 2NO2 has A = standard free energy of 1 mol of N2O4, C = standard free energy of 2 mol of NO2 and B = equilibrium point, with ΔG = 5.40 kJ from A to C and 0.84 kJ from A down to B. Which statements are correct? [MULTI CORRECT]
(a) The complete conversion of N2O4 into NO2 is spontaneous   (b) At point B, free energy of the system becomes constant with time   (c) In the region between A and B the reaction has a tendency to move in the forward direction   (d) In the region between B and C, ΔG for the reaction is greater than zero		Reading a free-energy wellGibb's Energy and Spontaneity - Thermodynamics (Part 28)🟡 Similar(b), (c), (d)
Concept The free-energy curve for N2O4 ⇌ 2NO2 is a valley. Point A is pure reactant, C is pure product, and B is the minimum where the mixture settles. Reading the slope on either side of B answers every part of the question.
Key relation The system rolls downhill towards the minimum; ΔG < 0 going towards B, ΔG > 0 going away from it A to C is a rise of 5.40 kJ, so ΔG° is positive and complete conversion is uphill overall.
Solution
1. (a) Going all the way from A to C raises the free energy by 5.40 kJ, so ΔG° = +5.40 kJ. Complete conversion of N2O4 into NO2 is therefore not spontaneous, and (a) is false.
2. (b) Point B is the minimum of the curve, so the composition stops changing and the free energy stays put. "Constant with time" is exactly what equilibrium means. True.
3. (c) Between A and B the curve descends, so moving forward lowers G and ΔG is negative. The reaction does tend to move forward there. True.
4. (d) Between B and C the curve ascends, so continuing forward would raise G and ΔG is greater than zero. In that region the reverse reaction is the spontaneous one. True.
5. The two numbers support the picture: the minimum lies 0.84 kJ below A and 5.40 − 0.84 = 6.24 kJ below C, so the equilibrium mixture sits nearer the reactant side, as a positive ΔG° requires.
6. The correct set is therefore bcd.
 G
 A .                     . C   (A->C = +5.40 kJ)
    \                  /
     \      B         /
      \____ * _______/    B is 0.84 kJ below A

 A to B : G falls -> dG < 0   forward  (c) OK
 B to C : G rises -> dG > 0            (d) OK
 at B   : minimum, no net change       (b) OK
 A to C overall uphill -> (a) FALSE
⚡ JEE Trick On a G-versus-extent plot, the sign of ΔG is just the slope in the direction you are walking. Downhill is spontaneous, the bottom is equilibrium, uphill is not — no algebra required.
Answer: (b), (c) and (d)
6Passage (i)–(iii). Adiabatic reversible work is w = nR(Ti − Tf)/(γ − 1); adiabatic curves are steeper than isothermal by a factor γ; PVγ = constant.
(i) A gas expands adiabatically satisfying VT3 = constant; the ratio of specific heats γ is?   (ii) In an adiabatic expansion of air the volume increases by 5%; the percentage change in pressure is?   (iii) Helium obeys T/P2/5 = constant; the heat given when the temperature of m moles of He is raised from T to 2T is?		Adiabatic relations passage13 REVERSIBLE AND IRREVERSIBLE ADIABATIC PROCESS | IIT ADVANCED | JEE MAIN | CHEMISTRY CLASS 11🟢 Exact(i) d, (ii) a, (iii) d
Concept Every adiabatic relation is the same equation in different variables. PVγ, TVγ−1 and TγP1−γ are all constant, so a given "V Tn = constant" or "T/Pm = constant" immediately identifies γ — or reveals that the process is adiabatic and hence q = 0.
Key relation TVγ−1 = const ⇒ V T1/(γ−1) = const; PVγ = const ⇒ dP/P = −γ dV/V; TγP1−γ = const Matching exponents is faster than re-deriving each relation.
Solution
1. (i) The adiabatic law TVγ−1 = constant rearranges to V T1/(γ−1) = constant. The stem gives VT3 = constant, so 1/(γ − 1) = 3.
2. Hence γ − 1 = 1/3 and γ = 4/3 — option (d), the value for a polyatomic gas.
3. (ii) Differentiating PVγ = constant logarithmically gives dP/P = −γ(dV/V). Air is diatomic, so γ = 1.4.
4. With dV/V = +5%, dP/P = −1.4 × 5 = −7%, a 7% fall in pressure — option (a).
5. (iii) Rearrange the given T/P2/5 = constant to T5/P2 = constant. For helium γ = 5/3, and the standard adiabatic form TγP1−γ = constant becomes T5/3P−2/3 = constant, which cubes to exactly T5/P2 = constant.
6. So the stated law is the adiabatic condition for helium, and an adiabatic process exchanges no heat: q = zero, option (d). The temperature doubling is a decoy — the energy comes from work, not heat.
 (i)   V T^(1/(g-1)) = const, given V T^3
       1/(g-1) = 3  ->  g = 4/3           (d)

 (ii)  dP/P = -g dV/V = -1.4(5%) = -7%    (a)

 (iii) T/P^(2/5) = const  ->  T^5/P^2
       He: T^(5/3) P^(-2/3) = const, cubed
       same law  ->  adiabatic  ->  q = 0 (d)
⚡ JEE Trick Before computing anything, test whether the quoted relation is simply the adiabatic law for that gas. If it is, q = 0 and the question is over, however elaborate the rest of the wording.
Answer: (i) d, (ii) a, (iii) d
7Passage (i)–(iii). ΔG = −nFEcell; for a reversible cell d(ΔG) = (ΔV)dp − (ΔS)dT, so at constant P, ΔG = ΔH + T(dΔG/dT)P, and (dEcell/dT)P is the temperature coefficient of emf.
(i) The temperature coefficient (dE/dT)P is given by?   (ii) At 300 K, ΔH for Zn + 2AgCl → ZnCl2 + 2Ag is −218 kJ/mol while the emf is 1.015 V; find (dE/dT)P.   (iii) The ΔS for the cell reaction is?		Temperature coefficient of cell emfTemperature Coefficient of Cell / Change in Enthalpy and entropy / electrochemistry / class 12🟢 Exact(i) b, (ii) b, (iii) a
Concept The temperature coefficient of a cell's emf is a direct measurement of the reaction entropy. Differentiating ΔG = −nFE with respect to temperature at constant pressure links (dE/dT)P to ΔS, and the Gibbs equation then supplies whichever quantity is missing.
Key relation ΔG = −nFE; (dE/dT)P = ΔS/(nF); ΔS = (ΔH − ΔG)/T Since (dΔG/dT)P = −ΔS, the minus signs cancel and the coefficient carries the sign of ΔS.
Solution
1. (i) From ΔG = −nFE, differentiate: (dΔG/dT)P = −nF(dE/dT)P. But (dΔG/dT)P = −ΔS, so (dE/dT)P = ΔS/(nF) — option (b).
2. (ii) For Zn + 2AgCl → ZnCl2 + 2Ag, two electrons transfer, so n = 2 and ΔG = −nFE = −2(96500)(1.015) = −195895 J mol−1.
3. Entropy from the Gibbs equation: ΔS = (ΔH − ΔG)/T = (−218000 + 195895)/300 = −22105/300 = −73.7 J K−1 mol−1.
4. Temperature coefficient: (dE/dT) = ΔS/(nF) = −73.7/193000 = −3.82 × 10−4 V K−1, matching option (b).
5. (iii) The entropy just found is the answer to the third part: ΔS ≈ −73.7 J K−1 mol−1. Of the four options only (a) −75.53 is even negative, so it is the intended choice; the small gap comes from the key's rounding of F and E.
6. The negative sign makes chemical sense: solid Zn and AgCl give ZnCl2 in solution plus solid Ag, and the net ordering of the ions lowers the entropy.
 (i)   dG = -nFE ; (ddG/dT) = -dS
       => (dE/dT) = dS/(nF)              (b)

 (ii)  dG = -2(96500)(1.015) = -195895 J
       dS = (-218000 + 195895)/300 = -73.7
       dE/dT = -73.7/193000 = -3.82e-4 V/K (b)

 (iii) dS = -73.7 J/K/mol ; key -75.53    (a)
⚡ JEE Trick The sign of (dE/dT) is the sign of ΔS. A cell whose voltage falls as it warms is running a reaction that loses entropy — you can read the thermodynamics off a voltmeter and a thermometer.
Answer: (i) b, (ii) b, (iii) a (ΔS ≈ −73.7 J K−1 mol−1)
8Passage (i)–(iii). Gibbs energy combines enthalpy and entropy: ΔG = ΔH − TΔS; a decrease in G measures useful work and is zero at equilibrium.
(i) The lattice energy of a salt AB is 764 kJ and the hydration energy is 755 kJ; if the entropy of dissolution in water is 40 J/K/mol at 298 K, the Gibbs energy change of dissolution is?   (ii) If 20 g argon is compressed from 10 L to 5 L under reversible isothermal conditions at 300 K, the Gibbs energy change would be? [R = 8.3 J; At. mass Ar = 40]   (iii) If the standard Gibbs energy change of a system is zero, the equilibrium constant would be?		Applying ΔG to solution and compressionCalculated the Gibbs energy change on dissolving one mole of sodium chloride at 25^(@)C. Lattice...🟡 Similar(i) b, (ii) b, (iii) d
Concept Three quick applications of ΔG. The first balances lattice against hydration energy for a dissolving salt, the second computes the free-energy cost of compressing a gas, and the third reads the isotherm backwards from ΔG° = 0.
Key relation ΔHsoln = Ulattice + ΔHhyd; ΔG = nRT ln(V1/V2) for isothermal compression; ΔG° = 0 ⇒ K = 1 Compression raises the free energy, so that middle answer must come out positive.
Solution
1. (i) Breaking the lattice costs +764 kJ and hydrating the ions returns −755 kJ, so ΔHsoln = 764 − 755 = +9 kJ mol−1, slightly endothermic.
2. Entropy term: TΔS = 298 × 0.040 = 11.92 kJ mol−1. Hence ΔG = 9 − 11.92 = −2.92 kJ — option (b). Dissolution is spontaneous, driven entirely by entropy.
3. (ii) 20 g of argon is 20/40 = 0.5 mol. For isothermal compression from 10 L to 5 L, ΔG = nRT ln(V1/V2) = 0.5 × 8.3 × 300 × ln 2.
4. Evaluate: 1245 × 0.693 = +863 J = +0.863 kJ — option (b). The sign is positive because squeezing a gas raises its free energy; work has to be supplied.
5. (iii) From ΔG° = −RT ln K, setting ΔG° = 0 gives ln K = 0 and therefore K = 1.0 — option (d).
6. Note that K = 1 is not the same as "no reaction": it means products and reactants are equally favoured at standard conditions, so the equilibrium sits squarely in the middle. Option (b) "zero" is the trap for reading ΔG° = 0 as K = 0.
 (i)   dH = 764 - 755 = +9 kJ
       T dS = 298(0.040) = 11.92 kJ
       dG = 9 - 11.92 = -2.92 kJ         (b)

 (ii)  n = 20/40 = 0.5 mol
       dG = 0.5(8.3)(300) ln2 = +863 J   (b)

 (iii) dG0 = 0 -> lnK = 0 -> K = 1.0     (d)
⚡ JEE Trick Fix the sign by direction: expansion lowers G, compression raises it. If a compression question yields a negative ΔG, the volume ratio has been inverted.
Answer: (i) b, (ii) b, (iii) d
9Match the Column:
(A) For an isothermal reversible process   (B) For any reversible cyclic process   (C) For an adiabatic process   (D) For an isochoric process
(p) ΔH = 0, ΔE = 0, ΔS = 0   (q) dq = 0   (r) dV = 0   (s) ΔE = 0
(a) (A)→(p,s), (B)→(s,r), (C)→(r), (D)→(q)   (b) (A)→(s), (B)→(p,r,s), (C)→(q), (D)→(r)   (c) (A)→(p,r,s), (B)→(s), (C)→(r), (D)→(q)   (d) None of these		Matching process to its vanishing quantityTHERMODYNAMICS - 03 || PATH FUNCTION & STATE FUNCTION.🟡 Similarb
Concept Each process is defined by whichever quantity it holds fixed, and a cyclic process is special because every state function returns to its starting value. Rows may therefore match more than one entry.
Key relation Isothermal ideal gas: ΔE = 0  |  cyclic: ΔH = ΔE = ΔS = 0 and ΔV = 0  |  adiabatic: dq = 0  |  isochoric: dV = 0 A cycle ends where it began, so it satisfies several of the entries at once.
Solution
1. (A) Isothermal reversible process. For an ideal gas the internal energy depends only on temperature, so holding T fixed gives ΔE = 0 → (s). Note ΔS is not zero here, since the gas expands.
2. (B) Any reversible cyclic process. The system returns to its initial state, so ΔH = 0, ΔE = 0 and ΔS = 0 — that is entry (p) in full — and also ΔE = 0 separately as (s).
3. Because the cycle also returns to its original volume, ΔV = 0 and entry (r) applies too. So (B) → (p), (r), (s).
4. (C) Adiabatic process. By definition no heat crosses the boundary, so dq = 0 → (q).
5. (D) Isochoric process. The volume is held constant, so dV = 0 → (r), and consequently no expansion work is done.
6. Assembling: (A)→(s), (B)→(p),(r),(s), (C)→(q), (D)→(r), which is option (b). Option (a) misses that a cycle also has ΔV = 0, and option (c) wrongly gives the isothermal row ΔS = 0.
 (A) isothermal reversible : dE = 0        (s)
 (B) reversible cyclic     : dH=dE=dS=0    (p)
                             dV = 0        (r)
                             dE = 0        (s)
 (C) adiabatic             : dq = 0        (q)
 (D) isochoric             : dV = 0        (r)
 ------------------------------------------------
 A->s ; B->p,r,s ; C->q ; D->r   = option (b)
⚡ JEE Trick Do not confuse "isothermal" with "isoentropic". An isothermal expansion has ΔE = 0 but ΔS > 0; only a reversible adiabatic change has ΔS = 0.
Answer: (b) (A)→(s), (B)→(p,r,s), (C)→(q), (D)→(r)
10Match the Column:
(A) Isothermal vapourisation of water at 100°C and 1 atm   (B) Adiabatic irreversible free expansion of an ideal gas   (C) Adiabatic reversible process   (D) Isothermal irreversible process against a constant pressure
(p) ΔT = 0   (q) Change in internal energy as a function of T is zero   (r) q = 0   (s) ΔSsystem = 0   (t) Work done = 0
(a) (A)→(q,p), (B)→(s,r), (C)→(p,q), (D)→(p,q,r,s,t)   (b) (A)→(p,q), (B)→(p,q,r,s,t), (C)→(r,s), (D)→(p,q)   (c) (A)→(p,q,r,s,t), (B)→(p,q), (C)→(s,r), (D)→(q,p)   (d) None of these		Matching processes to zero quantities#2- Extensive Properties | Intensive Properties | State Function | Path Function | Thermodynamics🟡 Similarb
Concept Four processes must be matched against five conditions, and most rows carry several. The efficient route is to fix the rows you are certain of — the adiabatic and free-expansion ones — and let those eliminate the options.
Key relation Free expansion: q = 0, w = 0, ΔU = 0, ΔT = 0  |  adiabatic reversible: q = 0 and ΔSsystem = 0  |  isothermal: ΔT = 0 Only a reversible adiabatic change has zero system entropy; an irreversible one does not.
Solution
1. (A) Isothermal vaporisation of water at 100 °C. The temperature is fixed, so ΔT = 0 → (p), and the internal energy therefore has no temperature-driven change → (q). Work is done and latent heat flows, so (r) and (t) do not apply.
2. (C) Adiabatic reversible process. No heat crosses the boundary, q = 0 → (r), and because it is reversible ΔSsystem = 0 → (s). So (C) → (r), (s).
3. (D) Isothermal irreversible expansion against constant pressure. The temperature is held fixed, so ΔT = 0 → (p) and again (q). Work is non-zero, so (t) is excluded.
4. (B) Adiabatic irreversible free expansion of an ideal gas. Expanding into vacuum gives w = 0 → (t); adiabatic gives q = 0 → (r); hence ΔU = 0 and ΔT = 0 → (p) and (q).
5. Only option (b) carries the three certain assignments (A)→(p,q), (C)→(r,s) and (D)→(p,q), so it is the answer.
6. One caveat: option (b) also attaches (s) to row (B). That is not strictly right, since a free expansion is irreversible and raises the system's entropy by nR ln(V2/V1). The other three rows nevertheless fix the choice.
 (A) isothermal vaporisation : p, q
 (B) free expansion          : p, q, r, t
       (option b also lists s - not strict)
 (C) adiabatic reversible    : r, s
 (D) isothermal irreversible : p, q
 -----------------------------------------
 only option (b) fits A, C and D
⚡ JEE Trick Free expansion is the one process that switches off almost everything at once: q, w, ΔU and ΔT are all zero. The single thing it does not zero is the entropy.
Answer: (b) (A)→(p,q), (B)→(p,q,r,s,t), (C)→(r,s), (D)→(p,q)

#QuestionTopicYouTubeMatchAnswer
1One mole of ideal monatomic gas is carried through the reversible cyclic process A → B → C → A, where A is at (V°, P°), B is at (V°, 3P°) and C is at (2V°, P°), with B→C a straight line. Calculate (a) work done by the gas, (b) the heat exchanged by the gas in path CA and AB, (c) net heat absorbed by the gas in the path BC, (d) the maximum temperature attained by the gas during the cycle.Full analysis of a three-step cycleCYCLIC PROCESS: WORK DONE Questions Explained | First Law of Thermodynamics | IIT JEE Physics🟡 Similarw = P°V°; q<sub>AB</sub> = 3P°V°, q<sub>CA</sub> = −(5/2)P°V°; q<sub>BC</sub> = ½P°V°; T<sub>max</sub> = (25/8)(P°V°/R)
Concept A triangular cycle on P–V: the net work is the enclosed area, the two straight legs take CV and CP, and the sloping leg's heat follows from ΔU = 0 round the loop. The maximum temperature lies inside the sloping leg, not at a corner.
Key relation Cycle: ΔU = 0 so Σq = wby; on the line P = 5P° − (2P°/V°)V, T = PV/R Maximising T means maximising the product PV along the straight line.
Solution
1. Corners. A(V°, P°), B(V°, 3P°), C(2V°, P°), so TA = P°V°/R, TB = 3P°V°/R, TC = 2P°V°/R.
2. (a) Work. A→B is isochoric so w = 0. B→C is the trapezium under the line, ½(3P° + P°)(V°) = 2P°V°. C→A compresses at P° giving −P°V°. Net work done by the gas = P°V°.
3. (b) Heats on the straight legs. A→B at constant volume: q = CVΔT = (3/2)R(3P°V°/R − P°V°/R) = 3P°V°, absorbed. C→A at constant pressure: q = CPΔT = (5/2)R(P°V°/R − 2P°V°/R) = −(5/2)P°V°, released.
4. (c) Heat on BC. Round the cycle ΔU = 0, so Σq equals the net work P°V°. Hence qBC = P°V° − 3P°V° + (5/2)P°V° = ½P°V°.
5. (d) Maximum temperature. The line through B and C is P = 5P° − (2P°/V°)V, so RT = PV = 5P°V − (2P°/V°)V2. Differentiate: 5P° = (4P°/V°)V, giving V = 5V°/4.
6. Substitute back: RT = 5P°(5V°/4) − (2P°/V°)(25V°2/16) = 25P°V°/4 − 25P°V°/8, so Tmax = (25/8)(P°V°/R) — hotter than any corner, since TB is only 3P°V°/R.
 A(V0,P0)  B(V0,3P0)  C(2V0,P0)

 w : 0 + 2P0V0 - P0V0        = P0V0
 qAB = (3/2)R dT             = 3 P0V0
 qCA = (5/2)R dT             = -5/2 P0V0
 qBC = P0V0 - 3P0V0 + 5/2P0V0 = 1/2 P0V0

 line BC: P = 5P0 - (2P0/V0)V
 dT/dV = 0 at V = 5V0/4
 Tmax = (25/8)(P0V0/R)
⚡ JEE Trick On a sloping P–V leg the hottest point is almost never a corner. Write RT = PV along the line and differentiate — the maximum sits at the midpoint of the pressure range.
Answer: w = P°V°; qAB = 3P°V°, qCA = −(5/2)P°V°; qBC = ½P°V°; Tmax = (25/8)(P°V°/R)
2A monatomic ideal gas of two moles is taken through a reversible cyclic process starting from A, with VB/VA = 2 and VD/VA = 4 on a V–T diagram (A→B straight line through the origin, B→C isothermal, C→D isochoric, D→A isothermal). If TA at A is 27°C, calculate (a) the temperature of the gas at point B, (b) heat absorbed or released by the gas in each process, (c) the total work done by the gas during the complete cycle.Cycle on a V–T diagram14 CYCLIC PROCESS | THERMODYNAMICS | IIT ADVANCED | JEE MAIN | CHEMISTRY CLASS 11 | OLYMPIAD🟡 Similar(a) T<sub>B</sub> = 600 K; (b) q<sub>AB</sub> = 3000 cal, q<sub>BC</sub> = 1663 cal, q<sub>CD</sub> = −1800 cal, q<sub>DA</sub> = −1663 cal, total 1200 cal; (c) w = 1200 cal done by the gas
Concept On a V–T diagram a straight line through the origin is an isobar, a vertical line is an isotherm and a horizontal line is an isochore. Reading the geometry that way turns the figure into four standard legs.
Key relation Isobaric q = nCPΔT; isothermal q = nRT ln(Vf/Vi); isochoric q = nCVΔT; over a cycle wby = Σq With R = 2 cal K−1 mol−1 and n = 2, CV = 3 and CP = 5 cal K−1 mol−1.
Solution
1. (a) A→B lies on a line through the origin, so V/T is constant: TB/TA = VB/VA = 2. With TA = 300 K, TB = 600 K.
2. A→B (isobaric). q = nCPΔT = 2 × 5 × (600 − 300) = +3000 cal, absorbed.
3. B→C (isothermal at 600 K). The volume goes from 2VA to 4VA, so q = nRT ln 2 = 2 × 2 × 600 × 0.693 = +1663 cal, absorbed.
4. C→D (isochoric). Cooling at V = 4VA from 600 K to 300 K: q = nCVΔT = 2 × 3 × (−300) = −1800 cal, released.
5. D→A (isothermal at 300 K). The volume falls from 4VA back to VA, so q = 2 × 2 × 300 × ln(1/4) = 1200 × (−1.386) = −1663 cal, released.
6. (c) Summing: 3000 + 1663 − 1800 − 1663 = +1200 cal. Since ΔU = 0 over the cycle, that net heat is the work done by the gas; equivalently the work done on the gas is −1200 cal.
 TB/TA = VB/VA = 2  ->  TB = 600 K

 A->B isobaric  : 2(5)(300)        = +3000
 B->C isothermal: 2(2)(600)ln2     = +1663
 C->D isochoric : 2(3)(-300)       = -1800
 D->A isothermal: 2(2)(300)ln(1/4) = -1663
 -----------------------------------------
 total Q = +1200 cal = w done by gas
⚡ JEE Trick Learn the V–T geometry: through the origin means constant pressure, vertical means constant temperature, horizontal means constant volume. Misreading the axes is what makes these figures look hard.
Answer: TB = 600 K; q = +3000, +1663, −1800, −1663 cal; net Q = wby = 1200 cal
3Methane (ideal gas) initially at 25°C and 1 bar is heated at constant pressure until the volume has doubled. The molar heat capacity varies as CP = 22.34 + 48.1 × 10−3T, where CP is in J K−1 mol−1. Calculate molar (a) ΔH and (b) ΔU.Integrating a temperature-dependent CPMeasurements of ∆U and ∆H || Calorimetery || Thermodynamics Part - 5 || Class-11 | Chemistry🟡 Similar(a) ΔH = 13.064 kJ mol<sup>−1</sup>; (b) ΔU = 10.587 kJ mol<sup>−1</sup>
Concept At constant pressure, doubling the volume of an ideal gas doubles its absolute temperature. The heat capacity is temperature-dependent, so ΔH must be obtained by integrating CPdT rather than multiplying.
Key relation ΔH = ∫(a + bT)dT = a(T2 − T1) + (b/2)(T22 − T12); ΔU = ΔH − nRΔT The linear term integrates to a difference of squares, halved — not to bΔT.
Solution
1. Constant pressure with V doubling means T doubles: T1 = 298 K → T2 = 596 K.
2. Constant term: a(T2 − T1) = 22.34 × 298 = 6657.3 J mol−1.
3. Linear term: (b/2)(T22 − T12) = (48.1 × 10−3/2)(5962 − 2982) = 0.02405 × (355216 − 88804) = 0.02405 × 266412 = 6407.2 J mol−1.
4. (a) Adding: ΔH = 6657.3 + 6407.2 = 13064.5 J mol−1 = 13.064 kJ mol−1.
5. (b) At constant pressure Δ(PV) = nRΔT = 8.314 × 298 = 2477.6 J mol−1, so ΔU = 13064.5 − 2477.6 = 10.587 kJ mol−1.
6. Sense check: the two contributions to ΔH are almost equal here, which is the whole point of supplying a temperature-dependent CP. Treating CP as its 298 K value of 36.7 J K−1 mol−1 would have given only 10.9 kJ mol−1, an error of some 17%.
 constant P, V doubles  ->  T: 298 -> 596 K

 a dT       = 22.34(298)              = 6657.3
 (b/2) dT^2 = 0.02405(355216 - 88804) = 6407.2
 ------------------------------------------------
 dH = 13064.5 J/mol = 13.064 kJ/mol

 nR dT = 8.314(298) = 2477.6 J
 dU = 13064.5 - 2477.6 = 10.587 kJ/mol
⚡ JEE Trick For CP = a + bT, integrate term by term: the constant gives aΔT, the linear part gives (b/2)(T22 − T12). Writing bΔT instead is the standard mistake.
Answer: ΔH = 13.064 kJ mol−1, ΔU = 10.587 kJ mol−1
4Boron exists in allotropic forms all containing icosahedral units (12 corners, 20 faces) with boron atoms at all 12 corners and all bonds equivalent. Calculate the heat evolved at constant pressure (in kJ) per mole of boron atoms undergoing B(g) → B12 icosahedron, if ΔHBE(B−B) = 200 kJ/mol.Counting bonds in an icosahedronTHERMOCHEMISTRY । Class 11 (L3) I Bond energy | Resonance energy | Born Haber Cycle | JEE | NEET🟡 Similar500 kJ per mole of boron atoms (heat evolved)
Concept The whole question is a counting exercise in solid geometry. Forming the B12 cluster from free atoms creates one B−B bond per edge of the icosahedron, so the heat released per mole of atoms is (edges × bond energy) divided by the number of atoms.
Key relation Euler's formula V − E + F = 2, so E = V + F − 2 = 12 + 20 − 2 = 30 edges Bonds form along edges, and all bonds are stated to be equivalent.
Solution
1. The icosahedron has 12 vertices (one boron atom each) and 20 faces, as the question states.
2. Apply Euler's formula for a convex polyhedron: V − E + F = 2, so E = 12 + 20 − 2 = 30 edges.
3. Cross-check by counting: each vertex of an icosahedron touches 5 edges, so the total is 12 × 5 / 2 = 30, since every edge is shared by two vertices. The two counts agree.
4. Forming the cluster from 12 free boron atoms therefore creates 30 B−B bonds, releasing 30 × 200 = 6000 kJ per mole of B12 units.
5. The question asks per mole of boron atoms, and each B12 unit contains 12 atoms.
6. So the heat evolved is 6000/12 = 500 kJ per mole of boron atoms. The sign is negative for ΔH, since bond formation always releases energy.
 icosahedron : V = 12 vertices, F = 20 faces
 Euler       : E = V + F - 2 = 30 edges

 check: 12 vertices x 5 edges / 2 = 30

 30 bonds x 200 kJ = 6000 kJ per B12
 per mole of atoms : 6000 / 12 = 500 kJ
⚡ JEE Trick Whenever a cluster question gives vertices and faces, reach for Euler's formula to get the edges. Edges are bonds, and everything else is division.
Answer: 500 kJ evolved per mole of boron atoms (30 B−B bonds per B12 unit)
5A container is separated into two 1 litre compartments by a piston of negligible mass. In the lower compartment 0.1 mole of an ideal monatomic gas is filled at 0.1 bar pressure and the other compartment is empty. The stoppers are removed so that the gas expands to 2 litre. Heat is supplied to the gas so that finally the pressure of gas equals 0.1 bar. Calculate ΔH of the process in joule. [1 bar·litre = 100 J]ΔH for free expansion plus heatingExpansion of a gas in vacuum is called free expansion. Calculate the work done and the change in ...🟡 SimilarΔH = 25 J
Concept The process has two parts — a free expansion into the empty compartment, then heating until the pressure returns to its original value. Because enthalpy is a state function, only the initial and final states matter, and for an ideal gas ΔH can be written entirely in terms of PV.
Key relation For an ideal gas ΔH = nCPΔT = (5/2)nRΔT = (5/2)Δ(PV) nRT = PV, so the temperature never has to be computed at all.
Solution
1. Initial state: 0.1 mol in V1 = 1 L at P1 = 0.1 bar, so P1V1 = 0.1 bar L.
2. Final state: the gas fills V2 = 2 L and the pressure is brought back to P2 = 0.1 bar, so P2V2 = 0.2 bar L.
3. For a monatomic ideal gas CP = (5/2)R, so ΔH = n(5/2)RΔT = (5/2)Δ(nRT) = (5/2)Δ(PV).
4. Substitute: ΔH = (5/2)(0.2 − 0.1) = (5/2)(0.1) = 0.25 bar L.
5. Convert with the supplied factor 1 bar·L = 100 J: ΔH = 0.25 × 100 = 25 J.
6. Cross-check the long way: T1 = P1V1/nR = 0.1/(0.1 × 0.0831) = 12.03 K and T2 = 24.07 K, so ΔH = 0.1 × (5/2)(8.314) × 12.04 = 25.0 J. Note the free-expansion leg contributes nothing on its own, since q = w = 0 there.
 initial : 0.1 mol, 1 L, 0.1 bar -> PV = 0.1
 final   :          2 L, 0.1 bar -> PV = 0.2

 dH = (5/2) d(PV) = (5/2)(0.1) = 0.25 bar.L
    = 0.25 x 100 J = 25 J

 check: T1 = 12.03 K, T2 = 24.07 K
        dH = 0.1(2.5)(8.314)(12.04) = 25.0 J
⚡ JEE Trick For an ideal gas, ΔH = (CP/R)Δ(PV) and ΔU = (CV/R)Δ(PV). Working straight from the PV products skips the temperatures entirely and avoids two divisions.
Answer: ΔH = 25 J

1Match LIST-I with LIST-II: (A) Work done in reversible isothermal expansion of 2 mol of ideal gas from 2 dm3 to 20 dm3 at 300 K; (B) Work done in irreversible isothermal expansion of 1 mol ideal gas from 1 m3 to 3 m3 at 300 K against a constant pressure of 3 kPa; (C) Change in internal energy for adiabatic expansion of 1 mol ideal gas with change of temperature = 320 K and C̄V = (3/2)R; (D) Change in enthalpy at constant pressure of 1 mole ideal gas with change of temperature = 337 K and C̄P = (5/2)R.
LIST-II magnitudes in kJ: (I) 4, (II) 11.5, (III) 6, (IV) 7
