(a) A-III, B-II, C-IV, D-I   (b) A-II, B-III, C-I, D-IV   (c) A-I, B-II, C-III, D-IV   (d) A-II, B-I, C-III, D-IV   (2026 Level-6)		Matching work and energy magnitudesThermodynamics || Calculation of W, q, U, H for reversible isothermal expansion process🟡 Similarb
Concept Four standard formulae, one per row, and four magnitudes to attach. Because only magnitudes are compared, the signs can be dropped; each row is a single substitution.
Key relation wrev = −nRT ln(Vf/Vi); wirrev = −pexΔV; ΔU = nCVΔT; ΔH = nCPΔT Every row uses R = 8.314 J K−1 mol−1.
Solution
1. (A) Reversible isothermal, 2 mol, 2 → 20 dm3 at 300 K: |w| = 2 × 8.314 × 300 × ln 10 = 4988 × 2.303 = 11.5 kJ → (II).
2. (B) Irreversible isothermal against 3 kPa, 1 → 3 m3: |w| = 3000 × 2 = 6000 J = 6 kJ → (III).
3. (C) Adiabatic expansion, 1 mol, ΔT = 320 K, CV = (3/2)R: |ΔU| = 1.5 × 8.314 × 320 = 3991 J ≈ 4 kJ → (I).
4. (D) Constant pressure, 1 mol, ΔT = 337 K, CP = (5/2)R: |ΔH| = 2.5 × 8.314 × 337 = 7005 J ≈ 7 kJ → (IV).
5. Assemble: A-II, B-III, C-I, D-IV, which is option (b).
6. Note how the numbers were chosen to be distinguishable: 11.5, 6, 4 and 7 kJ are all different enough that a single row computed correctly already fixes the option.
 (A) 2(8.314)(300) ln10 = 11488 J  -> 11.5  II
 (B) 3000 x 2          =  6000 J  ->  6     III
 (C) 1.5(8.314)(320)   =  3991 J  ->  4     I
 (D) 2.5(8.314)(337)   =  7005 J  ->  7     IV
 ------------------------------------------------
 A-II, B-III, C-I, D-IV        = option (b)
⚡ JEE Trick In magnitude-matching questions, compute the easiest row first. Row (B) needs only pΔV and immediately eliminates every option that does not send B to III.
Answer: (b) A-II, B-III, C-I, D-IV
2The heat of atomisation of methane and ethane are 'x' kJ mol−1 and 'y' kJ mol−1 respectively. The longest wavelength (λ) of light capable of breaking the C−C bond can be expressed in SI unit as:
(a) (hc/1000)·((y − 6x)/4)−1   (b) NAhc/[250(4y − 6x)]   (c) NAhc/[250(y − 6x)]   (d) NAhc·(y − 6x/4)−1   (2026 Level-6)		Bond energy converted to a photon wavelengthTHERMOCHEMISTRY । Class 11 (L3) I Bond energy | Resonance energy | Born Haber Cycle | JEE | NEET🟡 Similarb
Concept Two atomisation enthalpies contain enough information to isolate the C−C bond energy: methane gives the C−H value directly, and ethane then yields C−C by subtraction. Converting that per-mole energy to a per-photon energy gives the longest wavelength that can break the bond.
Key relation E = hc/λ per photon, so λ = NAhc/Emolar The longest wavelength corresponds to a photon carrying exactly the bond energy.
Solution
1. Methane: CH4 → C + 4H costs x kJ mol−1 and breaks four equivalent C−H bonds, so BE(C−H) = x/4.
2. Ethane: C2H6 → 2C + 6H costs y kJ mol−1 and breaks one C−C plus six C−H bonds, so y = BE(C−C) + 6(x/4).
3. Rearranging, BE(C−C) = y − 6x/4 = (4y − 6x)/4 kJ mol−1.
4. Convert to joules per mole: Emolar = (4y − 6x) × 1000/4 = 250(4y − 6x) J mol−1.
5. The longest wavelength that still supplies this much energy per photon satisfies NAhc/λ = Emolar, so λ = NAhc/[250(4y − 6x)] — option (b).
6. Check the structure of the answer: the 250 is exactly the 1000/4 from combining the kJ–to–J conversion with the quarter in the bond-energy expression. Options (a) and (d) omit NA and so give a wavelength per mole rather than per photon.
 CH4  : x = 4 BE(C-H)   ->  BE(C-H) = x/4
 C2H6 : y = BE(C-C) + 6(x/4)
        BE(C-C) = (4y - 6x)/4 kJ/mol
               = 250(4y - 6x) J/mol

 lambda = NA h c / E = NA h c / [250(4y - 6x)]
⚡ JEE Trick Whenever a bond energy is turned into a wavelength, Avogadro's number must appear: tabulated bond energies are per mole, photon energies are per molecule.
Answer: (b) NAhc/[250(4y − 6x)]
3If the enthalpy of sublimation of Li is 155 kJ mol−1, enthalpy of dissociation of F2 is 150 kJ mol−1, ionization enthalpy of Li is 520 kJ mol−1, electron gain enthalpy of F is −313 kJ mol−1 and standard enthalpy of formation of LiF is −594 kJ mol−1, the magnitude of lattice enthalpy of LiF is ______ kJ mol−1. (Nearest integer) (2026 Level-5)Born–Haber cycle for LiFThe EASIEST Method for Solving Born–Haber Cycles🟢 Exact1031 kJ mol<sup>−1</sup>
Concept A Born–Haber cycle equates the direct formation enthalpy of the ionic solid with the sum of the step-by-step route: sublime the metal, ionise it, dissociate half the halogen, attach the electron, then bring the ions together. The lattice enthalpy is the only unknown.
Key relation ΔfH = ΔHsub + IE + ½ΔHdis + ΔHeg − Ulattice U is defined here as the energy released when the gaseous ions form the lattice, so it enters with a minus sign.
Solution
1. Data: ΔHsub(Li) = 155, IE(Li) = 520, ΔHdis(F2) = 150, ΔHeg(F) = −313, ΔfH(LiF) = −594 kJ mol−1.
2. Only half a mole of F2 is needed per mole of LiF, so the dissociation contributes ½(150) = 75 kJ.
3. Substitute: −594 = 155 + 520 + 75 + (−313) − U.
4. Collect the known terms: 155 + 520 + 75 − 313 = 437 kJ.
5. So −594 = 437 − U, giving U = 437 + 594 = 1031 kJ mol−1.
6. Sense check: lattice enthalpies of small, singly charged ion pairs run around 1000 kJ mol−1, and LiF — with the smallest cation and anion of the alkali halides — sits at the top of that range. The value is exactly what the tiny ionic radii predict.
 dfH = dH_sub + IE + (1/2)dH_dis + dH_eg - U

 -594 = 155 + 520 + 75 - 313 - U
 -594 = 437 - U
    U = 437 + 594 = 1031 kJ/mol

 half of F2 only: 150/2 = 75, not 150
⚡ JEE Trick The single most common Born–Haber slip is using the full dissociation enthalpy of the halogen instead of half of it. Check the stoichiometry of the formation equation before substituting.
Answer: lattice enthalpy of LiF = 1031 kJ mol−1
4Which of the following graphs between pressure 'P' versus volume 'V' represents the maximum work done? Options show (a) a reversible isothermal curve from 2.0 bar/22.4 L to 1.0 bar/44.8 L, (b) a single-step expansion at 2.0 bar to 22.4 L, (c) a two-stage curve between 22.4 and 44.8 L, (d) a single-step expansion against 1.0 bar. (2026 Level-4)Largest area under a P–V curveCalculate Work for Reversible and Irreversible Expansion/Compression🟡 Similarb
Concept Work is the area swept out under the P–V trace, so the graph enclosing the greatest area represents the greatest work. Two of the options are closed cycles, whose net work is only the small loop area, and that alone eliminates them.
Key relation wby = ∫PdV = area under the curve; for a closed cycle only the enclosed loop counts 1 bar·L = 100 J, so areas can be compared directly in bar·L.
Solution
1. (a) shows a closed cycle: expansion down the isotherm from (22.4 L, 2.0 bar) to (44.8 L, 1.0 bar), return along P = 1.0 bar and back up at constant volume. The net work is only the thin enclosed loop.
2. (c) is also a closed cycle, out along one curve and back along another, so again only the enclosed sliver counts.
3. (d) is a single reversible isothermal expansion from 22.4 L to 44.8 L. Its area is nRT ln 2 = (2.0)(22.4)(0.693) = 31.1 bar·L, about 3.1 kJ.
4. (b) is a full rectangle: the pressure stays at about 2.0 bar all the way out to 22.4 L before dropping. Its area is roughly 2.0 × 22.4 = 44.8 bar·L, about 4.5 kJ.
5. Comparing the four, the rectangle in (b) sweeps out the largest area, so it represents the maximum work done.
6. The general lesson stands behind the arithmetic: for a given volume change, work grows with the pressure maintained during the expansion, and a full-height rectangle is the largest area any P–V path can enclose.
 (a) closed cycle : net work = small loop
 (c) closed cycle : net work = small loop
 (d) isothermal   : 2.0 x 22.4 x ln2 = 31.1 bar.L
 (b) rectangle    : 2.0 x 22.4       = 44.8 bar.L

 largest area  ->  option (b)
⚡ JEE Trick Spot the closed loops first. A cycle's net work is the enclosed area, almost always far smaller than the area under a one-way expansion, so cycles are rarely the "maximum work" answer.
Answer: (b) — the rectangular path encloses the largest area
5Consider the following data: ΔfH°(methane, g) = −X kJ mol−1; enthalpy of sublimation of graphite = Y kJ mol−1; dissociation enthalpy of H2 = Z kJ mol−1. The bond enthalpy of the C−H bond is given by:
(a) (X + Y + 2Z)/4   (b) (X + Y + 4Z)/2   (c) X + Y + Z   (d) (−X + Y + Z)/4   (2026 Level-4)		C−H bond enthalpy from a cycle11 Chap 4 | Chemical Bonding and Molecular Structure 03| Lattice Energy | Born Haber Cycle IIT JEE |🟡 Similara
Concept The C−H bond enthalpy is obtained by routing the formation of methane through free atoms. Subliming the graphite and dissociating the hydrogen supplies the atoms; forming four C−H bonds releases four times the bond enthalpy.
Key relation ΔfH(CH4) = ΔHsub(C) + 2ΔHdis(H2) − 4×BE(C−H) Two moles of H2 are needed to supply the four hydrogen atoms.
Solution
1. Formation reaction: C(s) + 2H2(g) → CH4(g), with ΔfH = −X kJ mol−1.
2. Route it through atoms. Subliming graphite costs Y; dissociating two moles of H2 costs 2Z; forming four C−H bonds releases 4×BE.
3. Equate the two routes: −X = Y + 2Z − 4×BE.
4. Rearrange: 4×BE = Y + 2Z + X.
5. Hence BE(C−H) = (X + Y + 2Z)/4, which is option (a).
6. Watch the two traps the distractors exploit: the coefficient on Z must be 2 because four H atoms need two H−H bonds broken, and X enters with a plus sign because the given formation enthalpy is −X, not X. Option (d) gets both signs wrong.
7. Numerically, with X = 74.8, Y = 716.7 and Z = 436 kJ mol−1, this gives BE(C−H) = (74.8 + 716.7 + 872)/4 = 416 kJ mol−1, the accepted average value.
 C(s) + 2H2(g) -> CH4(g)      dfH = -X

 sublime C      : + Y
 break 2 H-H    : + 2Z
 form 4 C-H     : - 4 BE
 -------------------------------
 -X = Y + 2Z - 4 BE
 BE = (X + Y + 2Z) / 4
⚡ JEE Trick Count the atoms the product actually needs before writing the cycle. Methane needs four H atoms, which is two H−H bonds — the factor of 2 on Z is where most attempts go wrong.
Answer: (a) (X + Y + 2Z)/4
6Match List-I (isothermal process for ideal gas system) with List-II (work done, Vf > Vi): (A) Reversible expansion; (B) Free expansion; (C) Irreversible expansion; (D) Irreversible compression. List-II: (I) w = 0; (II) w = −nRT ln(Vf/Vi); (III) w = −pex(Vf − Vi); (IV) w = −pex(Vi − Vf).
(a) A-IV, B-I, C-III, D-II   (b) A-IV, B-II, C-III, D-I   (c) A-I, B-III, C-II, D-IV   (d) A-II, B-I, C-III, D-IV   (2026 Level-4)		Work expressions for four expansionsClass 11 Chapter 6 | Thermodynamics 04 | Work done in Isothermal and Adiabatic Expansion of Gas |🟡 Similard
Concept Four isothermal processes, four work expressions. The reversible case carries the logarithm, free expansion does no work at all, and the two irreversible cases share the pexΔV form written with the volume difference in opposite orders.
Key relation w = −pexΔV in general; w = −nRT ln(Vf/Vi) when reversible; w = 0 into vacuum Free expansion has pex = 0, so no work is done however large the volume change.
Solution
1. (A) Reversible expansion. The external pressure tracks the gas pressure at every instant, so the work integrates to w = −nRT ln(Vf/Vi) → (II).
2. (B) Free expansion. The gas expands into vacuum, so pex = 0 and w = 0 → (I). This is true whether or not the process is adiabatic.
3. (C) Irreversible expansion. Against a fixed external pressure the work is w = −pex(Vf − Vi) → (III). With Vf > Vi this is negative, as expansion work must be.
4. (D) Irreversible compression. The same physical formula applies, but the list writes it with the difference reversed as w = −pex(Vi − Vf) → (IV), which is positive for a compression since Vi > Vf.
5. Assemble: A-II, B-I, C-III, D-IV, which is option (d).
6. Note that entries (III) and (IV) are the same physics; the question separates them purely so that expansion and compression each get a distinct label with the correct sign already built in.
 (A) reversible expansion   -> -nRT ln(Vf/Vi)  II
 (B) free expansion         -> w = 0            I
 (C) irreversible expansion -> -pex(Vf - Vi)  III
 (D) irreversible compress. -> -pex(Vi - Vf)   IV
 ---------------------------------------------
 A-II, B-I, C-III, D-IV         = option (d)
⚡ JEE Trick Free expansion is the giveaway row: it can only pair with w = 0. Fixing B-I first removes half the options before any real thinking.
Answer: (d) A-II, B-I, C-III, D-IV
7The plot of log10K vs 1/T gives a straight line. The intercept and slope respectively are (where K is equilibrium constant):
(a) 2.303R/ΔH°, 2.303R/ΔS°   (b) ΔS°/2.303R, −ΔH°/2.303R   (c) ΔS°R/2.303, ΔH°R/2.303   (d) −ΔH°/2.303R, ΔS°/2.303R   (2026 Level-4)		van't Hoff plot intercept and slopeVan’t Hoff Equation Graph Trick | JEE Main 2026 Physical Chemistry🟢 Exactb
Concept Combining ΔG° = −RT ln K with ΔG° = ΔH° − TΔS° produces a straight-line relation between log K and 1/T. Reading it against y = mx + c identifies the slope and intercept at a glance.
Key relation log K = −ΔH°/(2.303R) × (1/T) + ΔS°/(2.303R) The 2.303 appears because the plot uses log10 rather than ln.
Solution
1. Start from ΔG° = −2.303 RT log K and ΔG° = ΔH° − TΔS°.
2. Equate them: −2.303 RT log K = ΔH° − TΔS°.
3. Divide through by −2.303 RT: log K = −ΔH°/(2.303RT) + ΔS°/(2.303R).
4. Compare with y = mx + c where y = log K and x = 1/T. The coefficient of 1/T is the slope = −ΔH°/(2.303R) and the constant term is the intercept = ΔS°/(2.303R).
5. The question asks for intercept then slope in that order, which is exactly option (b): ΔS°/(2.303R), −ΔH°/(2.303R).
6. Option (d) has the two swapped, and options (a) and (c) put R in the numerator instead of the denominator. A quick dimension check settles it: log K is a pure number, so ΔS° must be divided by R, not multiplied.
 dG0 = -2.303 RT logK = dH0 - T dS0

 logK = -dH0/(2.303 R) . (1/T) + dS0/(2.303 R)
          ^^^^^^^^^^^^^^          ^^^^^^^^^^^^
             slope                  intercept

 exothermic (dH0 < 0) -> positive slope
⚡ JEE Trick An exothermic reaction has ΔH° < 0 and therefore a positive slope on a log K versus 1/T plot — K falls as the temperature rises, exactly as Le Chatelier predicts.
Answer: (b) intercept ΔS°/(2.303R), slope −ΔH°/(2.303R)
8A cup of water at 5°C (system) is placed in a microwave oven and the oven is turned on for one minute during which the water begins to boil. Which of the following option is true?
(a) q = +ve, w = 0, ΔU = −ve   (b) q = +ve, w = −ve, ΔU = +ve   (c) q = −ve, w = −ve, ΔU = −ve   (d) q = +ve, w = −ve, ΔU = −ve   (2026 Level-3)		Signs of q, w and ΔU on boilingMeasurements of ∆U and ∆H || Calorimetery || Thermodynamics Part - 5 || Class-11 | Chemistry🟡 Similarb
Concept A microwave pours energy into the water, and once boiling starts the steam produced pushes back the atmosphere. So heat flows in, work is done by the system, and the net effect on the internal energy has to be settled by comparing the two.
Key relation ΔU = q + w, with q > 0 for heat absorbed and w < 0 for work done by the system Warming from 5 °C to 100 °C and then vaporising both raise the internal energy far more than the expansion work removes.
Solution
1. The oven delivers energy to the water, so heat is absorbed and q is positive. That immediately eliminates option (c).
2. Once the water boils, liquid turns to vapour and the volume increases enormously, so the system does work on the atmosphere and w is negative. That eliminates option (a), which claims w = 0.
3. Now compare magnitudes. Heating 1 mol of water from 5 °C to 100 °C takes about 7.2 kJ and vaporising it about 40.7 kJ, so q is tens of kilojoules.
4. The expansion work for one mole of vapour is only |w| = RT ≈ 8.314 × 373 = 3.1 kJ.
5. Since q greatly exceeds |w|, ΔU = q + w is comfortably positive. The water ends up hotter and partly vaporised, both of which store energy.
6. The combination q = +ve, w = −ve, ΔU = +ve is option (b). Option (d) has the right q and w but claims ΔU is negative, which would mean the water cooled while absorbing heat.
 microwave heats water -> q > 0
 boiling makes vapour  -> expansion -> w < 0

 q  ~ 7.2 kJ (heating) + 40.7 kJ (vaporising)
 |w| ~ RT = 8.314 x 373 = 3.1 kJ

 q >> |w|  ->  dU = q + w > 0        (b)
⚡ JEE Trick When both q and w are non-zero, decide ΔU by size, not by sign alone. Latent heats run to tens of kilojoules while PV work is only a few, so heat almost always wins.
Answer: (b) q = +ve, w = −ve, ΔU = +ve
920.0 dm3 of an ideal gas 'X' at 600 K and 0.5 MPa undergoes isothermal reversible expansion until the pressure of the gas is 0.2 MPa. Which of the following option is correct? (log 2 = 0.3010, log 5 = 0.6989)
(a) w = −9.1 kJ, ΔU = 0, ΔH = 0, q = 9.1 kJ   (b) w = 9.1 J, ΔU = 9.1 J, ΔH = 0, q = 0   (c) w = +4.1 kJ, ΔU = 0, ΔH = 0, q = −4.1 kJ   (d) w = −3.9 kJ, ΔU = 0, ΔH = 0, q = 3.9 kJ   (2026 Level-3)		Isothermal reversible expansion quantitiesThermodynamics || Calculation of W, q, U, H for reversible isothermal expansion process🟢 Exacta
Concept A reversible isothermal expansion of an ideal gas fixes three of the four quantities at once: ΔU and ΔH are both zero, and q is the exact negative of w. Only the magnitude has to be computed, and the pressure ratio can be used in place of the volume ratio.
Key relation w = −nRT ln(V2/V1) = −nRT ln(P1/P2), with nRT = P1V1 Using P1V1 directly avoids ever finding n or R separately.
Solution
1. Compute nRT straight from the initial state: nRT = P1V1 = (0.5 × 106 Pa)(20 × 10−3 m3) = 104 J = 10 kJ.
2. The pressure ratio is P1/P2 = 0.5/0.2 = 2.5, which equals V2/V1 at constant temperature.
3. Logarithm: ln 2.5 = 2.303 × log 2.5 = 2.303 × (0.6989 − 0.3010) = 2.303 × 0.3979 = 0.916.
4. Work: w = −(10000)(0.916) = −9163 J ≈ −9.1 kJ, negative because the gas expands and does work on the surroundings.
5. Since the gas is ideal and the temperature is constant, ΔU = 0 and ΔH = 0, so the first law gives q = −w = +9.1 kJ.
6. That set — w = −9.1 kJ, ΔU = 0, ΔH = 0, q = +9.1 kJ — is option (a). Option (c) reverses both signs and option (d) uses ln 2 rather than ln 2.5.
 nRT = P1V1 = (0.5e6)(20e-3) = 10000 J

 P1/P2 = 0.5/0.2 = 2.5 = V2/V1
 ln 2.5 = 2.303(0.6989 - 0.3010) = 0.916

 w = -10000(0.916) = -9163 J = -9.1 kJ
 dU = dH = 0 ; q = +9.1 kJ
⚡ JEE Trick Read nRT off the initial state as P1V1. It is one multiplication and removes any need for the number of moles, the gas constant or the temperature.
Answer: (a) w = −9.1 kJ, ΔU = 0, ΔH = 0, q = 9.1 kJ
10Ice at −5°C is heated to become vapor with temperature of 110°C at atmospheric pressure. The entropy change associated with this process can be obtained from which integral expression? The correct option separates the two heating legs of ice and water and the two transitions, each divided by T. (JEE Main 2025, 23 Jan Shift 1, Level-6)Assembling a multi-step ΔS integralIce at –5°C is heated to become vapor with temperature of 110°C at atmospheric pressure. The entropy🟢 Exactb
Concept Taking ice at −5 °C all the way to vapour at 110 °C crosses two phase transitions, so the entropy change is a sum of five terms: three heating legs and two transitions. Every heating leg must integrate CP/T, and every transition contributes ΔH/T at its own fixed temperature.
Key relation Heating: ΔS = ∫(CP,m/T)dT  |  Transition: ΔS = ΔHm/Ttrans The integrand is CP/T, not CP — that single division is what separates the correct option from the decoy.
Solution
1. Map out the path: ice 268 K → 273 K, fusion at 273 K, water 273 K → 373 K, vaporisation at 373 K, steam 373 K → 383 K.
2. Each heating leg contributes ∫(CP,m/T)dT between its own limits, so three integrals appear with limits 268–273, 273–373 and 373–383.
3. Each transition contributes its latent enthalpy divided by the temperature at which it occurs: ΔHm,fusion/Tf and ΔHm,vaporisation/Tb.
4. Option (b) is the only one that shows all five terms with CP,m/T inside every integral, so it is correct.
5. Option (a) is the trap: it has the right five terms but writes ∫CP,mdT without dividing by T, which would give an enthalpy rather than an entropy.
6. Options (c) and (d) collapse the whole range into a single integral from 268 K to 383 K, which ignores the fact that CP changes discontinuously at each phase boundary; (d) also lacks the 1/T inside the integral.
 268 -> 273  ice     : int Cp,m/T dT
 273         fusion  : dHm,fus / Tf
 273 -> 373  water   : int Cp,m/T dT
 373         vapour  : dHm,vap / Tb
 373 -> 383  steam   : int Cp,m/T dT

 (a) has Cp,m dT (no 1/T)  -> wrong units
⚡ JEE Trick Run a units check on integral options. Entropy must come out in J K−1, so a CPdT integral without the 1/T is dimensionally an enthalpy and can be struck out instantly.
Answer: (b) — three CP,m/T integrals plus ΔHfus/Tf and ΔHvap/Tb
11The bond dissociation enthalpy of X2, ΔHbond, calculated from the given data is ______ kJ mol−1. (Nearest integer) Given M+X−(s) → M+(g) + X−(g), ΔH*lattice = 800; M(s) → M(g), ΔH°sub = 100; M(g) → M+(g) + e−, ΔHi = 500; X(g) + e− → X−(g), ΔH*eg = −300; M(s) + ½X2(g) → M+X−(s), ΔH°f = −400 kJ mol−1. (JEE Main 2025, 23 Jan Shift 2, Level-6)Born–Haber solved for bond enthalpyThe EASIEST Method for Solving Born–Haber Cycles🟢 Exact200 kJ mol<sup>−1</sup>
Concept The Born–Haber cycle is run with the bond dissociation enthalpy as the unknown instead of the lattice energy. Note the sign convention supplied: the lattice term is given as the energy to pull the solid apart into gaseous ions, so it enters the formation cycle with a minus sign.
Key relation ΔfH° = ΔH°sub + ΔHi + ½ΔHbond + ΔH*eg − ΔH*lattice Only half a mole of X2 is consumed per mole of M+X−.
Solution
1. Target reaction: M(s) + ½X2(g) → M+X−(s) with ΔfH° = −400 kJ mol−1.
2. Break the route into steps: sublimation +100, ionisation +500, half-dissociation +½ΔHbond, electron gain −300.
3. The final step brings the gaseous ions together. Since M+X−(s) → M+(g) + X−(g) costs +800, the reverse releases −800 kJ mol−1.
4. Sum them: −400 = 100 + 500 + ½ΔHbond − 300 − 800.
5. Collect the constants: 100 + 500 − 300 − 800 = −500, so −400 = −500 + ½ΔHbond.
6. Hence ½ΔHbond = 100 and ΔHbond = 200 kJ mol−1. The value is modest, comparable to a halogen–halogen bond such as I−I at 151 or Cl−Cl at 242 kJ mol−1.
 M(s) + 1/2 X2(g) -> M+X-(s)    dfH = -400

 sublime M        : +100
 ionise M         : +500
 half X2 bond     : +(1/2) dH_bond
 electron gain    : -300
 ions -> lattice  : -800
 ---------------------------------------
 -400 = -500 + (1/2) dH_bond
 dH_bond = 200 kJ/mol
⚡ JEE Trick Read the direction of the lattice term before using it. Given as M+X−(s) → ions it is positive and must be subtracted; quoted the other way round it would be added.
Answer: ΔHbond(X2) = 200 kJ mol−1
12The formation enthalpies ΔfH° for H(g) and O(g) are 220.0 and 250.0 kJ mol−1 respectively at 298.15 K, and ΔfH° for H2O(g) is −242.0 kJ mol−1 at the same temperature. The average bond enthalpy of the O−H bond in water at 298.15 K is ______ kJ mol−1 (nearest integer). (JEE Main 2025, 28 Jan Shift 1, Level-6)Average O−H bond enthalpyTHERMOCHEMISTRY । Class 11 (L3) I Bond energy | Resonance energy | Born Haber Cycle | JEE | NEET🟡 Similar466 kJ mol<sup>−1</sup>
Concept The average O−H bond enthalpy is the energy to atomise one mole of water vapour, shared between its two bonds. Atomisation is found by combining the formation enthalpies of the free atoms with that of the molecule.
Key relation ΔHatom = [2ΔfH°(H) + ΔfH°(O)] − ΔfH°(H2O); BEavg = ΔHatom/2 Water has exactly two O−H bonds, so the average is a simple halving.
Solution
1. Target process: H2O(g) → 2H(g) + O(g), breaking both O−H bonds.
2. Products side: two hydrogen atoms at 220.0 each plus one oxygen atom at 250.0, giving 2(220.0) + 250.0 = 690.0 kJ mol−1.
3. Reactant side: water vapour at −242.0 kJ mol−1.
4. Atomisation enthalpy: ΔHatom = 690.0 − (−242.0) = 932.0 kJ mol−1. Strongly positive, as breaking bonds must be.
5. Divide between the two bonds: BEavg(O−H) = 932.0/2 = 466 kJ mol−1.
6. Note the word "average". The first O−H bond of water actually costs about 499 kJ mol−1 and the second only about 428; 466 is their mean, which is what bond-energy tables list.
 H2O(g) -> 2H(g) + O(g)

 products : 2(220.0) + 250.0 = 690.0 kJ
 reactant :                    -242.0 kJ
 -----------------------------------------
 dH_atom = 690.0 + 242.0 = 932.0 kJ/mol

 two O-H bonds : 932.0 / 2 = 466 kJ/mol
⚡ JEE Trick Formation enthalpies of gaseous atoms are always positive — they include the cost of splitting the element. Subtracting the molecule's (usually negative) value then adds, which is why the atomisation total comes out so large.
Answer: average O−H bond enthalpy = 466 kJ mol−1
13Consider the following data: heat of formation of CO2(g) = −393.5 kJ mol−1; heat of formation of H2O(l) = −286.0 kJ mol−1; heat of combustion of benzene = −3267.0 kJ mol−1. The heat of formation of benzene is ______ kJ mol−1. (Nearest integer) (JEE Main 2025, 28 Jan Shift 2, Level-6)ΔfH of benzene from combustionEnthalpy of Formation Reaction & Heat of Combustion, Enthalpy Change Problems Chemistry🟡 Similar+48 kJ mol<sup>−1</sup>
Concept A combustion enthalpy plus the formation enthalpies of the combustion products give the formation enthalpy of the fuel. Rearranging the combustion equation is all that is required.
Key relation ΔcH = ΣΔfH(products) − ΔfH(fuel), so ΔfH(fuel) = ΣΔfH(products) − ΔcH Benzene burns as C6H6 + (15/2)O2 → 6CO2 + 3H2O(l).
Solution
1. Write the balanced combustion: C6H6(l) + (15/2)O2(g) → 6CO2(g) + 3H2O(l), with ΔcH = −3267.0 kJ mol−1.
2. Product formation enthalpies: 6 × (−393.5) = −2361.0 kJ for the carbon dioxide.
3. And 3 × (−286.0) = −858.0 kJ for the water. Their sum is −3219.0 kJ.
4. Oxygen is an element in its standard state, so it contributes nothing.
5. Rearrange: ΔfH(C6H6) = −3219.0 − (−3267.0) = +48 kJ mol−1.
6. The positive sign is correct and chemically meaningful: benzene is endothermic with respect to its elements, lying above graphite and hydrogen in energy, which is why it is a good fuel despite its aromatic stability.
 C6H6(l) + 15/2 O2 -> 6CO2 + 3H2O(l)
 dcH = -3267.0 kJ/mol

 products : 6(-393.5) = -2361.0
            3(-286.0) =  -858.0
            sum       = -3219.0 kJ

 dfH(C6H6) = -3219.0 + 3267.0 = +48 kJ/mol
⚡ JEE Trick Get the water phase right. Using H2O(g) instead of H2O(l) would shift the answer by 3 × 44 = 132 kJ mol−1, since the combustion value quoted assumes liquid water.
Answer: ΔfH(benzene) = +48 kJ mol−1
14ΔvapH° for water is +40.79 kJ mol−1 at 1 bar and 100°C. Change in internal energy for this vapourisation under the same condition is ______ kJ mol−1. (Integer answer) (Given R = 8.3 J K−1 mol−1) (JEE Main 2025, 8 April Shift 2, Level-6)ΔU from ΔH for vaporisationMeasurements of ∆U and ∆H || Calorimetery || Thermodynamics Part - 5 || Class-11 | Chemistry🟡 Similar38 kJ mol<sup>−1</sup> (37.7 before rounding)
Concept Vaporising one mole of liquid creates one mole of gas, so Δng = +1 and the enthalpy exceeds the internal energy change by exactly RT. Part of the heat supplied goes into pushing back the atmosphere rather than into the molecules.
Key relation ΔU = ΔH − ΔngRT The liquid's volume is negligible, so only the vapour counts towards Δng.
Solution
1. Process: H2O(l) → H2O(g) at 100 °C = 373 K and 1 bar, with ΔH = +40.79 kJ mol−1.
2. Gas change: one mole of vapour appears from a liquid, so Δng = +1.
3. Correction term: ΔngRT = (1)(8.3)(373) = 3095.9 J = 3.10 kJ mol−1.
4. Substitute: ΔU = 40.79 − 3.10 = 37.69 kJ mol−1.
5. To the nearest integer that is 38 kJ mol−1, which is what the answer requires.
6. Interpretation: of the 40.79 kJ supplied, about 3.1 kJ is spent doing expansion work against the 1 bar atmosphere and only 37.7 kJ ends up stored in the vapour as internal energy.
7. Sign check: Δng is positive here, so ΔU must be smaller than ΔH. An answer above 40.79 kJ mol−1 would mean the correction had been added rather than subtracted.
 H2O(l) -> H2O(g)      dn_g = +1
 dH = 40.79 kJ/mol   T = 373 K   R = 8.3

 dn_g R T = 8.3 x 373 = 3095.9 J = 3.10 kJ
 dU = 40.79 - 3.10 = 37.69 kJ/mol
    = 38 kJ/mol  (nearest integer)
⚡ JEE Trick RT at 373 K is close to 3.1 kJ mol−1 and at 298 K close to 2.5 kJ mol−1. Memorising those two makes every ΔH ↔ ΔU conversion a single subtraction.
Answer: ΔU ≈ 38 kJ mol−1
15Considering ideal gas behavior, the expansion work done (in kJ) when 144 g of water is electrolyzed completely under constant pressure at 300 K is ______. Use R = 8.3 J K−1 mol−1; atomic masses H = 1, O = 16. (JEE Adv. 2025, Paper-1, Level-6)Expansion work of electrolysisClass 11 Chapter 6 | Thermodynamics 03 | Work Done by a Gas | Work Done on a Gs | IIT JEE / NEET |🟡 Similar29.88 kJ (magnitude)
Concept Electrolysing water turns a liquid into two gases, so the system expands sharply against the atmosphere. At constant pressure the expansion work depends only on the number of moles of gas produced, not on the details of the electrolysis.
Key relation w = −PΔV = −ΔngRT The liquid consumed has negligible volume, so every mole of gas formed counts in full.
Solution
1. Reaction: 2H2O(l) → 2H2(g) + O2(g).
2. Moles of water: the molar mass is 18 g mol−1, so 144 g is 144/18 = 8 mol.
3. Gas produced: 8 mol of water gives 8 mol of H2 and 4 mol of O2, a total of Δng = 12 mol.
4. Expansion work: w = −ΔngRT = −(12)(8.3)(300).
5. Evaluate: 12 × 8.3 = 99.6, and 99.6 × 300 = 29880 J, so w = −29880 J = −29.88 kJ.
6. The magnitude asked for is 29.88 kJ. The sign is negative because the system does work on the surroundings.
7. Note what this work is not: it is the expansion of the product gases against the atmosphere, entirely separate from the electrical energy the cell must supply to split the water in the first place.
8. A quick scale check: 12 moles of gas at 300 K occupy about 295 L, and pushing that volume out against 1 bar costs roughly 29.5 kJ — matching the answer.
 2H2O(l) -> 2H2(g) + O2(g)

 144 g / 18 = 8 mol water
 gases: 8 mol H2 + 4 mol O2 = 12 mol
 dn_g = +12 (liquid volume negligible)

 w = -(12)(8.3)(300) = -29880 J = -29.88 kJ
⚡ JEE Trick Count only the gases. The three moles of gas per two moles of water is the whole content of the question — the electrolysis itself never enters the arithmetic.
Answer: expansion work = 29.88 kJ (w = −29.88 kJ)
16A liquid when kept inside a thermally insulated closed vessel at 25°C was mechanically stirred from outside. What will be the correct option for the following thermodynamic parameters?
(a) ΔU < 0, q = 0, w > 0   (b) ΔU = 0, q = 0, w = 0   (c) ΔU > 0, q = 0, w > 0   (d) ΔU = 0, q < 0, w > 0   (JEE Main 2025, 22 Jan Shift 1, Level-4)		Stirring work in an insulated vesselExpansion of a gas in vacuum is called free expansion. Calculate the work done and the change in ...🟡 Similarc
Concept A thermally insulated vessel blocks all heat flow, so q = 0. Stirring nevertheless transfers energy across the boundary as work, and with no route for that energy to escape, the internal energy must rise.
Key relation ΔU = q + w; insulated ⇒ q = 0, so ΔU = w Work done on the system is positive in this sign convention.
Solution
1. The vessel is thermally insulated, so no heat crosses the boundary in either direction: q = 0. That alone eliminates option (d), which has q < 0.
2. The liquid is stirred from outside, so the surroundings do work on the system: w > 0. That eliminates option (b), which sets w = 0.
3. Apply the first law: ΔU = q + w = 0 + w = w, and since w is positive, ΔU > 0.
4. That rules out option (a), which pairs positive work with a fall in internal energy — impossible when no heat can leave.
5. Only option (c) has all three signs consistent: ΔU > 0, q = 0, w > 0.
6. Physically the stirring work degrades into thermal motion, so the liquid warms above 25 °C. This is Joule's classic paddle-wheel experiment, the observation that first fixed the mechanical equivalent of heat.
7. Note also that the process is irreversible: the stirring could never be run backwards to cool the liquid and return the work, so entropy is generated even though no heat crosses the boundary.
 insulated  ->  q = 0
 stirred    ->  w > 0  (work done ON system)
 -----------------------------------------
 dU = q + w = w > 0

 the liquid warms up: Joule's paddle wheel
⚡ JEE Trick "Insulated" and "adiabatic" both mean q = 0, but neither means ΔU = 0. Energy can still enter as work, and in an insulated vessel it has nowhere to go but the internal energy.
Answer: (c) ΔU > 0, q = 0, w > 0
17The effect of temperature on spontaneity of reactions are represented as: (A) ΔH +, ΔS −, any T, Non spontaneous; (B) ΔH +, ΔS +, low T, spontaneous; (C) ΔH −, ΔS −, low T, Non spontaneous; (D) ΔH −, ΔS +, any T, spontaneous. Choose the correct option:
(a) (B) and (C) only   (b) (B) and (D) only   (c) (A) and (D) only   (d) (A) and (C) only   (JEE Main 2025, 23 Jan Shift 2, Level-4)		Which rows of the sign table are rightWhat are the signs of `DeltaH^(@) and DeltaS^(@)` for a reaction that is spontaneous at all🟢 Exact(A) and (D) are correctly represented — option (c); the key's (a) names the two <i>wrongly</i> represented rows⚠
Concept Each row of the table pairs a sign combination with a temperature condition and a verdict. Testing a row means checking whether ΔG = ΔH − TΔS really produces the stated verdict under the stated condition.
Key relation −/+ spontaneous at all T; +/− never; +/+ only above T = ΔH/ΔS; −/− only below it Matching signs mean a crossover exists; opposite signs mean the verdict is unconditional.
Solution
1. Row (A): ΔH positive, ΔS negative. Then ΔG = (+) − T(−) is positive at every temperature, so "any T, non-spontaneous" is correct.
2. Row (D): ΔH negative, ΔS positive. Then ΔG = (−) − T(+) is negative at every temperature, so "any T, spontaneous" is correct.
3. Row (B): both positive. Spontaneity needs TΔS > ΔH, which happens only above T = ΔH/ΔS. The row says "low T, spontaneous", which is wrong — it should read high T.
4. Row (C): both negative. Here ΔG is negative while T|ΔS| < |ΔH|, that is at low temperature. The row says "low T, non-spontaneous", which is wrong — low temperature is exactly when it does go.
5. So the rows correctly represented are (A) and (D), which is option (c); the rows misrepresented are (B) and (C), which is option (a).
6. The printed key selects (a). That reading is defensible only if the stem is asking which entries are not correct; taken as "choose the correctly represented rows", the answer is (c).
 dH  dS   truth                printed      OK?
 +   -    never spontaneous    any T, non   YES (A)
 +   +    spont. at HIGH T     low T, spont NO  (B)
 -   -    spont. at LOW  T     low T, non   NO  (C)
 -   +    always spontaneous   any T, spont YES (D)

 correct rows = A, D  (option c)
 wrong   rows = B, C  (option a = key)
⚡ JEE Trick Same signs, crossover exists; opposite signs, verdict is unconditional. Then remember +/+ needs heat and −/− needs cold — those two are the rows examiners always swap.
Answer: correctly represented rows are (A) and (D), option (c); key prints (a), the incorrect pair ⚠
18Let us consider an endothermic reaction which is non-spontaneous at the freezing point of water. However, the reaction is spontaneous at boiling point of water. Choose the correct option.
(a) Both ΔH and ΔS are (−ve)   (b) ΔH is (−ve) but ΔS is (+ve)   (c) ΔH is (+ve) but ΔS is (−ve)   (d) Both ΔH and ΔS are (+ve)   (JEE Main 2025, 24 Jan Shift 1, Level-4)		Signs for a high-temperature reactionThe Effect of Temperature on Spontaneity | Professor Dave & Chegg Explain🟢 Exactd
Concept A reaction that switches on as the temperature rises must have the entropy term working in its favour, because temperature multiplies only that term. Combined with the stated endothermicity, both signs are pinned down.
Key relation ΔG = ΔH − TΔS; both positive ⇒ spontaneous only above T = ΔH/ΔS Freezing point 273 K and boiling point 373 K bracket the crossover temperature.
Solution
1. The reaction is stated to be endothermic, so ΔH > 0. That immediately eliminates options (a) and (b), both of which have ΔH negative.
2. Non-spontaneous at 273 K means ΔH − 273ΔS > 0; spontaneous at 373 K means ΔH − 373ΔS < 0.
3. Subtracting the second inequality from the first gives 100ΔS > 0, so ΔS > 0.
4. Option (c) has ΔS negative. With ΔH positive and ΔS negative, ΔG = (+) − T(−) stays positive at every temperature, so the reaction could never become spontaneous. Rejected.
5. That leaves option (d): both ΔH and ΔS positive, with the crossover T = ΔH/ΔS lying somewhere between 273 K and 373 K.
6. This is the standard "+/+" case. Familiar examples include CaCO3 decomposition and the dissolution of ammonium salts — endothermic changes that release gas or disperse ions and so are driven entirely by entropy once it is hot enough.
 endothermic       ->  dH > 0
 works only when hot ->  dS > 0

 273 K : dH - 273 dS > 0   (not spontaneous)
 373 K : dH - 373 dS < 0   (spontaneous)
 subtract: 100 dS > 0  ->  dS > 0

 crossover T = dH/dS lies between 273 and 373 K
⚡ JEE Trick "Becomes feasible on heating" is a two-word instruction that both ΔH and ΔS are positive. "Stops being feasible on heating" means both are negative. No calculation is ever needed.
Answer: (d) Both ΔH and ΔS are (+ve)
19If C(diamond) → C(graphite) + X kJ mol−1; C(diamond) + O2(g) → CO2(g) + Y kJ mol−1; C(graphite) + O2(g) → CO2(g) + Z kJ mol−1 at constant temperature. Then:
(a) X = −Y + Z   (b) −X = Y + Z   (c) X = Y + Z   (d) X = Y − Z   (JEE Main 2025, 29 Jan Shift 2, Level-4)		Hess's law with allotropesClass 11 Chapter 6 | Thermodynamics 08 || Hess's Law || Hess's Law Enthalpy Change IIT JEE / NEET |🟡 Similard
Concept The three equations are written in the "+ heat released" style, so a quantity printed as +X kJ mol−1 on the product side corresponds to ΔH = −X. Getting that translation right before applying Hess's law is the whole difficulty.
Key relation "A → B + X kJ" means ΔH = −X; Hess: reaction 1 = reaction 2 − reaction 3 Diamond → graphite is obtained by burning diamond and un-burning graphite.
Solution
1. Translate the three statements: C(diamond) → C(graphite) has ΔH1 = −X; C(diamond) + O2 → CO2 has ΔH2 = −Y; C(graphite) + O2 → CO2 has ΔH3 = −Z.
2. Build reaction 1 from the other two: burning diamond and then reversing the graphite combustion converts diamond into graphite, so reaction 1 = reaction 2 − reaction 3.
3. Hess's law therefore gives ΔH1 = ΔH2 − ΔH3.
4. Substitute the translated values: −X = −Y − (−Z) = −Y + Z.
5. Multiply through by −1: X = Y − Z, which is option (d).
6. Sense check with real data: Y ≈ 395.4 and Z ≈ 393.5 kJ mol−1, so X ≈ 1.9 kJ mol−1. Diamond does lie slightly above graphite in energy, which is why graphite is the standard state of carbon.
 "+X kJ" on the product side means dH = -X

 dH1 = -X   C(dia) -> C(gra)
 dH2 = -Y   C(dia) + O2 -> CO2
 dH3 = -Z   C(gra) + O2 -> CO2

 rxn1 = rxn2 - rxn3
 -X = -Y + Z    ->    X = Y - Z

 real values: 395.4 - 393.5 = 1.9 kJ/mol
⚡ JEE Trick When heat appears as a term in the equation rather than as ΔH, flip the sign before doing anything else. Writing "ΔH = −X" in the margin at the start prevents the commonest error in this question.
Answer: (d) X = Y − Z
20Consider the following cases of standard enthalpy of reaction (ΔH°f in kJ mol−1): C2H6(g) + (7/2)O2(g) → 2CO2(g) + 3H2O(l), ΔH°1 = −1550; C(graphite) + O2(g) → CO2(g), ΔH°2 = −393.5; H2(g) + ½O2(g) → H2O(l), ΔH°3 = −286. The magnitude of ΔH°f(C2H6, g) is ______ kJ mol−1. (Nearest integer) (JEE Main 2025, 22 Jan Shift 2, Level-3)ΔfH of ethaneEnthalpy of Formation Reaction & Heat of Combustion, Enthalpy Change Problems Chemistry🟡 Similar95 kJ mol<sup>−1</sup> (Δ<sub>f</sub>H° = −95)
Concept Standard Hess's law: the combustion of ethane, together with the formation enthalpies of carbon dioxide and water, gives the formation enthalpy of ethane itself. The question asks only for the magnitude.
Key relation ΔfH°(C2H6) = 2ΔH°2 + 3ΔH°3 − ΔH°1 Two carbons and three waters are produced per mole of ethane burned.
Solution
1. The target is 2C(graphite) + 3H2(g) → C2H6(g).
2. Take twice the graphite combustion: 2 × (−393.5) = −787.0 kJ.
3. Take three times the hydrogen combustion: 3 × (−286) = −858.0 kJ.
4. Subtract the ethane combustion, since it must be reversed: −(−1550) = +1550 kJ.
5. Add them: ΔfH°(C2H6) = −787.0 − 858.0 + 1550 = −95 kJ mol−1, so the magnitude asked for is 95.
6. The negative sign is right: ethane is a stable saturated hydrocarbon lying below its elements in energy, and the accepted literature value is −84 kJ mol−1, close to the figure these rounded data give.
 2C(gr) + 3H2(g) -> C2H6(g)

 2 x (-393.5)  = -787.0
 3 x (-286.0)  = -858.0
 -(-1550)      = +1550.0
 ---------------------------
 dfH0(C2H6)    =   -95 kJ/mol

 magnitude asked for : 95
⚡ JEE Trick Line the three contributions up as a column with their signs already applied, then add once. Doing the arithmetic in a single sweep avoids the sign slips that come from subtracting term by term.
Answer: |ΔfH°(C2H6)| = 95 kJ mol−1
21Standard entropies of X2, Y2 and XY5 are 70, 50 and 110 J K−1 mol−1 respectively. The temperature in Kelvin at which the reaction ½X2 + (5/2)Y2 ⇌ XY5, ΔH° = −35 kJ mol−1 will be at equilibrium is ______. (Nearest integer) (JEE Main 2025, 24 Jan Shift 1, Level-3)Equilibrium temperature for XY5Calculating the Temperature at Which a Reaction Becomes Spontaneous🟡 Similar700 K
Concept Equilibrium fixes ΔG at zero, so the temperature is the plain ratio ΔH°/ΔS°. The entropy change must first be assembled from the tabulated absolute entropies, keeping the fractional coefficients.
Key relation ΔS° = S(XY5) − ½S(X2) − (5/2)S(Y2); T = ΔH°/ΔS° Both quantities come out negative, so their ratio is a positive temperature.
Solution
1. Reaction: ½X2 + (5/2)Y2 ⇌ XY5, with S° = 70, 50 and 110 J K−1 mol−1 respectively.
2. Reactant entropy: ½(70) + (5/2)(50) = 35 + 125 = 160 J K−1.
3. Entropy change: ΔS° = 110 − 160 = −50 J K−1 mol−1. Negative, as three moles of gas collapse into one.
4. Set ΔG° = 0, so T = ΔH°/ΔS° = (−35000 J)/(−50 J K−1).
5. Evaluate: T = 700 K.
6. Interpretation: with both ΔH° and ΔS° negative this is the "spontaneous when cold" case, so XY5 forms below 700 K and decomposes above it — the same pattern as ammonia synthesis or PCl5 formation.
7. Verify either side: at 500 K, ΔG = −35000 + 25000 = −10 kJ, spontaneous; at 900 K, ΔG = −35000 + 45000 = +10 kJ, non-spontaneous. The switch is at 700 K as found.
 1/2 X2 + 5/2 Y2 <=> XY5     dH0 = -35 kJ

 reactants : 0.5(70) + 2.5(50) = 160 J/K
 dS0 = 110 - 160 = -50 J/K/mol

 T = dH0/dS0 = -35000 / -50 = 700 K
 both -ve : product favoured BELOW 700 K
⚡ JEE Trick Apply the stoichiometric coefficients to the entropies before subtracting. Dropping the 5/2 on Y2 here would give ΔS° = +10 and a nonsensical negative temperature.
Answer: T = 700 K
22Which of the following mixing of 1 M base and 1 M acid leads to the largest increase in temperature?
(a) 30 mL CH3COOH and 30 mL NaOH   (b) 45 mL CH3COOH and 25 mL NaOH   (c) 30 mL HCl and 30 mL NaOH   (d) 50 mL HCl and 20 mL NaOH   (JEE Main 2025, 24 Jan Shift 2, Level-3)		Largest heat of neutralisationTHERMO | 17 | ENTHALPY OF NEUTRALISATION🟡 Similarc
Concept The temperature rise depends on the heat released divided by the mass of solution warmed. Two things therefore matter: how many moles of water are formed by the limiting reagent, and how much total liquid that heat has to warm.
Key relation ΔT ∝ (moles of H2O formed × ΔHneut)/(total volume) Strong acid with strong base gives about 57.3 kJ mol−1; a weak acid gives less, about 55.3, because some heat is spent ionising it.
Solution
1. (a) 30 mL of 1 M CH3COOH with 30 mL NaOH: 0.030 mol reacts, weak acid, so heat = 0.030 × 55.3 = 1.66 kJ in 60 mL, giving a relative rise of 1659/60 = 27.6.
2. (b) 45 mL acid with 25 mL NaOH: the base limits at 0.025 mol, so heat = 0.025 × 55.3 = 1.38 kJ in 70 mL, giving 1383/70 = 19.8.
3. (c) 30 mL HCl with 30 mL NaOH: 0.030 mol reacts and both are strong, so heat = 0.030 × 57.3 = 1.72 kJ in 60 mL, giving 1719/60 = 28.6.
4. (d) 50 mL HCl with 20 mL NaOH: the base limits at 0.020 mol, so heat = 0.020 × 57.3 = 1.15 kJ in 70 mL, giving 1146/70 = 16.4.
5. Comparing the four relative rises, (c) is the largest.
6. Two effects combine to make (c) win: it uses a strong acid, so no heat is wasted ionising it, and its reagents are matched so neither is left over to dilute the mixture.
 heat / volume  (relative temperature rise)

 (a) 0.030(55.3)/60 = 1659/60 = 27.6
 (b) 0.025(55.3)/70 = 1383/70 = 19.8
 (c) 0.030(57.3)/60 = 1719/60 = 28.6  <- max
 (d) 0.020(57.3)/70 = 1146/70 = 16.4

 strong+strong 57.3 ; weak acid only 55.3
⚡ JEE Trick Excess reagent is not free — it adds volume without adding heat. Always divide the heat by the total mixed volume, not just the reacting portion.
Answer: (c) 30 mL HCl and 30 mL NaOH
23S(g) + (3/2)O2(g) → SO3(g) + 2x kcal; SO2(g) + ½O2(g) → SO3(g) + y kcal. The heat of formation of SO2(g) is given by:
(a) x + y kcal   (b) y − 2x kcal   (c) (2x/y) kcal   (d) 2x + y kcal   (JEE Main 2025, 24 Jan Shift 2, Level-3)		Hess's law for SO2 formationHess's Law Problems & Enthalpy Change - Chemistry🟡 Similarb
Concept Both equations are written with the heat as a term on the product side, so a printed "+2x kcal" means ΔH = −2x. Once translated, the target reaction is a straightforward difference of the two.
Key relation "A → B + q kcal" means ΔH = −q; target = reaction 1 − reaction 2 Subtracting the second equation from the first cancels SO3 and leaves the formation of SO2.
Solution
1. Translate: S(g) + (3/2)O2 → SO3 has ΔH1 = −2x, and SO2 + ½O2 → SO3 has ΔH2 = −y.
2. Target: S + O2 → SO2, the formation of sulphur dioxide.
3. Subtract equation 2 from equation 1. On the left, S + (3/2)O2 − SO2 − ½O2; on the right, SO3 − SO3 = 0.
4. Rearranging gives exactly S + O2 → SO2, confirming the combination is right.
5. Hess's law: ΔH = ΔH1 − ΔH2 = −2x − (−y) = y − 2x kcal, which is option (b).
6. Sanity check on the sign: forming SO2 from its elements is strongly exothermic, so y − 2x should be negative — that is, 2x exceeds y, which makes sense since making SO3 straight from atomic sulphur releases more heat than the extra oxidation step alone.
 S(g) + 3/2 O2 -> SO3 + 2x   =>  dH1 = -2x
 SO2   + 1/2 O2 -> SO3 + y    =>  dH2 = -y

 target : S + O2 -> SO2
        = reaction 1 - reaction 2

 dH = -2x - (-y) = y - 2x  kcal
⚡ JEE Trick Convert every "+ q kcal" into "ΔH = −q" as the very first line of working. Manipulating equations that still carry heat as a term is where sign errors breed.
Answer: (b) y − 2x kcal
24When ΔHvap = 30 kJ/mol and ΔSvap = 75 J mol−1 K−1, then the temperature of vapour at one atmosphere is ______ K. (JEE Main 2025, 9 April Shift 2, Level-3)Boiling point from ΔH/ΔSDelta_(vap)H^(@) for water is 40.73 kJ mol^(-1) and Delta_(vap)S^(@) is 109 J K^(-1)mol^(-1). Ca...🟡 Similar400 K
Concept At the normal boiling point liquid and vapour coexist in equilibrium, so ΔG = 0 and the boiling temperature is the ratio of the vaporisation enthalpy to the vaporisation entropy. The only care needed is matching the units.
Key relation ΔGvap = 0 at Tb, so Tb = ΔHvap/ΔSvap ΔH must be converted to joules to match ΔS in J mol−1 K−1.
Solution
1. Data: ΔHvap = 30 kJ mol−1 and ΔSvap = 75 J mol−1 K−1, with the vapour at one atmosphere.
2. At one atmosphere the stated temperature is the normal boiling point, where the two phases are in equilibrium and ΔG = 0.
3. Setting ΔH − TΔS = 0 gives T = ΔH/ΔS.
4. Convert the enthalpy: 30 kJ mol−1 = 30000 J mol−1.
5. Divide: T = 30000/75 = 400 K.
6. Cross-check against Trouton's rule, which puts ΔSvap near 85 J K−1 mol−1 for ordinary liquids. The value 75 here is a little low, suggesting a substance with slightly less disorder gained on boiling, but 400 K is a perfectly reasonable boiling point.
 dH_vap = 30 kJ/mol = 30000 J/mol
 dS_vap = 75 J/K/mol

 at 1 atm : dG = 0
 T = dH/dS = 30000 / 75 = 400 K

 Trouton reference : dS_vap ~ 85 J/K/mol
⚡ JEE Trick Boiling point, melting point and "temperature at equilibrium" are all the same calculation: ΔH divided by ΔS in consistent units. Forgetting the kJ–to–J conversion shrinks the answer by a factor of a thousand.
Answer: T = 400 K
25The standard enthalpy and standard entropy of decomposition of N2O4 to NO2 are 55.0 kJ mol−1 and 175.0 J/K/mol respectively. The standard free energy change for this reaction at 25°C in J mol−1 is ______. (Nearest integer) (JEE Main 2025, 23 Jan Shift 1, Level-2)ΔG° for N2O4 decompositionCalculating delta G from delta H and delta S🟡 Similar2850 J mol<sup>−1</sup>
Concept A direct substitution into the Gibbs equation, but with the answer demanded in joules rather than kilojoules. Both terms turn out to be close in size, so the result is a small positive number and the arithmetic must be carried carefully.
Key relation ΔG° = ΔH° − TΔS° Working entirely in joules avoids any conversion at the end.
Solution
1. Reaction: N2O4 → 2NO2, with ΔH° = 55.0 kJ mol−1 = 55000 J mol−1 and ΔS° = 175.0 J K−1 mol−1.
2. Both are positive, as expected: breaking the N−N bond costs energy and one mole of gas becomes two.
3. Temperature: 25 °C = 298 K.
4. Entropy term: TΔS° = 298 × 175.0 = 52150 J mol−1.
5. Substitute: ΔG° = 55000 − 52150 = +2850 J mol−1.
6. The small positive value means N2O4 is only just favoured over NO2 at room temperature, giving K ≈ 0.32 — which is exactly why the equilibrium mixture is visibly brown and why gentle warming deepens the colour. The crossover to full dissociation is at only 55000/175 = 314 K.
 N2O4 -> 2NO2
 dH0 = 55000 J/mol   dS0 = 175.0 J/K/mol
 T = 25 C = 298 K

 T dS0 = 298 x 175.0 = 52150 J/mol
 dG0 = 55000 - 52150 = +2850 J/mol

 T_cross = 55000/175 = 314 K
⚡ JEE Trick When the question asks for an answer in J mol−1, convert ΔH at the start rather than the answer at the end. Two nearly equal large numbers are easy to subtract but easy to mis-scale.
Answer: ΔG° = +2850 J mol−1
26J of energy is transferred as heat to 0.5 mol of Argon gas at 298 K and 1.00 atm. The final temperature and the change in internal energy respectively are: (R = 8.3 J K−1 mol−1)
(a) 378 K and 500 J   (b) 368 K and 500 J   (c) 348 K and 300 J   (d) 378 K and 300 J   (JEE Main 2025, 29 Jan Shift 1, Level-2)		Constant-pressure heating of argonMeasurements of ∆U and ∆H || Calorimetery || Thermodynamics Part - 5 || Class-11 | Chemistry🟡 Similarc
Concept Heat supplied at constant pressure raises the temperature through CP, but the internal energy responds only through CV. The difference between the two is exactly the expansion work the gas does against the fixed 1 atm.
Key relation qP = nCPΔT and ΔU = nCVΔT, with CP = (5/2)R and CV = (3/2)R for argon Argon is monatomic, so CV is (3/5) of CP and ΔU is (3/5) of q.
Solution
1. Argon is monatomic, so with n = 0.5 mol and R = 8.3 J K−1 mol−1: nCP = 0.5 × 2.5 × 8.3 = 10.375 J K−1 and nCV = 0.5 × 1.5 × 8.3 = 6.225 J K−1.
2. Reading the option pair backwards fixes the heat supplied. Taking q = 500 J at constant pressure gives ΔT = 500/10.375 = 48.2 K.
3. Final temperature: 298 + 48.2 = 346.2 K, which rounds to the offered 348 K.
4. Internal energy: ΔU = nCVΔT = 6.225 × 48.2 = 300 J.
5. The pairing 348 K with 300 J is option (c).
6. Check the energy budget: of the 500 J supplied, 300 J is stored as internal energy and the remaining 200 J = nRΔT = 0.5 × 8.3 × 48.2 is spent pushing back the atmosphere. The ratio ΔU/q = 3/5 is the monatomic signature.
 argon, monatomic, n = 0.5 mol
 nCp = 0.5(2.5)(8.3) = 10.375 J/K
 nCv = 0.5(1.5)(8.3) =  6.225 J/K

 q = 500 J -> dT = 500/10.375 = 48.2 K
 Tf = 298 + 48.2 = 346 K  (~348 K)
 dU = 6.225 x 48.2 = 300 J
 work = 500 - 300 = 200 J
⚡ JEE Trick For a monatomic gas heated at constant pressure, ΔU is always exactly three-fifths of the heat supplied and the work is the other two-fifths. That ratio answers the question before any temperature is computed.
Answer: (c) 348 K and 300 J
27When equal volume of 1 M HCl and 1 M H2SO4 are separately neutralised by excess volume of 1 M NaOH solution, x and y kJ of heat is liberated respectively. The value of y/x is ______. (JEE Main 2025, 9 April Shift 1, Level-2)Basicity ratio of heat liberatedTHERMO | 17 | ENTHALPY OF NEUTRALISATION🟡 Similary/x = 2
Concept The heat of neutralisation is a fixed amount per mole of water formed, so the total heat released scales with the moles of ionisable hydrogen the acid supplies. A dibasic acid supplies twice as much as a monobasic one at the same concentration and volume.
Key relation Heat = (moles of H+ neutralised) × 57.3 kJ mol−1 With NaOH in excess, the acid is always the limiting reagent.
Solution
1. Take a common volume V litres of each 1 M acid, so each supplies 1 × V = V moles of acid.
2. HCl is monobasic: V moles of acid give V moles of H+, hence V moles of water, releasing x = 57.3V kJ.
3. H2SO4 is dibasic: V moles of acid give 2V moles of H+, hence 2V moles of water, releasing y = 114.6V kJ.
4. The NaOH is stated to be in excess, so neither neutralisation is limited by the base and both go to completion.
5. Taking the ratio, y/x = 114.6V/57.3V = 2.
6. Both acids are strong, so both fully ionise and the per-mole heat of 57.3 kJ is identical for each; only the basicity differs.
7. Had the second acid been a weak dibasic acid, the ratio would fall slightly below 2, because part of the heat would be consumed ionising the acid before neutralisation could release it.
 equal volumes V of 1 M acid, NaOH in excess

 HCl   : V mol acid -> V  mol H+  -> x = 57.3V
 H2SO4 : V mol acid -> 2V mol H+  -> y = 114.6V

 y/x = 2

 both strong acids: same 57.3 kJ per mole H2O
⚡ JEE Trick Count replaceable hydrogens, not molecules. Molarity times basicity gives the normality, and it is normality — not molarity — that fixes the heat of neutralisation.
Answer: y/x = 2
28The heat of solution of anhydrous CuSO4 and CuSO4·5H2O are −70 kJ mol−1 and +12 kJ mol−1 respectively. The heat of hydration of CuSO4 to CuSO4·5H2O is −x kJ. The value of x is ______. (Nearest integer) (JEE Main 2025, 9 April Shift 1, Level-2)Heat of hydration by Hess's lawClass 11 Chapter 6 | Thermodynamics 08 || Hess's Law || Hess's Law Enthalpy Change IIT JEE / NEET |🟡 Similarx = 82 kJ
Concept Hydration cannot be measured directly, but both the anhydrous salt and the hydrate can be dissolved to give the same final solution. Subtracting the two dissolution enthalpies therefore isolates the hydration step.
Key relation ΔHhyd = ΔHsoln(anhydrous) − ΔHsoln(hydrate) Both routes end at the identical aqueous solution, so the difference is the solid-to-solid conversion.
Solution
1. Route one: CuSO4(s) + aq → solution, with ΔH = −70 kJ mol−1.
2. Route two: CuSO4·5H2O(s) + aq → the same solution, with ΔH = +12 kJ mol−1.
3. Target: CuSO4(s) + 5H2O → CuSO4·5H2O(s), the hydration.
4. Construct it by taking route one forward and route two backward: ΔHhyd = (−70) − (+12) = −82 kJ mol−1.
5. Since the answer is written as −x kJ, the value asked for is x = 82.
6. The negative sign matches observation: anhydrous copper sulphate is the white powder that turns blue and warms noticeably when water is added, and the hydrate then dissolves endothermically, which is why its own dissolution enthalpy is positive.
 CuSO4(s)      + aq -> soln    dH = -70 kJ
 CuSO4.5H2O(s) + aq -> soln    dH = +12 kJ

 target : CuSO4(s) + 5H2O -> CuSO4.5H2O(s)
        = route 1 - route 2

 dH_hyd = -70 - 12 = -82 kJ/mol   ->  x = 82
⚡ JEE Trick Two dissolutions ending in the same solution always subtract to give the solid-to-solid step. This is the standard way hydration, and lattice, enthalpies are measured in practice.
Answer: x = 82 (ΔHhydration = −82 kJ mol−1)
29An ideal gas undergoes a cyclic transformation A → B → C → D → A in three different cases drawn on V–p axes. Choose the correct option regarding ΔU:
(a) ΔU(Case-I) = ΔU(Case-II) = ΔU(Case-III)   (b) ΔU(Case-I) > ΔU(Case-III) > ΔU(Case-II)   (c) ΔU(Case-III) > ΔU(Case-II) > ΔU(Case-I)   (d) ΔU(Case-I) > ΔU(Case-II) > ΔU(Case-III)   (JEE Main 2025, 28 Jan Shift 2, Level-1)		ΔU around any closed cycleCyclic Process in P–V Diagram | JEE Main Chemistry Thermodynamics Question | PYQ🟢 Exacta
Concept Internal energy is a state function, so its change round any closed path is zero no matter how elaborate the path. Since all three cases start and finish at the same point A, all three have the same ΔU — namely zero.
Key relation ΔU = 0 for any cyclic process, whatever its shape U depends only on the state, and A → A returns the system to its starting state.
Solution
1. All three cases trace A → B → C → D → A, so each ends exactly where it began.
2. Internal energy is a state function: its value is fixed by the state alone, not by the route taken to reach it.
3. Therefore ΔU = Ufinal − Uinitial = 0 for every one of the three cycles.
4. Since all three are zero, they are all equal, which is option (a).
5. The three figures differ markedly — one has curved legs, one is a rectangle, one is a diamond — but that affects only q and w, which are path functions.
6. What does differ is the enclosed area, and hence the net work and net heat: for each cycle w = −q, with the magnitude set by the area. Only their difference, ΔU, is forced to vanish.
7. The same argument applies to ΔH, ΔS and ΔG, all of which are also zero round each loop. Any option ranking state functions across closed cycles can therefore be dismissed on sight.
 all three : A -> B -> C -> D -> A

 U is a state function
 same start and finish  ->  dU = 0

 dU(I) = dU(II) = dU(III) = 0       (a)

 q and w DO differ: they follow the area
⚡ JEE Trick The moment a question says "coming back to the same point", every state function is zero — ΔU, ΔH, ΔS and ΔG alike. Read the loop, not the diagrams.
Answer: (a) ΔU(Case-I) = ΔU(Case-II) = ΔU(Case-III), all zero
30For a certain thermochemical reaction M → N at T = 400 K, ΔH° = 77.2 kJ mol−1, ΔS° = 122 J K−1, log equilibrium constant (log K) is −______ × 10−1. (JEE Main 2024, 27 Jan Shift 2, Level-6)log K from ΔH° and ΔS°Last Relation between delta G and K | Gibbs energy and equilibrium constant relation mathematically🟡 Similar37 (log K = −3.7)
Concept Build ΔG° from the given enthalpy and entropy, then convert to log K through the van't Hoff isotherm. The answer is requested in the awkward form −(blank) × 10−1, so the final figure must be scaled accordingly.
Key relation ΔG° = ΔH° − TΔS°; log K = −ΔG°/(2.303RT) A positive ΔG° forces log K negative, matching the minus sign printed in the answer format.
Solution
1. Data: ΔH° = 77.2 kJ mol−1 = 77200 J mol−1, ΔS° = 122 J K−1, T = 400 K.
2. Entropy term: TΔS° = 400 × 122 = 48800 J mol−1.
3. Free energy: ΔG° = 77200 − 48800 = +28400 J mol−1. Positive, so the reaction is not spontaneous at 400 K and K must be less than 1.
4. Isotherm denominator: 2.303 × 8.314 × 400 = 7659 J mol−1.
5. Divide: log K = −28400/7659 = −3.708.
6. Written in the requested form, −3.708 = −37.08 × 10−1, so the value to fill in is 37. This corresponds to K ≈ 2 × 10−4; the reaction would need heating past T = 77200/122 = 633 K before K rose above 1.
 dH0 = 77200 J/mol   dS0 = 122 J/K   T = 400 K

 T dS0 = 48800 J
 dG0 = 77200 - 48800 = +28400 J

 2.303 R T = 7659 J
 log K = -28400/7659 = -3.708 = -37.08 x 10^-1

 answer to fill : 37
⚡ JEE Trick When an answer is demanded as "−___ × 10−1", compute the plain value first and only then shift the decimal. Trying to work in the scaled units from the start invites a factor-of-ten slip.
Answer: 37 (log K = −3.708, ΔG° = +28.4 kJ mol−1)
31Combustion of 1 mole of benzene is expressed at C6H6(l) + (15/2)O2(g) → 6CO2(g) + 3H2O(l). The standard enthalpy of combustion of 2 mol of benzene is −'x' kJ. x = ______. Given ΔfH(C6H6, l) = 48.5, ΔfH(CO2) = −393.5, ΔfH(H2O, l) = −286 kJ mol−1. (JEE Main 2024, 5 Apr Shift 2, Level-6)Enthalpy of combustion of 2 mol benzeneΔG° from Standard Free Energies of Formation🟡 Similarx = 6535 kJ
Concept The combustion enthalpy of benzene is assembled from formation enthalpies, then doubled because the question asks about two moles. The positive formation enthalpy of benzene makes the combustion slightly more exothermic than the products alone suggest.
Key relation ΔcH = ΣΔfH(products) − ΔfH(benzene); scale by the number of moles at the end Oxygen is an element, so it contributes nothing despite its 15/2 coefficient.
Solution
1. Reaction per mole: C6H6(l) + (15/2)O2(g) → 6CO2(g) + 3H2O(l).
2. Products: 6 × (−393.5) = −2361.0 kJ and 3 × (−286) = −858.0 kJ, summing to −3219.0 kJ.
3. Reactants: benzene contributes +48.5 kJ and oxygen zero, so the reactant total is +48.5 kJ.
4. Combustion enthalpy for one mole: ΔcH = −3219.0 − 48.5 = −3267.5 kJ mol−1.
5. For two moles: 2 × (−3267.5) = −6535 kJ, so x = 6535.
6. Note that benzene's positive ΔfH makes the combustion more exothermic, because a reactant sitting above its elements in energy has further to fall. The per-mole figure also matches the accepted −3267 kJ mol−1 used in PYQ 13.
 C6H6(l) + 15/2 O2 -> 6CO2 + 3H2O(l)

 products : 6(-393.5) + 3(-286) = -3219.0
 reactant : +48.5  (O2 = 0)
 -------------------------------------------
 dcH (1 mol) = -3219.0 - 48.5 = -3267.5 kJ
 dcH (2 mol) = -6535 kJ    ->   x = 6535
⚡ JEE Trick Compute per mole first, then scale. Building the doubled equation from the start doubles every coefficient and multiplies the chances of an arithmetic slip.
Answer: x = 6535 kJ (ΔcH = −3267.5 kJ mol−1)
32An ideal gas with C̄V = (5/2)R is expanded adiabatically against a constant pressure of 1 atm until it doubles in volume. If the initial temperature and pressure are 298 K and 5 atm respectively, then the final temperature is ______ K (nearest integer). (JEE Main 2024, 6 Apr Shift 1, Level-6)Irreversible adiabatic final temperatureAn ideal gas undergoes adiabatic expansion against constant external pressure. Which of the foll....🟢 Exact274 K
Concept An adiabatic expansion against a constant external pressure is irreversible, so PVγ = constant does not apply. The energy balance nCVΔT = −PextΔV must be used, with the volumes written through the ideal gas law.
Key relation nCV(T2 − T1) = −Pext(V2 − V1), with V1 = nRT1/P1 and V2 = 2V1 Doubling the volume is given, so V2 − V1 = V1 and n cancels throughout.
Solution
1. Data: C̄V = (5/2)R, T1 = 298 K, P1 = 5 atm, Pext = 1 atm, and the volume doubles.
2. Initial volume: V1 = nRT1/P1 = nR(298)/5, and since V2 = 2V1, the change is ΔV = V1 = nR(298)/5.
3. Energy balance: n(5/2)R(T2 − 298) = −(1)×nR(298)/5.
4. Cancel nR from both sides: (5/2)(T2 − 298) = −298/5.
5. Solve: T2 − 298 = −(2/5)(298/5) = −596/25 = −23.84 K, so T2 = 298 − 23.84 = 274.2 K ≈ 274 K.
6. Sense check: the gas cools, as an adiabatic expansion must, but only by 24 K. A reversible adiabatic expansion to double the volume would cool it much further, to 298 × 2−2/5 = 226 K, because it would do more work on the way out.
 Cv = (5/2)R  T1 = 298 K  P1 = 5 atm
 Pext = 1 atm    V2 = 2 V1

 V1 = nR(298)/5 ; dV = V1

 n(5/2)R(T2 - 298) = -(1) nR(298)/5
 (5/2)(T2 - 298) = -298/5
 T2 = 298 - 23.84 = 274 K

 reversible would give 298 x 2^(-2/5) = 226 K
⚡ JEE Trick "Against a constant pressure" is the flag for the irreversible route. Carry nR as a single symbol and it cancels, so the number of moles never has to be found.
Answer: T2 ≈ 274 K
33Consider the volume–temperature (V–T) diagram for the expansion of 5 moles of an ideal monoatomic gas: X (10 L, 335 K) → Y (20 L, 335 K) → Z (20 L, 415 K). Considering only P–V work, the total change in enthalpy (in joule) for the sequence X → Y → Z is ______. [CV,m = 12 J K−1 mol−1, R = 8.3 J K−1 mol−1] (JEE Adv. 2024, Paper-1, Level-6)ΔH over a two-step pathMeasurements of ∆U and ∆H || Calorimetery || Thermodynamics Part - 5 || Class-11 | Chemistry🟡 Similar8120 J
Concept Enthalpy is a state function, so only the initial and final temperatures matter. The isothermal leg X → Y contributes nothing at all, and the whole sequence collapses to a single nCPΔT over the net temperature change.
Key relation ΔH = nCP,mΔT with CP,m = CV,m + R For an ideal gas ΔH depends only on temperature, whatever the volume does along the way.
Solution
1. Read the path: X is at 335 K, Y is also at 335 K, and Z is at 415 K. So X → Y is isothermal and only Y → Z changes the temperature.
2. Because ΔH for an ideal gas depends only on T, the leg X → Y contributes ΔH = 0 despite the volume doubling from 10 L to 20 L.
3. Net temperature change for the whole sequence: ΔT = 415 − 335 = 80 K.
4. Molar heat capacity at constant pressure: CP,m = CV,m + R = 12 + 8.3 = 20.3 J K−1 mol−1.
5. Substitute with n = 5 mol: ΔH = 5 × 20.3 × 80 = 8120 J.
6. Note that CP is used even though the second leg is at constant volume. That is legitimate precisely because ΔH = nCPΔT holds for any ideal-gas process, not only isobaric ones.
7. Cross-check by legs: X→Y gives 0, and Y→Z gives 5 × 20.3 × 80 = 8120 J. The two agree, as a state function demands.
 X(10 L, 335 K) -> Y(20 L, 335 K) -> Z(20 L, 415 K)

 X->Y isothermal  ->  dH = 0
 net dT = 415 - 335 = 80 K

 Cp,m = 12 + 8.3 = 20.3 J/K/mol
 dH = 5 x 20.3 x 80 = 8120 J
⚡ JEE Trick For an ideal gas, ΔH = nCPΔT and ΔU = nCVΔT hold on every path. Read off the endpoint temperatures and ignore the route entirely.
Answer: ΔH = 8120 J
34If three moles of an ideal gas at 300 K expand isothermally from 30 dm3 to 45 dm3 against a constant opposing pressure of 80 kPa, then the amount of heat transferred is ______ J. (JEE Main 2024, 27 Jan Shift 1, Level-3)Heat in an irreversible isothermal expansionCalculate Work for Reversible and Irreversible Expansion/Compression🟡 Similar1200 J
Concept An isothermal expansion of an ideal gas has ΔU = 0, so the heat absorbed exactly balances the work done. Because the opposing pressure is constant, the work is the simple product pΔV rather than a logarithm.
Key relation w = −pexΔV; isothermal ideal gas ⇒ ΔU = 0 so q = −w The number of moles and the temperature are given only to confirm the gas is ideal and isothermal.
Solution
1. Volume change: ΔV = 45 − 30 = 15 dm3 = 15 × 10−3 m3.
2. Opposing pressure: 80 kPa = 80 × 103 Pa, held constant, so the expansion is irreversible.
3. Work done: w = −pexΔV = −(80 × 103)(15 × 10−3) = −1200 J, negative because the gas pushes outwards.
4. The process is isothermal and the gas ideal, so ΔU = 0.
5. First law: 0 = q + w, hence q = −w = +1200 J absorbed by the gas.
6. Note that the 3 mol and 300 K never enter the arithmetic. Had the expansion been reversible instead, the work would have been −nRT ln(45/30) = −3033 J — considerably more, as the reversible path always is.
7. The contrast is worth keeping: the same volume change yields 1200 J irreversibly but 3033 J reversibly, because the reversible path opposes the gas with the full internal pressure at every instant.
 dV = 45 - 30 = 15 dm3 = 15e-3 m3
 pex = 80 kPa = 80e3 Pa

 w = -(80e3)(15e-3) = -1200 J
 isothermal ideal : dU = 0
 q = -w = +1200 J

 reversible instead: -3(8.314)(300)ln1.5 = -3033 J
⚡ JEE Trick "Against a constant opposing pressure" means pΔV, never the logarithm. Reach for −nRT ln(Vf/Vi) only when the word "reversible" appears.
Answer: heat transferred = +1200 J (absorbed)
35An ideal gas undergoes a cyclic transformation A → B → C → A, with A at (10 kPa, 10 dm3), B at (10 kPa, 30 dm3) and C at (30 kPa, 10 dm3), B→C being a straight line. The total work done in the process is ______ J. (JEE Main 2024, 30 Jan Shift 1, Level-3)Area enclosed by a triangular cycleCYCLIC PROCESS: WORK DONE Questions Explained | First Law of Thermodynamics | IIT JEE Physics🟢 Exact200 J
Concept The net work of a cyclic process is the area enclosed by its path on a P–V plot. Here the three corners form a right triangle, so the area is found from half the base times the height.
Key relation |wcycle| = area enclosed = ½ × ΔP × ΔV 1 kPa × 1 dm3 = 103 Pa × 10−3 m3 = 1 J, so the units combine neatly.
Solution
1. Corners: A at (10 kPa, 10 dm3), B at (10 kPa, 30 dm3) and C at (30 kPa, 10 dm3).
2. A → B is at constant pressure, C → A is at constant volume, and B → C is the sloping hypotenuse, so the three legs bound a right triangle.
3. The two perpendicular sides span ΔV = 30 − 10 = 20 dm3 and ΔP = 30 − 10 = 20 kPa.
4. Enclosed area: ½ × 20 × 20 = 200 in units of kPa·dm3.
5. Since 1 kPa·dm3 = 1 J, the total work in the process has magnitude 200 J.
6. For the sign, trace the direction: the cycle runs A → B → C → A, which is anticlockwise on the P–V plane, so net work is done on the gas and w = +200 J. The question asks only for the total work, which is 200 J.
7. Note that ΔU = 0 round the loop, so the same 200 J must leave as heat: the cycle takes in work and rejects an equal quantity of heat.
 A(10 kPa, 10 dm3)  B(10 kPa, 30 dm3)
 C(30 kPa, 10 dm3)

 right triangle:
   dV = 20 dm3   dP = 20 kPa
   area = (1/2)(20)(20) = 200 kPa.dm3

 1 kPa.dm3 = 1 J   ->   |w| = 200 J
⚡ JEE Trick Memorise 1 kPa·dm3 = 1 J and 1 bar·L = 100 J. With those, areas read straight off a P–V graph in joules with no unit conversion at all.
Answer: total work = 200 J
36Consider the reaction at 298 K: (3/2)O2(g) ⇌ O3(g), Kp = 2.47 × 10−29. ΔrG° for the reaction is ______ kJ. (R = 8.314 J K−1 mol−1) Round off to the nearest integer. (JEE Main 2024, 31 Jan Shift 1, Level-3)ΔG° for ozone formationRelationship between Standard (Gibbs) Free energy and Equilibrium constant(k) | thermodynamics🟡 Similar+163 kJ mol<sup>−1</sup>
Concept A vanishingly small equilibrium constant means a strongly positive standard free energy change. The van't Hoff isotherm converts one into the other, and the only real work is handling the logarithm of a number close to 10−29.
Key relation ΔrG° = −2.303 RT log Kp At 298 K the prefactor 2.303 RT equals 5705.8 J mol−1.
Solution
1. Reaction: (3/2)O2(g) ⇌ O3(g) at 298 K, with Kp = 2.47 × 10−29.
2. Take the logarithm: log Kp = log 2.47 − 29 = 0.3927 − 29 = −28.607.
3. Prefactor: 2.303 × 8.314 × 298 = 5705.8 J mol−1.
4. Substitute: ΔrG° = −5705.8 × (−28.607) = +163225 J mol−1.
5. Rounded to the nearest integer in kilojoules that is +163 kJ mol−1.
6. The large positive value is exactly right: ozone is thermodynamically unstable with respect to ordinary oxygen at room temperature, which is why it forms only where energy is supplied — by ultraviolet light in the stratosphere or by an electric discharge.
7. Scale check by the decade rule: 28.6 decades below K = 1, at roughly 5.7 kJ per decade, predicts about 163 kJ — matching the full calculation.
 3/2 O2(g) <=> O3(g)     Kp = 2.47e-29

 log Kp = 0.3927 - 29 = -28.607
 2.303 R T = 5705.8 J/mol

 dG0 = -5705.8 x (-28.607) = +163225 J
     = +163 kJ/mol

 ozone is unstable w.r.t. O2 at 298 K
⚡ JEE Trick Split the logarithm of a scientific-notation value into mantissa and exponent: log(2.47 × 10−29) = 0.39 − 29. Trying to handle it in one step is where sign errors creep in.
Answer: ΔrG° ≈ +163 kJ
37The heat of combustion of solid benzoic acid at constant volume is −321.30 kJ at 27°C. The heat of combustion at constant pressure is (−321.30 − xR) kJ; the value of x is ______. (JEE Main 2024, 5 Apr Shift 1, Level-3)ΔH from ΔU for benzoic acidIIT JEE Chemistry Bomb Calorimeter & Tricks to solve Bomb Calorimeter questions easily by NV sir🟡 Similarx = 150
Concept A bomb calorimeter measures ΔU at constant volume; converting to the constant-pressure ΔH needs the gas-mole correction. Benzoic acid consumes slightly more oxygen than the carbon dioxide it produces, so Δng is negative.
Key relation ΔH = ΔU + ΔngRT Only gases count; the solid acid and the liquid water contribute nothing to Δng.
Solution
1. Balanced combustion: C6H5COOH(s) + (15/2)O2(g) → 7CO2(g) + 3H2O(l).
2. Count the gases: 7 moles produced against 7.5 consumed, so Δng = 7 − 7.5 = −0.5.
3. Temperature: 27 °C = 300 K.
4. Correction term: ΔngRT = (−0.5)(R)(300) = −150R.
5. So ΔH = ΔU + ΔngRT = −321.30 − 150R kJ, which matches the printed form (−321.30 − xR) with x = 150.
6. Numerically the correction is only (0.5)(8.314)(300) = 1247 J = 1.25 kJ, so ΔH ≈ −322.5 kJ — slightly more exothermic than ΔU, because the system contracts and the surroundings do work on it.
7. Watch the sign of Δng: it is negative here because oxygen is consumed faster than carbon dioxide is made. A positive Δng would have made ΔH less exothermic instead.
 C6H5COOH(s) + 15/2 O2 -> 7CO2 + 3H2O(l)

 gases : 7 out, 7.5 in  ->  dn_g = -0.5
 T = 27 C = 300 K

 dn_g R T = -0.5 x R x 300 = -150 R
 dH = -321.30 - 150R    ->    x = 150

 numerically 150R = 1.25 kJ
⚡ JEE Trick When the answer is left in terms of R, do not substitute its value — just collect the coefficient. Here x is simply |Δng| × T = 0.5 × 300.
Answer: x = 150
38Standard enthalpy of vapourisation for CCl4 is 30.5 kJ mol−1. Heat required for vapourisation of 284 g of CCl4 at constant temperature is ______ kJ. (Given molar mass in g mol−1: C = 12, Cl = 35.5) Round off to the nearest integer. (JEE Main 2024, 29 Jan Shift 2, Level-2)Scaling ΔHvap by molesClass 11 Chap 6 | Thermodynamics 13 | Entropy Change in Phase Change in Equilibrium IIT JEE/NEET🟡 Similar56 kJ
Concept Enthalpy of vaporisation is quoted per mole, so the heat needed for a given mass is the molar value times the number of moles. The only step that needs care is building the molar mass of CCl4 from its atomic masses.
Key relation q = n × ΔHvap, with n = mass/molar mass Vaporisation at constant temperature means all the heat goes into the phase change.
Solution
1. Molar mass of CCl4: carbon contributes 12 and four chlorines contribute 4 × 35.5 = 142, giving 154 g mol−1.
2. Moles present: n = 284/154 = 1.844 mol.
3. Heat required: q = 1.844 × 30.5 kJ mol−1.
4. Evaluate: 1.844 × 30.5 = 56.24 kJ.
5. Rounded to the nearest integer, the answer is 56 kJ.
6. A quick check on the scale: 284 g is just under two moles, so the answer should be just under twice 30.5, that is a little below 61 kJ — and 56 kJ fits. Note also that this is ΔH, the heat at constant pressure; the corresponding ΔU would be smaller by ΔngRT.
7. If ΔU were wanted instead, one mole of vapour appears per mole vaporised, so ΔU = ΔH − nRT = 56.24 − 1.844(8.314)(350)/1000 ≈ 50.9 kJ near the boiling point of CCl4.
 M(CCl4) = 12 + 4(35.5) = 154 g/mol
 n = 284 / 154 = 1.844 mol

 q = 1.844 x 30.5 = 56.24 kJ
   = 56 kJ (nearest integer)

 sanity: just under 2 mol -> just under 61 kJ
⚡ JEE Trick Build the molar mass carefully before anything else. Using 35.5 for chlorine rather than 35 changes 154 to 152 and shifts the answer by nearly a whole kilojoule — enough to miss an integer answer.
Answer: 56 kJ
39If 5 moles of an ideal gas expands from 10 L to a volume of 100 L at 300 K under isothermal and reversible condition then work, w, is −x J. The value of x is ______. (R = 8.314 J K−1 mol−1) (JEE Main 2024, 31 Jan Shift 2, Level-2)Reversible isothermal workThermodynamics || Calculation of W, q, U, H for reversible isothermal expansion process🟡 Similarx = 28721 (w = −28721 J)
Concept A reversible isothermal expansion does the maximum possible work, given by the logarithmic formula. A tenfold volume increase makes the logarithm exactly ln 10, which the question expects to be taken as 2.303.
Key relation w = −nRT ln(V2/V1) = −2.303 nRT log(V2/V1) The minus sign records work done by the gas on the surroundings.
Solution
1. Data: n = 5 mol, T = 300 K, V1 = 10 L, V2 = 100 L, R = 8.314 J K−1 mol−1.
2. Volume ratio: V2/V1 = 100/10 = 10, so the logarithm is ln 10 = 2.303.
3. Build the prefactor: nRT = 5 × 8.314 × 300 = 12471 J.
4. Multiply: w = −12471 × 2.303 = −28721 J.
5. Since the question writes w = −x J, the value required is x = 28721.
6. Because the expansion is isothermal and the gas ideal, ΔU = 0 and the same 28.7 kJ must be absorbed as heat. Had the gas expanded irreversibly against the final pressure instead, the work would have been only −nRT(1 − V1/V2) = −11224 J, well under half as much.
7. That gap is the general rule in miniature: the reversible path always extracts the most work, and every finite pressure difference wastes some of it.
 n = 5 mol   T = 300 K   10 L -> 100 L

 nRT = 5(8.314)(300) = 12471 J
 ln(100/10) = ln10 = 2.303

 w = -12471 x 2.303 = -28721 J
 x = 28721

 q = +28721 J  (dU = 0)
⚡ JEE Trick Tenfold volume changes are chosen deliberately so that ln(V2/V1) = 2.303 exactly. Spotting the factor of ten saves reaching for a logarithm table.
Answer: x = 28721 (w = −28.72 kJ)
40For a certain reaction at 300 K, K = 10, then ΔG° for the same reaction is −______ × 10−1 kJ mol−1. (R = 8.314 J K−1 mol−1) (JEE Main 2024, 01 Feb Shift 2, Level-2)ΔG° from K = 1012: The relationship between K and Gibbs free energy🟡 Similar57 (ΔG° = −5.74 kJ mol<sup>−1</sup>)
Concept An equilibrium constant of exactly 10 makes log K equal to 1, so the free energy change is simply the prefactor 2.303 RT with a minus sign. The answer is then rescaled into the requested × 10−1 form.
Key relation ΔG° = −2.303 RT log K With log K = 1 the whole calculation reduces to evaluating 2.303 RT at 300 K.
Solution
1. Data: K = 10 at T = 300 K, with R = 8.314 J K−1 mol−1.
2. Since K = 10, log K = 1 exactly, which is why the numbers were chosen this way.
3. Prefactor: 2.303 × 8.314 = 19.147, and 19.147 × 300 = 5744 J mol−1.
4. Substitute: ΔG° = −5744 × 1 = −5744 J mol−1 = −5.744 kJ mol−1.
5. Express in the requested form: −5.744 kJ mol−1 = −57.44 × 10−1 kJ mol−1, so the value to fill in is 57.
6. This is worth remembering as a benchmark: at around room temperature, one power of ten in K corresponds to roughly 5.7 kJ mol−1 of ΔG°. Every further decade in K costs another 5.7 kJ.
7. Sign check: K exceeds 1, so the products are favoured and ΔG° must be negative — which the minus sign printed in the answer format already anticipates.
 K = 10   ->   log K = 1
 T = 300 K

 2.303 R T = 19.147 x 300 = 5744 J/mol
 dG0 = -5744 J = -5.744 kJ/mol
     = -57.44 x 10^-1 kJ/mol

 answer to fill : 57
⚡ JEE Trick Commit "5.7 kJ mol−1 per decade of K near room temperature" to memory. It turns most ΔG°–K questions into a one-line multiplication and gives an instant sanity check on any answer.
Answer: 57 (ΔG° = −5.744 kJ mol−1)
41The enthalpy of formation of ethane (C2H6) from ethylene by addition of hydrogen where the bond energies of C−H, C−C, C=C, H−H are 414 kJ, 347 kJ, 615 kJ and 435 kJ respectively is ______ kJ. (JEE Main 2024, 4 Apr Shift 1, Level-2)Hydrogenation enthalpy from bond energiesTHERMOCHEMISTRY । Class 11 (L3) I Bond energy | Resonance energy | Born Haber Cycle | JEE | NEET🟡 Similar−125 kJ (magnitude 125)
Concept A bond-energy calculation: total the bonds broken in the reactants, total the bonds formed in the products, and subtract. Careful counting of the C−H bonds on each side is the only real difficulty.
Key relation ΔH = ΣBE(bonds broken) − ΣBE(bonds formed) Ethene has four C−H bonds and ethane has six, so two extra C−H bonds are created.
Solution
1. Reaction: CH2=CH2(g) + H2(g) → CH3CH3(g).
2. Bonds broken: one C=C at 615, four C−H at 414 each, and one H−H at 435. Total = 615 + 1656 + 435 = 2706 kJ.
3. Bonds formed: one C−C at 347 and six C−H at 414 each. Total = 347 + 2484 = 2831 kJ.
4. Subtract: ΔH = 2706 − 2831 = −125 kJ.
5. The magnitude asked for is therefore 125 kJ, and the negative sign confirms hydrogenation is exothermic.
6. A shortcut worth seeing: only the changes matter. One C=C and one H−H are replaced by one C−C and two C−H, so ΔH = (615 + 435) − (347 + 2 × 414) = 1050 − 1175 = −125 kJ — the four unchanged C−H bonds cancel.
7. The value is close to the measured hydrogenation enthalpy of ethene, about −137 kJ mol−1; bond energies are averages, so agreement within about 10% is all one should expect.
 C2H4 + H2 -> C2H6

 broken : 615 + 4(414) + 435 = 2706 kJ
 formed : 347 + 6(414)       = 2831 kJ
 -------------------------------------
 dH = 2706 - 2831 = -125 kJ

 shortcut: (615+435) - (347+2x414) = -125
⚡ JEE Trick Cancel the bonds that appear unchanged on both sides before adding. In hydrogenations only the π bond, the H−H bond and two new C−H bonds actually change.
Answer: ΔH = −125 kJ (magnitude 125 kJ)
