42For the reaction at 298 K, 2A + B → C, ΔH = 400 kJ mol−1 and ΔS = 0.2 kJ mol−1 K−1. The reaction will become spontaneous above ______ K. (JEE Main 2024, 6 Apr Shift 2, Level-2)Crossover temperatureProblem: Find temperature a reaction becomes spontaneous🟡 Similar2000 K
Concept With both ΔH and ΔS positive, the reaction is blocked when cold and runs when hot. The switch happens where ΔG passes through zero, at the ratio ΔH/ΔS.
Key relation ΔG = 0 ⇒ T = ΔH/ΔS; spontaneous for T > Tcross Both quantities are supplied in kilojoule units here, so no conversion is needed.
Solution
1. Data: ΔH = 400 kJ mol−1 and ΔS = 0.2 kJ mol−1 K−1 for 2A + B → C.
2. Both are positive, so this is the "spontaneous only at high temperature" family and a finite crossover must exist.
3. Note that the entropy is already in kJ K−1, matching the enthalpy, so the units combine directly.
4. Set ΔG = ΔH − TΔS = 0, giving T = ΔH/ΔS = 400/0.2.
5. Evaluate: T = 2000 K. Above this temperature ΔG turns negative and the reaction becomes spontaneous.
6. Check either side: at 1000 K, ΔG = 400 − 200 = +200 kJ, blocked; at 3000 K, ΔG = 400 − 600 = −200 kJ, spontaneous. The switch sits at 2000 K exactly as found.
7. A crossover of 2000 K is very high, so at any ordinary laboratory temperature this reaction is firmly blocked — the large positive ΔH simply overwhelms the entropy term.
 2A + B -> C
 dH = 400 kJ/mol   dS = 0.2 kJ/K/mol

 dG = 0  ->  T = 400 / 0.2 = 2000 K

 T = 1000 K -> dG = +200 kJ  blocked
 T = 3000 K -> dG = -200 kJ  spontaneous
⚡ JEE Trick Check the entropy unit before dividing. Here it is already kJ K−1; had it been 200 J K−1 the same answer would need ΔH in joules, and mixing the two gives 2 K or 2,000,000 K.
Answer: spontaneous above 2000 K
43Which of the following is not correct?
(a) ΔG is negative for a spontaneous reaction   (b) ΔG is positive for a spontaneous reaction   (c) ΔG is zero for a reversible reaction   (d) ΔG is positive for a non-spontaneous reaction   (JEE Main 2024, 29 Jan Shift 1, Level-1)		Spotting the reversed sign ruleGibb's Energy and Spontaneity - Thermodynamics (Part 28)🟡 Similarb
Concept Three of the four statements are the standard sign rules for ΔG and one is a deliberate inversion. Writing the three-line criterion out first makes the odd one obvious.
Key relation ΔG < 0 spontaneous; ΔG = 0 equilibrium or reversible; ΔG > 0 non-spontaneous A single sign distinguishes the three cases at constant T and P.
Solution
1. (a) "ΔG is negative for a spontaneous reaction" — this is the criterion itself, so it is correct.
2. (b) "ΔG is positive for a spontaneous reaction" — this reverses the rule. A positive ΔG means work must be supplied, so the reaction cannot be spontaneous. Not correct.
3. (c) "ΔG is zero for a reversible reaction" — a reversible change proceeds through equilibrium states, where ΔG vanishes. Correct.
4. (d) "ΔG is positive for a non-spontaneous reaction" — the direct statement of the third line of the criterion. Correct.
5. The question asks which is not correct, so the answer is (b).
6. Note that (a) and (b) directly contradict each other, so exactly one of them must be false. Spotting that pair immediately halves the work, and the criterion then decides which one to discard.
 dG < 0   spontaneous          (a) OK
 dG > 0   spontaneous          (b) WRONG
 dG = 0   reversible/eqm       (c) OK
 dG > 0   non-spontaneous      (d) OK

 (a) and (b) contradict: one must be false
⚡ JEE Trick Scan for contradictory pairs first. When two options make opposite claims about the same quantity, one of them is the answer to a "which is not correct" question.
Answer: (b) ΔG is positive for a spontaneous reaction
44Choose the correct option for free expansion of an ideal gas under adiabatic condition:
(a) q = 0, ΔT ≠ 0, w = 0   (b) q = 0, ΔT < 0, w ≠ 0   (c) q ≠ 0, ΔT < 0, w = 0   (d) q = 0, ΔT = 0, w = 0   (JEE Main 2024, 01 Feb Shift 1, Level-1)		Adiabatic free expansionFree expansion of an ideal gas | Thermodynamics | Chemistry | Khan Academy🟢 Exactd
Concept Free expansion into a vacuum combined with adiabatic walls switches off almost every quantity at once. No heat crosses the boundary, nothing opposes the gas so no work is done, and consequently the internal energy and temperature are unchanged.
Key relation Vacuum ⇒ pex = 0 so w = 0; adiabatic ⇒ q = 0; hence ΔU = 0 and for an ideal gas ΔT = 0 The internal energy of an ideal gas depends only on temperature, so ΔU = 0 forces ΔT = 0.
Solution
1. Heat. The condition is stated to be adiabatic, so no heat is exchanged: q = 0.
2. Work. Free expansion means expansion into a vacuum, where the external pressure is zero. Since w = −pexΔV, the work is w = 0 however large the volume change.
3. Internal energy. The first law gives ΔU = q + w = 0 + 0 = 0.
4. Temperature. For an ideal gas U depends only on T, so ΔU = 0 requires ΔT = 0. The gas neither warms nor cools.
5. The set q = 0, ΔT = 0, w = 0 is option (d).
6. The one quantity that is not zero is the entropy: ΔS = nR ln(V2/V1) > 0, because the process is thoroughly irreversible. That is exactly why free expansion never runs backwards on its own.
7. Contrast this with a reversible adiabatic expansion, where q = 0 but the gas does real work, so ΔU is negative and the gas cools noticeably. It is the vacuum, not the insulation, that makes ΔT vanish here.
 vacuum    ->  pex = 0  ->  w = 0
 adiabatic ->  q = 0
 -------------------------------
 dU = q + w = 0
 ideal gas -> dT = 0

 but dS = nR ln(V2/V1) > 0 : irreversible
⚡ JEE Trick Free expansion zeroes q, w, ΔU and ΔT — but never ΔS. Any option claiming the entropy is unchanged is wrong for exactly that reason.
Answer: (d) q = 0, ΔT = 0, w = 0
45Assertion (A): Enthalpy of neutralisation of strong monobasic acid with strong monoacidic base is always −57 kJ mol−1. Reason (R): Enthalpy of neutralisation is the amount of heat liberated when one mole of H+ ions furnished by acid combine with one mole of OH− ions furnished by base to form one mole of water.
(a) (A) is true but (R) is false   (b) Both (A) and (R) are true but (R) is not the correct explanation of (A)   (c) Both (A) and (R) are true and (R) is the correct explanation of (A)   (d) (A) is false but (R) is true   (JEE Main 2024, 5 Apr Shift 1, Level-1)		Assertion–reason on neutralisationTHERMO | 17 | ENTHALPY OF NEUTRALISATION🟡 Similarc
Concept Both statements have to be judged separately, and then the reason has to be tested as an explanation of the assertion. Here the definition in R is precisely what makes the constant value in A inevitable.
Key relation Strong acid + strong base: the only net reaction is H+(aq) + OH−(aq) → H2O(l), ΔH ≈ −57 kJ mol−1 Both are fully ionised already, so no energy is spent on ionisation.
Solution
1. Assertion. A strong monobasic acid and a strong monoacidic base are both completely dissociated in solution, so the spectator ions take no part.
2. Whatever the pair — HCl with NaOH, HNO3 with KOH — the only chemical change is H+ combining with OH− to make water, giving the same −57 kJ mol−1 every time. The assertion is true.
3. Reason. The definition quoted — the heat liberated when one mole of H+ from the acid combines with one mole of OH− from the base to form one mole of water — is the standard definition. True.
4. Is R the explanation of A? Yes. Precisely because the measured quantity is defined by that single reaction, and because strong electrolytes supply their ions already dissociated, the value cannot vary from one strong pair to another.
5. So both are true and R correctly explains A, which is option (c).
6. The contrast with weak acids confirms the logic: acetic acid gives only about −55.3 kJ mol−1, because roughly 2 kJ is consumed ionising the acid before neutralisation can release it.
 strong acid + strong base
   both fully ionised
   net reaction : H+ + OH- -> H2O
   dH = -57 kJ/mol every time      A TRUE

 R = the definition of dH_neut      R TRUE
 R explains why A is constant       -> (c)

 weak acid : -55.3 kJ/mol (ionisation cost)
⚡ JEE Trick In assertion–reason questions, decide truth first and explanation second. A true reason that merely restates a definition often is the explanation, as it is here.
Answer: (c) Both (A) and (R) are true and (R) is the correct explanation of (A)
4628.0 L of CO2 is produced on complete combustion of 16.8 L gaseous mixture of ethene and methane at 25°C and 1 atm. Heat evolved during the combustion process is ______ kJ. Given ΔHC(CH4) = −900 kJ mol−1, ΔHC(C2H4) = −1400 kJ mol−1. (JEE Main 2023, 25 Jan Shift 2, Level-6)Mixture combustion with a volume clueHess's Law Problems & Enthalpy Change - Chemistry🟡 Similar847 kJ
Concept Two simultaneous equations settle the mixture composition: one for the total volume and one for the carbon dioxide produced. Since all the gases are at the same temperature and pressure, volumes stand in for moles until the very last step.
Key relation CH4 gives 1 CO2, C2H4 gives 2 CO2; molar volume at 298 K, 1 atm = RT/P = 24.47 L mol−1 Equal volumes contain equal moles, so the carbon balance can be written directly in litres.
Solution
1. Let the mixture contain x litres of ethene and y litres of methane, so x + y = 16.8.
2. Carbon balance: each litre of C2H4 gives 2 L of CO2 and each litre of CH4 gives 1 L, so 2x + y = 28.0.
3. Subtract the first from the second: x = 11.2 L of ethene, and hence y = 5.6 L of methane.
4. Convert to moles using the molar volume 0.0821 × 298 = 24.47 L mol−1: n(C2H4) = 11.2/24.47 = 0.458 mol and n(CH4) = 5.6/24.47 = 0.229 mol.
5. Heat evolved: 0.458 × 1400 + 0.229 × 900 = 641 + 206 = 847 kJ.
6. Sense check: ethene is both the larger share of the mixture and the more exothermic fuel, so it should dominate the total — and it supplies about three-quarters of the 847 kJ.
 x = L of C2H4 , y = L of CH4
 x + y  = 16.8      (total volume)
 2x + y = 28.0      (CO2 produced)
 --------------------------------
 x = 11.2 L , y = 5.6 L

 molar volume = 0.0821 x 298 = 24.47 L/mol
 n(C2H4) = 0.458 , n(CH4) = 0.229

 heat = 0.458(1400) + 0.229(900) = 847 kJ
⚡ JEE Trick Work in litres for as long as possible. At fixed T and P, volume ratios are mole ratios, so the two simultaneous equations can be solved before any conversion is needed.
Answer: heat evolved = 847 kJ
47The enthalpy change for the conversion of ½Cl2(g) to Cl−(aq) is (−)______ kJ mol−1. (Nearest integer) Given ΔdisH°(Cl2) = 240 kJ mol−1, ΔegH°(Cl) = −350 kJ mol−1, ΔhydH°(Cl−) = −380 kJ mol−1. (JEE Main 2023, 31 Jan Shift 1, Level-6)Three-step cycle to hydrated chloride11 Chap 4 | Chemical Bonding and Molecular Structure 03| Lattice Energy | Born Haber Cycle IIT JEE |🟡 Similar610 kJ mol<sup>−1</sup> (ΔH = −610)
Concept Turning half a mole of chlorine gas into aqueous chloride takes three steps: split the molecule, add an electron, then hydrate the ion. Adding the three enthalpies gives the overall change.
Key relation ΔH = ½ΔdisH° + ΔegH° + ΔhydH° Only half the dissociation enthalpy is needed, since only one chlorine atom is produced.
Solution
1. Step 1, dissociation. ½Cl2(g) → Cl(g) costs ½ × 240 = +120 kJ mol−1.
2. Step 2, electron gain. Cl(g) + e− → Cl−(g) releases −350 kJ mol−1.
3. Step 3, hydration. Cl−(g) → Cl−(aq) releases −380 kJ mol−1.
4. Add the three: ΔH = 120 − 350 − 380 = −610 kJ mol−1.
5. The question writes the answer as (−)______, so the value to fill in is 610.
6. The overall process is strongly exothermic, which is why chloride ions are so stable in aqueous solution: the two releasing steps together return 730 kJ against the 120 kJ spent breaking the Cl−Cl bond.
7. Note that the electron gain enthalpy quoted here, −350 kJ mol−1, is for the gaseous atom; the hydration step is separate and is the larger of the two exothermic contributions for many halides.
 1/2 Cl2(g) -> Cl(g)        : +120 kJ
 Cl(g) + e- -> Cl-(g)       : -350 kJ
 Cl-(g)     -> Cl-(aq)      : -380 kJ
 -------------------------------------
 1/2 Cl2(g) -> Cl-(aq)      : -610 kJ

 answer to fill : 610
⚡ JEE Trick Halve the bond dissociation enthalpy whenever the equation starts from ½X2. Using the full 240 here would give −490 and miss the answer entirely.
Answer: 610 (ΔH = −610 kJ mol−1)
48The value of log K for the reaction A ⇌ B at 298 K is ______. (Nearest integer) Given ΔH° = −54.07 kJ mol−1, ΔS° = 10 J K−1 mol−1. (Taken 2.303 × 8.314 × 298 = 5705) (JEE Main 2023, 06 Apr Shift 1, Level-6)log K from thermodynamic dataLast Relation between delta G and K | Gibbs energy and equilibrium constant relation mathematically🟢 Exactlog K = 10
Concept Build ΔG° from the enthalpy and entropy, then convert to log K. Here both terms push in the same direction — the reaction is exothermic and entropy-increasing — so they add rather than cancel.
Key relation ΔG° = ΔH° − TΔS°; log K = −ΔG°/(2.303RT) The question supplies 2.303 × 8.314 × 298 = 5705 directly, so no prefactor arithmetic is needed.
Solution
1. Data: ΔH° = −54.07 kJ mol−1 = −54070 J mol−1, ΔS° = 10 J K−1 mol−1, T = 298 K.
2. Entropy term: TΔS° = 298 × 10 = 2980 J mol−1.
3. Free energy: ΔG° = −54070 − 2980 = −57050 J mol−1. Note the subtraction of a positive TΔS° from a negative ΔH° makes the result more negative.
4. Apply the isotherm with the supplied prefactor: log K = 57050/5705.
5. Divide: log K = 10.0 exactly, so K = 1010.
6. The numbers were clearly chosen to give a round answer, and the huge K confirms that A ⇌ B lies almost entirely on the B side — unsurprising when both driving factors are favourable.
7. The same data appear as Level-4 Q3 of the Strong Box, where the answer is also 10 — a useful consistency check across the two sections.
 dH0 = -54070 J/mol   dS0 = 10 J/K/mol
 T = 298 K

 T dS0 = 2980 J
 dG0 = -54070 - 2980 = -57050 J

 log K = 57050 / 5705 = 10.0
 K = 10^10
⚡ JEE Trick Exothermic plus entropy-increasing means the two terms add in magnitude. If |ΔG°| comes out smaller than |ΔH°| in that situation, a sign has gone astray.
Answer: log K = 10 (ΔG° = −57.05 kJ mol−1)
49Consider the graph of Gibbs free energy G vs extent of reaction, with point (a) high on the descending branch, (b) at the minimum and (c) just past the minimum. The number of statements from the following which are true with respect to points (a), (b) and (c) is ______.
(i) Reaction is spontaneous at (a) and (b)   (ii) Reaction is at equilibrium at point (b) and non-spontaneous at point (c)   (iii) Reaction is spontaneous at (a) and non-spontaneous at (c)   (iv) Reaction is non-spontaneous at (a) and (b)   (JEE Main 2023, 06 Apr Shift 1, Level-6)		Counting true statements on a G curveGibb's Energy and Spontaneity - Thermodynamics (Part 28)🟡 Similar2
Concept On a plot of Gibbs energy against extent of reaction, the sign of ΔG is simply the slope in the forward direction. Point (a) is on the descending branch, (b) is the minimum, and (c) is past it on the rising branch.
Key relation Descending ⇒ ΔG < 0 spontaneous; minimum ⇒ ΔG = 0 equilibrium; ascending ⇒ ΔG > 0 non-spontaneous The curve is a valley, and the system rolls to the bottom from either side.
Solution
1. Fix the three points: at (a) the curve is falling steeply, so the forward reaction is spontaneous; at (b) it is at its minimum, so the system is at equilibrium; at (c) it has begun to rise, so the forward reaction is non-spontaneous.
2. Statement (i): "spontaneous at (a) and (b)". Point (b) is equilibrium, where there is no net drive, so calling it spontaneous is wrong. False.
3. Statement (ii): "at equilibrium at (b) and non-spontaneous at (c)". Both halves match the reading above. True.
4. Statement (iii): "spontaneous at (a) and non-spontaneous at (c)". Again both halves are right. True.
5. Statement (iv): "non-spontaneous at (a) and (b)". Point (a) is firmly on the descending branch and is spontaneous, so this fails. False.
6. Two statements survive, so the number required is 2.
 G
 |*a
 | \
 |  \___ b ___ c
 +----------------> extent

 (a) falling  -> spontaneous
 (b) minimum  -> equilibrium
 (c) rising   -> non-spontaneous

 (i) F   (ii) T   (iii) T   (iv) F   ->  2
⚡ JEE Trick Equilibrium is not spontaneity. Any statement that lumps the minimum in with the descending branch is false, and that single distinction settles most questions of this type.
Answer: 2 statements are true (namely the second and third)
50For complete combustion of ethene, C2H4(g) + 3O2(g) → 2CO2(g) + 2H2O(l), the amount of heat produced as measured in bomb calorimeter is 1406 kJ mol−1 at 300 K. The minimum value of TΔS needed to reach equilibrium is (−)______ kJ. (Nearest integer) Given R = 8.3 J K−1 mol−1. (JEE Main 2023, 08 April Shift 2, Level-6)TΔS needed to reach ΔG = 0the amount of heat produced as measured by bomb calorimeter | 26 June 2022 |Jee Main PYQ🟢 Exact1411 kJ
Concept A bomb calorimeter reports ΔU, so the gas-mole correction is needed to reach ΔH. Equilibrium then requires ΔG = 0, which means TΔS must exactly match ΔH.
Key relation ΔH = ΔU + ΔngRT; at equilibrium ΔG = 0 so TΔS = ΔH The minimum TΔS that reaches equilibrium is therefore just ΔH itself.
Solution
1. Reaction: C2H4(g) + 3O2(g) → 2CO2(g) + 2H2O(l), with ΔU = −1406 kJ mol−1 at 300 K.
2. Count the gases: 2 moles of CO2 are produced against 4 moles consumed (1 ethene plus 3 oxygen), and the water is liquid. So Δng = 2 − 4 = −2.
3. Correction term: ΔngRT = (−2)(8.3)(300) = −4980 J = −4.98 kJ.
4. Enthalpy: ΔH = −1406 − 4.98 = −1410.98 kJ mol−1.
5. At equilibrium ΔG = ΔH − TΔS = 0, so TΔS = ΔH = −1411 kJ to the nearest integer.
6. Interpretation: combustion is so exothermic that an enormous entropy penalty would be needed to halt it. Since the real TΔS is nowhere near −1411 kJ, the reaction runs essentially to completion — which is why fuels burn rather than reach a balance.
 C2H4(g) + 3O2(g) -> 2CO2(g) + 2H2O(l)

 gases : 2 out, 4 in  ->  dn_g = -2
 dn_g R T = -2(8.3)(300) = -4980 J

 dH = -1406 - 4.98 = -1410.98 kJ
 equilibrium : T dS = dH = -1411 kJ
⚡ JEE Trick "Minimum TΔS needed to reach equilibrium" is just ΔH in disguise, since ΔG = 0 forces the two to be equal. The only real work is the ΔU → ΔH conversion.
Answer: TΔS = −1411 kJ (magnitude 1411)
51One mole of an ideal monatomic gas undergoes two reversible processes A→B (adiabatic, from V1 = 10 m3, T1 = 600 K to V2, T2 = 60 K) and B→C (isothermal at T2 to V3). If the total heat absorbed in the entire process is RT2 ln 10, the value of 2 log V3 is ______. [Cp,m = (5/2)R] (JEE Adv. 2023, Paper-1, Level-6)Adiabatic plus isothermal legs13 REVERSIBLE AND IRREVERSIBLE ADIABATIC PROCESS | IIT ADVANCED | JEE MAIN | CHEMISTRY CLASS 11🟡 Similar2 log V<sub>3</sub> = 7
Concept The adiabatic leg exchanges no heat, so the entire heat absorbed comes from the isothermal leg. Matching that to the given RT2 ln 10 fixes the volume ratio V3/V2, and the adiabatic relation supplies V2.
Key relation Adiabatic: T1V1γ−1 = T2V2γ−1; isothermal: q = nRT2 ln(V3/V2) Cp,m = (5/2)R marks a monatomic gas, so γ = 5/3 and γ − 1 = 2/3.
Solution
1. Path A→B is adiabatic, so q = 0 there. All the heat is absorbed on B→C, giving nRT2 ln(V3/V2) = RT2 ln 10.
2. With n = 1 this forces V3/V2 = 10.
3. Now find V2 from the adiabatic leg: 600 × 102/3 = 60 × V22/3.
4. Divide by 60: V22/3 = 10 × 102/3 = 105/3, so V2 = 105/2 m3.
5. Then V3 = 10 × V2 = 101 × 105/2 = 107/2, so log V3 = 7/2.
6. Hence 2 log V3 = 7. The awkward "2 log" wrapper exists precisely so that the half-integer exponent turns into a clean whole number.
 A->B adiabatic : q = 0
 B->C isothermal: q = RT2 ln(V3/V2) = RT2 ln10
                  ->  V3/V2 = 10

 600(10)^(2/3) = 60 V2^(2/3)
 V2^(2/3) = 10^(5/3)   ->  V2 = 10^(5/2)
 V3 = 10 x 10^(5/2) = 10^(7/2)

 2 log V3 = 2 x 3.5 = 7
⚡ JEE Trick Keep everything as powers of ten. Writing V2 = 105/2 rather than 316.2 makes the final logarithm exact and removes any rounding.
Answer: 2 log V3 = 7
52Passage I. The entropy versus temperature plot for phases α and β at 1 bar is given, with ST − S0 = 5 for α and 6 for β at 600 K. The transition temperature for α to β is 600 K and Cp,β − Cp,α = 1 J mol−1 K−1, independent of temperature between 200 and 700 K. The value of entropy change Sβ − Sα (in J mol−1 K−1) at 300 K is ______. [ln 2 = 0.69; Sβ − Sα = 0 at 0 K] (JEE Adv. 2023, Paper-2, Level-5)Entropy difference between two phases19 CALCULATION OF ENTROPY | THERMODYNAMICS | IIT ADVANCED | JEE MAIN | CHEMISTRY CLASS 11🟡 Similar0.31 J mol<sup>−1</sup> K<sup>−1</sup>
Concept The two phases have entropy curves that differ by a known amount at the transition temperature. Moving away from 600 K, the gap changes because the two heat capacities differ, and the change follows the integral of (CP,β − CP,α)/T.
Key relation Sβ(T) − Sα(T) = [Sβ − Sα]600 + (CP,β − CP,α) ln(T/600) The capacity difference is constant at 1 J mol−1 K−1 over the range 200–700 K.
Solution
1. Read the plot at the transition temperature: at 600 K the β curve sits at 6 and the α curve at 5, so Sβ − Sα = 1 J mol−1 K−1 there.
2. Below 600 K the gap changes at the rate d(ΔS)/dT = (CP,β − CP,α)/T = 1/T.
3. Integrating from 600 K down to 300 K: Δ(ΔS) = 1 × ln(300/600) = ln 0.5 = −0.69.
4. So Sβ − Sα at 300 K = 1 − 0.69 = 0.31 J mol−1 K−1.
5. The supplied value ln 2 = 0.69 is exactly what the halving of temperature requires, confirming the intended route.
6. Sense check on the direction: β has the larger heat capacity, so its entropy climbs faster with temperature. Cooling from 600 K to 300 K therefore narrows the gap, which is why the answer is smaller than 1.
 at 600 K : S_beta - S_alpha = 6 - 5 = 1 J/K/mol
 Cp,beta - Cp,alpha = 1 J/K/mol  (constant)

 d(dS) = (1) ln(300/600) = -ln2 = -0.69

 S_beta - S_alpha at 300 K = 1 - 0.69 = 0.31
⚡ JEE Trick Differences of state functions between two phases can be propagated with the difference of heat capacities. There is no need to know either CP separately.
Answer: Sβ − Sα = 0.31 J mol−1 K−1 at 300 K
53Passage II. Same α/β system as above. The value of enthalpy change Hβ − Hα (in J mol−1) at 300 K is ______. (JEE Adv. 2023, Paper-2, Level-5)Enthalpy difference between two phasesMeasurements of ∆U and ∆H || Calorimetery || Thermodynamics Part - 5 || Class-11 | Chemistry🟡 Similar300 J mol<sup>−1</sup>
Concept At the transition temperature the two phases are in equilibrium, so ΔG = 0 and the enthalpy gap equals T times the entropy gap. Away from 600 K the enthalpy difference shifts by the integral of the heat-capacity difference.
Key relation At 600 K: Hβ − Hα = T(Sβ − Sα); then ΔH changes by (CP,β − CP,α)ΔT Unlike the entropy, the enthalpy gap changes linearly in T, not logarithmically.
Solution
1. At the transition temperature the phases coexist, so ΔG = 0 and Hβ − Hα = 600 × (Sβ − Sα).
2. From the plot the entropy gap at 600 K is 1 J mol−1 K−1, so Hβ − Hα = 600 J mol−1 at 600 K.
3. Cooling to 300 K changes the gap by (CP,β − CP,α)ΔT = 1 × (300 − 600) = −300 J mol−1.
4. So at 300 K, Hβ − Hα = 600 − 300 = 300 J mol−1.
5. Note the contrast with the previous part: entropy propagates through ln(T2/T1) while enthalpy propagates through the plain difference (T2 − T1). That is simply because dH = CPdT but dS = CPdT/T.
6. Consistency check: at 300 K, ΔG = ΔH − TΔS = 300 − 300(0.31) = +207 J mol−1, positive — so below the transition the α phase is the stable one, exactly as it should be.
 at 600 K : dG = 0
   H_beta - H_alpha = 600 x 1 = 600 J/mol

 cooling to 300 K:
   change = (Cp,b - Cp,a)(300 - 600) = -300 J

 H_beta - H_alpha at 300 K = 300 J/mol

 check: dG(300) = 300 - 300(0.31) = +207 J
⚡ JEE Trick Remember which one takes the logarithm: enthalpy uses CPΔT and entropy uses CP ln(T2/T1). Swapping them is the standard error in two-phase problems.
Answer: Hβ − Hα = 300 J mol−1 at 300 K
54Which of the following relations are correct? (A) ΔU = q + pΔV; (B) ΔG = ΔH − TΔS; (C) ΔS = qrev/T; (D) ΔH = ΔU − ΔnRT.
(a) C and D only   (b) B and C only   (c) A and B only   (d) B and D only   (JEE Main 2023, 29 Jan Shift 2, Level-4)		Which two relations are rightTHERMODYNAMICS - 19 || GIBBS FREE ENERGY (∆G = ∆H - T∆S )🟡 Similarb
Concept Four familiar-looking relations, two of which carry a flipped sign. Checking each against its derivation — or simply against the sign convention for work — separates them quickly.
Key relation ΔU = q − pΔV; ΔG = ΔH − TΔS; ΔS = qrev/T; ΔH = ΔU + ΔnRT Two of the printed statements invert the sign on the last term.
Solution
1. (A) "ΔU = q + pΔV". The first law is ΔU = q + w with w = −pΔV for expansion, so the correct form is ΔU = q − pΔV. As printed, incorrect.
2. (B) "ΔG = ΔH − TΔS" is the Gibbs–Helmholtz equation exactly as derived. Correct.
3. (C) "ΔS = qrev/T" is the definition of entropy change for a reversible isothermal step. Correct.
4. (D) "ΔH = ΔU − ΔnRT". From H = U + PV and PV = nRT for gases, the correct relation is ΔH = ΔU + ΔnRT. The printed sign is reversed, so it is incorrect.
5. Only (B) and (C) survive, which is option (b).
6. A quick physical test on (D): when a reaction produces gas, Δn is positive and the system must do extra work pushing the atmosphere back, so ΔH should exceed ΔU. The printed minus sign would make it smaller, contradicting that.
 (A) dU = q + p dV       WRONG (should be -)
 (B) dG = dH - T dS      OK
 (C) dS = q_rev / T      OK
 (D) dH = dU - dn RT     WRONG (should be +)

 => B and C only         = option (b)
⚡ JEE Trick Both wrong options here fail on a single sign. Test any suspect relation on a case you know: gas-producing reactions must have ΔH > ΔU, and expanding gases must lose internal energy.
Answer: (b) B and C only
55Given (A) 2CO(g) + O2(g) → 2CO2(g), ΔH°1 = −x kJ mol−1; (B) C(graphite) + O2(g) → CO2(g), ΔH°2 = −y kJ mol−1. The ΔH° for C(graphite) + ½O2(g) → CO(g) is:
(a) (2x − y)/2   (b) (x + 2y)/2   (c) (x − 2y)/2   (d) 2y − x   (JEE Main 2023, 10 April Shift 1, Level-4)		Hess's law for CO formationClass 11 Chapter 6 | Thermodynamics 08 || Hess's Law || Hess's Law Enthalpy Change IIT JEE / NEET |🟡 Similarc
Concept The partial oxidation of graphite to carbon monoxide cannot be measured directly, but it is the difference between the full combustion of graphite and half the combustion of carbon monoxide. Hess's law then supplies the answer.
Key relation Target = (B) − ½(A), so ΔH° = ΔH°2 − ½ΔH°1 Equation (A) involves two moles of CO, so it must be halved before subtracting.
Solution
1. Given: (A) 2CO(g) + O2(g) → 2CO2(g) with ΔH°1 = −x, and (B) C(graphite) + O2(g) → CO2(g) with ΔH°2 = −y.
2. Target: C(graphite) + ½O2(g) → CO(g).
3. Halve (A) to get CO(g) + ½O2(g) → CO2(g) with ΔH = −x/2, then reverse it: CO2(g) → CO(g) + ½O2(g) with ΔH = +x/2.
4. Add that to (B): C + O2 → CO2 followed by CO2 → CO + ½O2 gives C + ½O2 → CO, as required.
5. Sum the enthalpies: ΔH° = −y + x/2 = (x − 2y)/2, which is option (c).
6. Sense check with real values: x ≈ 566 and y ≈ 393.5 kJ mol−1, giving (566 − 787)/2 = −110.5 kJ mol−1 — the accepted formation enthalpy of carbon monoxide.
 (A) 2CO + O2 -> 2CO2      dH1 = -x
 (B) C + O2   -> CO2       dH2 = -y

 target : C + 1/2 O2 -> CO
        = (B) - (1/2)(A)

 dH = -y - (1/2)(-x) = (x - 2y)/2

 check: (566 - 787)/2 = -110.5 kJ/mol
⚡ JEE Trick Halve the equation before reversing it, not after. Doing both operations in one step is where the factor of two usually goes missing.
Answer: (c) (x − 2y)/2
56Solid fuel used in rocket is a mixture of Fe2O3 and Al (in ratio 1:2). The heat evolved (kJ) per gram of the mixture is ______. (Nearest integer) Given ΔfH°(Al2O3) = −1700 kJ mol−1, ΔfH°(Fe2O3) = −840 kJ mol−1; molar masses of Fe, Al and O are 56, 27 and 16 g mol−1. (JEE Main 2023, 11 April Shift 1, Level-4)Thermite reaction heat per gramTricks to solve Thermochemistry problems easily | Enthalpy of formation combustion🟡 Similar4 kJ per gram
Concept The thermite reaction is a single displacement whose enthalpy comes from the two oxide formation values. Because the answer is wanted per gram, the molar heat must be divided by the mass of the whole 1:2 mixture, not by one component.
Key relation Fe2O3 + 2Al → Al2O3 + 2Fe; ΔH = ΔfH(Al2O3) − ΔfH(Fe2O3) Aluminium and iron are elements, so only the two oxides contribute.
Solution
1. The stated 1:2 ratio is exactly the stoichiometry: Fe2O3 + 2Al → Al2O3 + 2Fe, so neither reagent is in excess.
2. Reaction enthalpy: ΔH = (−1700) − (−840) = −860 kJ per mole of reaction.
3. Mass of Fe2O3: 2(56) + 3(16) = 112 + 48 = 160 g.
4. Mass of 2Al: 2 × 27 = 54 g, so the total mixture mass per mole of reaction is 160 + 54 = 214 g.
5. Heat evolved per gram: 860/214 = 4.02 kJ g−1, which to the nearest integer is 4.
6. This high energy density per gram is exactly why thermite is used for welding rails and in incendiary devices — and the reaction generates enough heat to melt the iron it produces.
 Fe2O3 + 2Al -> Al2O3 + 2Fe

 dH = -1700 - (-840) = -860 kJ per mole rxn

 mass Fe2O3 = 2(56) + 3(16) = 160 g
 mass 2Al   = 2(27)         =  54 g
 total                      = 214 g

 860 / 214 = 4.02 kJ per gram  ->  4
⚡ JEE Trick "Per gram of the mixture" means the combined mass of every reactant, not just the limiting one. Dividing by 160 alone would give 5.4 and miss the answer.
Answer: 4 kJ per gram of mixture
5730.4 kJ of heat is required to melt one mole of sodium chloride and the entropy change at the melting point is 28.4 J K−1 mol−1 at 1 atm. The melting point of sodium chloride is ______ K. (Nearest integer) (JEE Main 2023, 15 April Shift 1, Level-4)Melting point from ΔHfus/ΔSfusThe change in entropy for the fusion of 1 mole of ice is : (m.pt. of ice =273 K, molar enthalpy ...🟡 Similar1070 K
Concept At the melting point solid and liquid coexist in equilibrium, so ΔGfus = 0 and the melting temperature is the ratio of the fusion enthalpy to the fusion entropy.
Key relation ΔGfus = 0 at Tm, so Tm = ΔHfus/ΔSfus The enthalpy must be converted to joules to match the entropy in J K−1 mol−1.
Solution
1. Data: ΔHfus = 30.4 kJ mol−1 and ΔSfus = 28.4 J K−1 mol−1 at 1 atm.
2. At the melting point the two phases are in equilibrium, so ΔG = ΔH − TΔS = 0.
3. Rearranging gives Tm = ΔHfus/ΔSfus.
4. Convert: ΔHfus = 30400 J mol−1.
5. Divide: Tm = 30400/28.4 = 1070.4 K, which to the nearest integer is 1070 K.
6. That matches the real melting point of sodium chloride, 1074 K (801 °C). The high value reflects the strong ionic lattice, and the modest fusion entropy of 28.4 J K−1 mol−1 is typical of ionic solids, where the melt retains considerable local order.
7. Contrast with a molecular solid: ice melts at 273 K with ΔHfus only 6.0 kJ mol−1. The five-fold larger fusion enthalpy of NaCl is what pushes its melting point so high.
 dH_fus = 30.4 kJ/mol = 30400 J/mol
 dS_fus = 28.4 J/K/mol

 at melting point : dG = 0
 Tm = 30400 / 28.4 = 1070.4 K

 real value for NaCl : 1074 K (801 C)
⚡ JEE Trick Melting point, boiling point and sublimation point are all ΔH/ΔS for the relevant transition. Only the kJ–to–J conversion can go wrong.
Answer: Tm = 1070 K
58For independent processes at 300 K: A (ΔH = −25 kJ mol−1, ΔS = −80 J K−1), B (−22, +40), C (+25, −50), D (+22, +20). The number of non-spontaneous processes from the following is ______. (JEE Main 2023, 24 Jan Shift 1, Level-3)Counting non-spontaneous processesWhat are the signs of `DeltaH^(@) and DeltaS^(@)` for a reaction that is spontaneous at all🟡 Similar2
Concept Each process is judged on the sign of ΔG at the stated 300 K. Four separate substitutions are needed, and the count of positive results is the answer.
Key relation ΔG = ΔH − TΔS; non-spontaneous means ΔG > 0 ΔH is in kJ and ΔS in J K−1, so one of them must be rescaled.
Solution
1. Process A (−25 kJ, −80 J K−1): ΔG = −25000 − 300(−80) = −25000 + 24000 = −1000 J. Negative, so spontaneous — but only just.
2. Process B (−22 kJ, +40 J K−1): ΔG = −22000 − 12000 = −34000 J. Both factors favourable, so firmly spontaneous.
3. Process C (+25 kJ, −50 J K−1): ΔG = 25000 + 15000 = +40000 J. Both factors unfavourable, so non-spontaneous at every temperature.
4. Process D (+22 kJ, +20 J K−1): ΔG = 22000 − 6000 = +16000 J. Positive, so non-spontaneous at 300 K.
5. Counting the positive values gives 2 non-spontaneous processes, namely C and D.
6. The two are not alike, though. C can never be made to go, since both signs oppose it; D would turn spontaneous above T = 22000/20 = 1100 K, because both its signs are positive.
 dG = dH - T dS   at T = 300 K

 A : -25000 + 24000 = -1000 J   spontaneous
 B : -22000 - 12000 = -34000 J  spontaneous
 C : +25000 + 15000 = +40000 J  NOT
 D : +22000 -  6000 = +16000 J  NOT
 ------------------------------------------
 count of non-spontaneous = 2

 D turns over above 22000/20 = 1100 K
⚡ JEE Trick Sort by signs before computing. Rows with ΔH > 0 and ΔS < 0 are non-spontaneous without any arithmetic; only the mixed-sign rows actually need the substitution.
Answer: 2 non-spontaneous processes (C and D)
59One mole of an ideal monatomic gas is subjected to changes 1→2→3→1 as shown on a P–V graph, with P = 1.0 bar at 1 and 2, V = 20 L at 1, V = 40 L at 2 and 3, and P = 0.5 bar at 3. The magnitude of the work done (by the system or on the system) is ______ J. (nearest integer) Given log 2 = 0.3, ln 10 = 2.3. (JEE Main 2023, 24 Jan Shift 2, Level-3)Net work around a P–V cycleCyclic Process in P–V Diagram | JEE Main Chemistry Thermodynamics Question | PYQ🟡 Similar|w| ≈ 620 J⚠
Concept A three-leg cycle in which the isobaric expansion does work on the surroundings and the isothermal compression returns part of it. The isochoric leg contributes nothing, so the net work is the difference between the other two.
Key relation Isobaric: w = −PΔV; isochoric: w = 0; isothermal: w = −nRT ln(Vf/Vi) with nRT = PV 1 bar·L = 100 J, so the whole cycle can be worked in bar·L and converted once at the end.
Solution
1. Corners: state 1 at (1.0 bar, 20 L), state 2 at (1.0 bar, 40 L) and state 3 at (0.5 bar, 40 L).
2. States 1 and 3 both have PV = 20 bar·L, so they lie on the same isotherm — which is why leg 3→1 is drawn as a curve.
3. Leg 1→2, isobaric expansion: w = −(1.0)(40 − 20) = −20 bar·L = −2000 J.
4. Leg 2→3, isochoric: the volume is fixed at 40 L, so w = 0.
5. Leg 3→1, isothermal compression with nRT = 20 bar·L: w = −20 ln(20/40) = +20 × 0.69 = +13.8 bar·L = +1380 J.
6. Net work: −2000 + 0 + 1380 = −620 J, so the magnitude is about 620 J, done by the system. The supplied log 2 = 0.3 and ln 10 = 2.3 combine to give ln 2 = 0.69, exactly the value needed.
 1(1.0 bar, 20 L)  2(1.0 bar, 40 L)  3(0.5, 40)
 states 1 and 3 : PV = 20 bar.L (same isotherm)

 1->2 isobaric  : -(1.0)(20) = -20 bar.L = -2000 J
 2->3 isochoric :                            0 J
 3->1 isothermal: +20 ln2 = +13.8 bar.L = +1380 J
 ------------------------------------------------
 net w = -620 J        |w| = 620 J
⚡ JEE Trick Spot the two states sharing the same PV product — they sit on one isotherm, which identifies the curved leg immediately and gives nRT for the logarithmic work in the same breath.
Answer: |w| ≈ 620 J  —  key prints 6 ⚠
60An athlete is given 100 g of glucose (C6H12O6) for energy. This is equivalent to 1800 kJ of energy. 50% of this energy gained is utilized by the athlete for sports activities. In order to avoid storage of energy, the weight of extra water he would need to perspire is ______ g. (Nearest integer) The enthalpy of evaporation of water is 45 kJ mol−1; molar masses of C, H and O are 12, 1 and 16 g mol−1. (JEE Main 2023, 25 Jan Shift 1, Level-3)Energy dissipated as sweatClass 11 Chap 6 | Thermodynamics 13 | Entropy Change in Phase Change in Equilibrium IIT JEE/NEET🟡 Similar360 g
Concept Half the energy from the glucose has to be shed to avoid being stored, and the body sheds it by evaporating sweat. Dividing the surplus energy by the molar enthalpy of evaporation gives the moles of water, and hence the mass.
Key relation mass = (energy to shed / ΔHevap) × M(H2O) M(H2O) = 2(1) + 16 = 18 g mol−1 from the atomic masses given.
Solution
1. The glucose supplies 1800 kJ, of which 50% is used for sports activity.
2. Energy that must be shed to avoid storage: 0.50 × 1800 = 900 kJ.
3. Each mole of water evaporated carries away 45 kJ, so the moles required are 900/45 = 20 mol.
4. Molar mass of water: 2(1) + 16 = 18 g mol−1.
5. Mass of water: 20 × 18 = 360 g.
6. The figure is physically sensible: 360 g of sweat is roughly a third of a litre, a realistic amount for a bout of exercise. Note that the 100 g mass of glucose and the molar masses of C and O are decoys — only the 1800 kJ figure is used.
7. Note also that the enthalpy of evaporation used here, 45 kJ mol−1, is the value at skin temperature rather than the 40.7 kJ mol−1 quoted at 100 °C — evaporating water below its boiling point costs slightly more.
8. Cross-check the arithmetic the other way: 360 g is 20 mol, and 20 × 45 = 900 kJ, exactly half the energy supplied.
 energy supplied      = 1800 kJ
 50% used, so shed    =  900 kJ

 dH_evap(water) = 45 kJ/mol
 moles = 900 / 45 = 20 mol

 M(H2O) = 18 g/mol
 mass = 20 x 18 = 360 g
⚡ JEE Trick Watch for unused data. The 100 g of glucose and the carbon and oxygen masses are there to tempt a molar-mass calculation that the question never needs.
Answer: 360 g of water
611 mole of ideal gas is allowed to expand reversibly and adiabatically from a temperature of 27°C. The work done is 3 kJ mol−1. The final temperature of the gas is ______ K. (Nearest integer) Given CV = 20 J mol−1 K−1. (JEE Main 2023, 30 Jan Shift 2, Level-3)Final T of a reversible adiabatic expansion13 REVERSIBLE AND IRREVERSIBLE ADIABATIC PROCESS | IIT ADVANCED | JEE MAIN | CHEMISTRY CLASS 11🟡 Similar150 K
Concept In an adiabatic change no heat is exchanged, so the work done by the gas comes entirely out of its internal energy. The temperature drop then follows directly from ΔU = nCVΔT.
Key relation q = 0 ⇒ ΔU = w; ΔU = nCV(T2 − T1) Work done by the gas is +3 kJ, so w in the ΔU = q + w convention is −3000 J.
Solution
1. Data: n = 1 mol, T1 = 27 °C = 300 K, CV = 20 J mol−1 K−1, and the gas does 3 kJ mol−1 of work.
2. Adiabatic means q = 0, so the first law reduces to ΔU = w.
3. The gas does the work, so in the sign convention used here w = −3000 J and therefore ΔU = −3000 J.
4. Substitute into ΔU = nCVΔT: −3000 = (1)(20)(T2 − 300).
5. Solve: T2 − 300 = −150, so T2 = 150 K.
6. The cooling is dramatic — the gas loses half its absolute temperature — but that is what a reversible adiabatic expansion doing 3 kJ of work must cost when CV is only 20 J K−1. This is the principle behind gas-expansion refrigeration.
7. Cross-check: the temperature drop is w/(nCV) = 3000/20 = 150 K, independent of the starting temperature. Only the final value depends on where the expansion began.
 adiabatic : q = 0   ->   dU = w
 gas does 3 kJ of work  ->  w = -3000 J

 dU = n Cv (T2 - T1)
 -3000 = (1)(20)(T2 - 300)
 T2 - 300 = -150
 T2 = 150 K
⚡ JEE Trick Fix the sign of w from the wording before substituting. "Work done by the gas is 3 kJ" means w = −3000 J; "work done on the gas" would mean +3000 J and the gas would warm instead.
Answer: T2 = 150 K
62At 25°C the enthalpies of the following processes are given: H2(g) + O2(g) → 2OH(g), ΔH° = 78 kJ mol−1; H2(g) + ½O2(g) → H2O(g), ΔH° = −242 kJ mol−1; H2(g) → 2H(g), ΔH° = 436 kJ mol−1; ½O2(g) → O(g), ΔH° = 249 kJ mol−1. What would be the value of X for H2O(g) → H(g) + OH(g), ΔH° = X kJ mol−1? (Nearest integer) (JEE Main 2023, 1 February Shift 1, Level-3)First O−H bond dissociation enthalpyTHERMOCHEMISTRY । Class 11 (L3) I Bond energy | Resonance energy | Born Haber Cycle | JEE | NEET🟡 SimilarX = 499 kJ mol<sup>−1</sup>
Concept The first O−H bond dissociation of water is built by Hess's law from four supplied processes. Reversing the formation of water and then splitting the elements into the required fragments assembles the target exactly.
Key relation Target = −(2) + ½(3) + ½(1) Half of equation (3) makes one H atom and half of equation (1) makes one OH radical.
Solution
1. Target: H2O(g) → H(g) + OH(g), with ΔH° = X.
2. Step one, reverse the formation of water: H2O(g) → H2(g) + ½O2(g) costs +242 kJ.
3. Step two, make one hydrogen atom: half of H2(g) → 2H(g) gives ½H2 → H at +218 kJ.
4. Step three, make one hydroxyl radical: half of H2 + O2 → 2OH gives ½H2 + ½O2 → OH at +39 kJ.
5. Adding the three steps cancels H2 and O2 and leaves exactly H2O → H + OH, with X = 242 + 218 + 39 = 499 kJ mol−1.
6. Note that 499 is larger than the average O−H bond enthalpy of 466 found in PYQ 12. Breaking the first bond of water costs more than the second, and 466 is the mean of the two — the fourth equation, ½O2 → O, is a decoy not needed here.
 H2O(g) -> H2 + 1/2 O2        : +242
 1/2 H2 -> H(g)               : +218  (436/2)
 1/2 H2 + 1/2 O2 -> OH(g)     :  +39  (78/2)
 --------------------------------------------
 H2O(g) -> H(g) + OH(g)       :  499 kJ/mol

 average O-H = 466 ; first bond = 499
⚡ JEE Trick Build the target from the right-hand side backwards: decide which fragments you need, then halve whichever equations produce two of them. Unused data, like the oxygen atomisation here, are deliberate decoys.
Answer: X = 499 kJ mol−1
63Consider the following data: heat of combustion of H2(g) = −241.8 kJ mol−1; heat of combustion of C(s) = −393.5 kJ mol−1; heat of combustion of C2H5OH(l) = −1234.7 kJ mol−1. The heat of formation of C2H5OH(l) is (−)______ kJ mol−1. (Nearest integer) (JEE Main 2023, 06 April Shift 2, Level-3)ΔfH of ethanolEnthalpy of Formation Reaction & Heat of Combustion, Enthalpy Change Problems Chemistry🟡 Similar278 kJ mol<sup>−1</sup> (Δ<sub>f</sub>H = −278)
Concept Three combustion enthalpies combine to give the formation enthalpy of ethanol. The route is the standard one: burn the elements in the right proportions, then un-burn the compound.
Key relation ΔfH = 2ΔcH(C) + 3ΔcH(H2) − ΔcH(C2H5OH) Ethanol contains two carbons and six hydrogens, hence the coefficients 2 and 3.
Solution
1. Target: 2C(s) + 3H2(g) + ½O2(g) → C2H5OH(l).
2. Burn two carbons: 2 × (−393.5) = −787.0 kJ.
3. Burn three hydrogens: 3 × (−241.8) = −725.4 kJ.
4. Reverse the combustion of ethanol: −(−1234.7) = +1234.7 kJ.
5. Add: ΔfH = −787.0 − 725.4 + 1234.7 = −277.7 kJ mol−1, so the magnitude required is 278.
6. One caution: the hydrogen combustion value of −241.8 kJ mol−1 corresponds to water vapour. Using the liquid-water value of −286 instead would give −410 kJ mol−1, so the phase quoted in the data must be carried through consistently.
7. The accepted formation enthalpy of liquid ethanol is −277.7 kJ mol−1, so the data used here reproduce the literature value exactly.
 2C(s) + 3H2(g) + 1/2 O2 -> C2H5OH(l)

 2 x (-393.5)  =  -787.0
 3 x (-241.8)  =  -725.4
 -(-1234.7)    = +1234.7
 --------------------------
 dfH           =  -277.7 kJ/mol

 magnitude asked for : 278
⚡ JEE Trick Count the atoms in the target molecule to set the coefficients: two carbons and six hydrogens means 2 and 3, never 2 and 6. The hydrogens come in pairs.
Answer: 278 (ΔfH = −277.7 kJ mol−1)
64The number of endothermic process/es from the following is ______.
(a) I2(g) → 2I(g)   (b) HCl(g) → H(g) + Cl(g)   (c) H2O(l) → H2O(g)   (d) C(s) + O2(g) → CO2(g)   (e) Dissolution of ammonium chloride in water   (JEE Main 2023, 10 April Shift 2, Level-3)		Counting endothermic changesHow to Predict Sign of Delta S (Entropy Change) Practice Problems, Examples, Rules, Summary🟡 Similar4
Concept Endothermic means energy has to be put in. Breaking bonds, separating molecules into a gas and pulling an ionic lattice apart all cost energy; only forming strong new bonds releases it.
Key relation Bond breaking, vaporisation and most dissolutions of ammonium salts are endothermic; combustion is exothermic The sign follows from whether bonds are net broken or net formed.
Solution
1. (a) I2(g) → 2I(g) breaks the I−I bond, costing about 151 kJ mol−1. Endothermic.
2. (b) HCl(g) → H(g) + Cl(g) breaks the H−Cl bond, costing about 431 kJ mol−1. Endothermic.
3. (c) H2O(l) → H2O(g) is vaporisation, which must overcome hydrogen bonding at a cost of about 44 kJ mol−1. Endothermic.
4. (d) C(s) + O2(g) → CO2(g) is combustion, releasing 393.5 kJ mol−1. Exothermic — the only one.
5. (e) Dissolving ammonium chloride absorbs heat: its lattice energy slightly exceeds the hydration energy, which is why the beaker feels cold. Endothermic.
6. Four of the five are endothermic, so the answer is 4. Note that (e) is spontaneous nonetheless — driven by the entropy of dispersing the ions, a reminder that endothermic does not mean impossible.
 (a) I2(g) -> 2I(g)        bond broken   ENDO
 (b) HCl(g) -> H + Cl      bond broken   ENDO
 (c) H2O(l) -> H2O(g)      vaporisation  ENDO
 (d) C + O2 -> CO2         combustion    EXO
 (e) NH4Cl dissolving      lattice > hyd ENDO
 -----------------------------------------------
 count of endothermic = 4
⚡ JEE Trick Any process written as a molecule splitting into fragments is endothermic without exception. Only combinations — combustion, neutralisation, bond formation — give out heat.
Answer: 4 endothermic processes
65What happens when methane undergoes combustion in systems A (adiabatic) and B (diathermic container) respectively?
(a) System A → Temperature rises, System B → Temperature remains same   (b) System A → Temperature remains same, System B → Temperature rises   (c) System A → Temperature falls, System B → Temperature falls   (d) System A → Temperature falls, System B → Temperature rises   (JEE Main 2023, 13 April Shift 2, Level-3)		Adiabatic vs diathermic combustionFirst Law, Second Law, Third Law, Zeroth Law of Thermodynamics🟡 Similara
Concept The same exothermic reaction is run in two different containers. An adiabatic wall traps the heat inside, so the contents warm; a diathermic wall lets it escape to the surroundings, so the contents stay at ambient temperature.
Key relation Adiabatic: q = 0, so the released energy stays as internal energy and T rises; diathermic: heat flows out and T is held constant Combustion of methane is strongly exothermic, releasing about 890 kJ mol−1.
Solution
1. Methane combustion, CH4 + 2O2 → CO2 + 2H2O, is strongly exothermic, so chemical energy is converted into thermal energy in both systems.
2. System A is adiabatic. No heat can cross the boundary, so all the released energy remains inside and raises the internal energy. The temperature rises.
3. System B is a diathermic container. Heat passes freely through the walls, so the released energy flows out into the surroundings.
4. With the heat escaping, the contents of B return to the temperature of the surroundings, so the temperature remains the same.
5. That pairing — A rises, B unchanged — is option (a).
6. Neither temperature can fall, since the reaction is exothermic in both cases; that alone eliminates options (c) and (d). Option (b) reverses the roles of the two walls.
 CH4 + 2O2 -> CO2 + 2H2O    strongly exothermic

 A adiabatic  : q = 0, energy trapped -> T rises
 B diathermic : heat escapes          -> T same

 neither can cool: reaction gives out heat
⚡ JEE Trick Adiabatic walls keep heat in and let the temperature move; diathermic walls let heat out and hold the temperature fixed. Match the wall to the outcome and the option follows.
Answer: (a) System A → temperature rises, System B → temperature remains same
66A2 + B2 → 2AB, ΔHf = −200 kJ mol−1. AB, A2 and B2 are diatomic molecules. If the bond enthalpies of A2, B2 and AB are in the ratio 1 : 0.5 : 1, then the bond enthalpy of A2 is ______ kJ mol−1. (Nearest integer) (JEE Main 2023, 13 April Shift 1, Level-3)Bond enthalpies in a fixed ratioTHERMOCHEMISTRY । Class 11 (L3) I Bond energy | Resonance energy | Born Haber Cycle | JEE | NEET🟡 Similar400 kJ mol<sup>−1</sup>
Concept All three bond enthalpies are expressed in terms of one unknown through the given ratio, so the bond-energy expression for ΔH collapses to a single equation in that unknown.
Key relation ΔH = ΣBE(broken) − ΣBE(formed) = [BE(A2) + BE(B2)] − 2BE(AB) The ratio 1 : 0.5 : 1 lets every term be written as a multiple of BE(A2).
Solution
1. Let BE(A2) = a. The stated ratio 1 : 0.5 : 1 then gives BE(B2) = 0.5a and BE(AB) = a.
2. Reaction: A2 + B2 → 2AB. Bonds broken are one A−A and one B−B; bonds formed are two A−B.
3. Substitute: ΔH = (a + 0.5a) − 2a = 1.5a − 2a = −0.5a.
4. Set this equal to the given formation enthalpy: −0.5a = −200 kJ mol−1.
5. Solve: a = 400 kJ mol−1, which is the bond enthalpy of A2.
6. Check the consistency: BE(B2) = 200 and BE(AB) = 400, so breaking costs 600 and forming returns 800, a net release of 200 kJ — exactly the quoted ΔHf. The reaction is exothermic because the A−B bond is much stronger than the weak B−B bond it replaces.
7. Note the ratio does the work here: had all three bonds been equal, ΔH would have come out as (a + a) − 2a = 0, and no bond enthalpy could have been extracted at all.
 BE(A2) = a , BE(B2) = 0.5a , BE(AB) = a

 A2 + B2 -> 2AB
 dH = (a + 0.5a) - 2a = -0.5a

 -0.5a = -200   ->   a = 400 kJ/mol

 check: broken 400+200=600, formed 2(400)=800
        net = -200 kJ    OK
⚡ JEE Trick When bond energies are given only as a ratio, name the smallest one and express the rest in terms of it. The unknown always cancels down to a single linear equation.
Answer: BE(A2) = 400 kJ mol−1
67Consider the following reaction approaching equilibrium at 27°C and 1 atm pressure: A + B ⇌ C + D with Kf = 103 and Kr = 102. The standard Gibbs energy change (ΔrG°) at 27°C is (−)______ kJ mol−1. (Nearest integer) Given R = 8.3 J K−1 mol−1 and ln 10 = 2.3. (JEE Main 2023, 29 Jan Shift 1, Level-2)ΔG° from forward/reverse rate constantsLast Relation between delta G and K | Gibbs energy and equilibrium constant relation mathematically🟡 Similar≈ 6 kJ mol<sup>−1</sup> (Δ<sub>r</sub>G° = −5.7)⚠
Concept The equilibrium constant of a reaction is the ratio of its forward and reverse rate constants. Once K is known, the van't Hoff isotherm converts it straight into the standard free energy change.
Key relation K = kf/kr; ΔrG° = −RT ln K At equilibrium the forward and reverse rates are equal, which is exactly what makes K the ratio of the two constants.
Solution
1. Given kf = 103 and kr = 102 for A + B ⇌ C + D at 27 °C = 300 K.
2. Equilibrium constant: K = kf/kr = 103/102 = 10.
3. Take logarithms: ln K = ln 10 = 2.3, as the question supplies.
4. Apply the isotherm: ΔrG° = −RT ln K = −(8.3)(300)(2.3).
5. Evaluate: 8.3 × 300 = 2490, and 2490 × 2.3 = 5727 J, so ΔrG° = −5727 J mol−1 = −5.7 kJ mol−1.
6. Written in the requested (−)______ kJ mol−1 form and rounded, the answer is 6. The key's 11 would require K = 100 rather than 10; a modest K of 10 corresponds to a modest free-energy drop, consistent with the 5.7 kJ per decade benchmark.
 K = kf/kr = 10^3 / 10^2 = 10
 ln K = 2.3     T = 300 K     R = 8.3

 dG0 = -(8.3)(300)(2.3) = -5727 J
     = -5.7 kJ/mol   ->  nearest integer 6

 11 kJ/mol would need K = 100
⚡ JEE Trick K is the ratio of rate constants, not their product. Multiplying 103 by 102 gives K = 105 and a wildly different free energy — a common slip.
Answer: 6 (ΔrG° = −5.7 kJ mol−1)  —  key prints 11 ⚠
68Enthalpies of formation of CCl4(g), H2O(g), CO2(g) and HCl(g) are −105, −242, −394 and −92 kJ mol−1 respectively. The magnitude of enthalpy of the reaction CCl4(g) + 2H2O(g) → CO2(g) + 4HCl(g) is ______ kJ mol−1. (nearest integer) (JEE Main 2023, 31 Jan Shift 2, Level-2)ΔrH from formation enthalpiesHess's Law Problems & Enthalpy Change - Chemistry🟡 Similar173 kJ mol<sup>−1</sup> (ΔH = −173)
Concept A straightforward products-minus-reactants sum using tabulated formation enthalpies. The only care needed is applying the stoichiometric coefficients, especially the 2 on water and the 4 on hydrogen chloride.
Key relation ΔrH = ΣnΔfH(products) − ΣnΔfH(reactants) Every species here is a compound, so none of the terms drops out.
Solution
1. Reaction: CCl4(g) + 2H2O(g) → CO2(g) + 4HCl(g).
2. Products: CO2 contributes −394 and 4HCl contributes 4 × (−92) = −368, giving a total of −762 kJ.
3. Reactants: CCl4 contributes −105 and 2H2O contributes 2 × (−242) = −484, giving a total of −589 kJ.
4. Subtract: ΔrH = −762 − (−589) = −762 + 589 = −173 kJ mol−1.
5. The magnitude asked for is therefore 173 kJ mol−1.
6. Check the mole balance before trusting the arithmetic: one carbon on each side, four chlorines on each side, and four hydrogens on each side. A mis-balanced equation is the usual reason these sums go wrong.
7. The reaction is exothermic overall because four strong H−Cl bonds and two C=O bonds are formed at the expense of four C−Cl and four O−H bonds.
 CCl4(g) + 2H2O(g) -> CO2(g) + 4HCl(g)

 products : -394 + 4(-92) = -394 - 368 = -762
 reactants: -105 + 2(-242) = -105 - 484 = -589
 -------------------------------------------
 drH = -762 + 589 = -173 kJ/mol

 magnitude : 173
⚡ JEE Trick Check the equation balances before summing. Here the 4 on HCl is easy to overlook, and using 2 instead would shift the answer by 184 kJ.
Answer: 173 kJ mol−1 (ΔrH = −173)
69When a 60 W electric heater is immersed in a gas for 100 s in a constant volume container with adiabatic walls, the temperature of the gas rises by 5°C. The heat capacity of the given gas is ______ J K−1. (Nearest integer) (JEE Main 2023, 08 April Shift 1, Level-2)Heat capacity from electrical heatingMeasurements of ∆U and ∆H || Calorimetery || Thermodynamics Part - 5 || Class-11 | Chemistry🟡 Similar1200 J K<sup>−1</sup>
Concept An electric heater delivers a known quantity of energy, and with adiabatic walls and a fixed volume all of it goes into raising the temperature of the gas. Heat capacity is then simply energy divided by temperature rise.
Key relation q = P × t; C = q/ΔT Constant volume means no expansion work, so every joule supplied becomes internal energy.
Solution
1. Energy delivered: a 60 W heater running for 100 s supplies q = 60 × 100 = 6000 J.
2. The container has adiabatic walls, so none of that energy leaks to the surroundings.
3. The volume is constant, so no expansion work is done and w = 0. Hence ΔU = q = 6000 J.
4. The temperature rises by 5 °C, which is a rise of 5 K since only a difference is involved.
5. Heat capacity: C = q/ΔT = 6000/5 = 1200 J K−1.
6. Note this is the heat capacity of the whole sample at constant volume, not a molar quantity: no amount of gas is given, so CV,m cannot be extracted. Had the container been free to expand, part of the 6000 J would have gone into work and the apparent capacity would have been larger.
7. As a scale check, 1200 J K−1 is roughly 96 times the molar CV of a monatomic gas, so the vessel holds on the order of a hundred moles — or rather less if the gas is polyatomic.
8. The word "adiabatic" matters: with conducting walls some of the 6000 J would have leaked away and the measured rise would have been smaller than 5 K.
 q = 60 W x 100 s = 6000 J
 adiabatic walls -> no loss
 constant volume -> w = 0, so dU = q

 dT = 5 C = 5 K
 C = 6000 / 5 = 1200 J/K

 this is C_V for the whole sample
⚡ JEE Trick A temperature rise of 5 °C equals a rise of 5 K, so no conversion is needed. Only absolute temperatures require the 273 shift.
Answer: heat capacity = 1200 J K−1
70One mole of an ideal gas at 350 K is in a 2.0 L vessel of thermally conducting walls in contact with the surroundings. It undergoes isothermal reversible expansion from 2.0 L to 3.0 L against a constant pressure of 4 atm. The change in entropy of the surroundings (ΔS) is ______ J K−1. (Nearest integer) Given R = 8.314 J K−1 mol−1. (JEE Main 2023, 12 April Shift 1, Level-2)ΔS of the surroundingsClass 11 chapter 6 |Thermodynamics 11 | Entropy of different process | How to find Entropy JEE MAINS🟡 Similar−3 J K<sup>−1</sup> (magnitude 3)
Concept The surroundings receive the mirror image of the heat the system absorbs, and because the walls are thermally conducting the exchange happens at the fixed temperature of 350 K. Since the expansion is described as reversible, the heat is the full logarithmic value.
Key relation ΔSsurr = −qsys/T = −nR ln(V2/V1) For a reversible isothermal change the surroundings lose exactly the entropy the system gains, so ΔStotal = 0.
Solution
1. The gas expands isothermally and reversibly from 2.0 L to 3.0 L at 350 K, with n = 1 mol.
2. Being isothermal and ideal, ΔU = 0, so the heat absorbed is q = −w = nRT ln(V2/V1).
3. Evaluate: q = (1)(8.314)(350) ln(1.5) = 2910 × 0.4055 = +1180 J absorbed by the gas.
4. The surroundings supply that heat at the same 350 K, so ΔSsurr = −1180/350 = −3.37 J K−1.
5. To the nearest integer the magnitude is 3 J K−1, and the sign is negative because the surroundings lose heat.
6. Two remarks. The system gains exactly +3.37 J K−1, so ΔStotal = 0 as a reversible change requires. And the "constant pressure of 4 atm" in the stem sits oddly with "reversible" — a genuinely irreversible expansion against 4 atm would give only q = 405 J and ΔSsurr = −1.2 J K−1.
 1 mol, 350 K, 2.0 L -> 3.0 L, reversible

 q = nRT ln(3/2) = (8.314)(350)(0.4055) = 1180 J
 dS_surr = -1180/350 = -3.37 J/K   ->  3

 dS_sys = +3.37 -> dS_total = 0 (reversible)

 if irreversible vs 4 atm: q = 405 J, dS = -1.2
⚡ JEE Trick The surroundings always take the negative of the system's heat at their own temperature. For a reversible change the two entropy changes cancel exactly, which is a useful check on the arithmetic.
Answer: ΔSsurr ≈ −3 J K−1
71Assertion A: In the Ellingham diagram, a sharp change in slope of the line is observed from Mg → MgO at ~1120°C. Reason R: There is a large change of entropy associated with the change of state.
(a) Both A and R are true but R is NOT the correct explanation of A   (b) A is false but R is true   (c) Both A and R are true and R is the correct explanation of A   (d) A is true but R is false   (JEE Main 2023, 12 April Shift 1, Level-2)		Ellingham kink at a boiling pointIn Ellingham diagram a sudden increase in slope indicates (1) Only vapourisation can take place ...🟢 Exactc
Concept An Ellingham line plots ΔG° against temperature, so its slope is −ΔS°. A sudden kink means ΔS° has changed abruptly, which happens exactly when one of the species changes phase.
Key relation ΔG° = ΔH° − TΔS°, so slope = −ΔS° Magnesium boils at about 1090 °C, so above that the reaction consumes gaseous rather than liquid magnesium.
Solution
1. Assertion. The Mg → MgO line does show a sharp change of slope near 1120 °C. This is a well-known feature of the Ellingham diagram, so A is true.
2. Reason. A change of state involves a large entropy change — vaporisation typically contributes about 85–110 J K−1 mol−1. R is true.
3. Do they connect? The slope of an Ellingham line is −ΔS° for the oxidation. Below the boiling point the reaction is 2Mg(l) + O2 → 2MgO(s), consuming one mole of gas.
4. Above about 1090 °C the magnesium is a vapour, so the reaction becomes 2Mg(g) + O2 → 2MgO(s) and now consumes three moles of gas instead of one.
5. That makes ΔS° far more negative, so the slope −ΔS° jumps to a much steeper positive value — precisely the kink described in the assertion.
6. The reason therefore explains the assertion, giving option (c). The same kink appears wherever a metal melts or boils on an Ellingham plot, which is why the diagram is drawn with these breaks marked.
 Ellingham : dG0 = dH0 - T dS0, slope = -dS0

 below 1090 C : 2Mg(l) + O2 -> 2MgO(s)
                one mole of gas consumed
 above 1090 C : 2Mg(g) + O2 -> 2MgO(s)
                three moles of gas consumed

 dS0 much more negative -> slope much steeper
⚡ JEE Trick Every kink in an Ellingham line marks a melting or boiling point. Count the moles of gas on each side of the transition and the direction of the slope change follows at once.
Answer: (c) Both A and R are true and R is the correct explanation of A
72The total number of intensive properties from the following is ______: Volume, Molar heat capacity, molarity, E°cell, Gibbs free energy change, Molar mass, Mole. (JEE Main 2023, 11 April Shift 2, Level-1)Counting intensive properties#2- Extensive Properties | Intensive Properties | State Function | Path Function | Thermodynamics🟢 Exact4
Concept An intensive property is independent of how much material is present; an extensive one scales with the amount. The quickest test is to imagine halving the sample and asking whether the number changes.
Key relation Extensive: V, n, mass, U, H, G, S  |  Intensive: T, P, density, any "molar" or "per unit" quantity, and E°cell Dividing an extensive property by the amount always produces an intensive one.
Solution
1. Volume doubles if the sample doubles, so it is extensive.
2. Molar heat capacity is a per-mole quantity, unchanged by sample size: intensive.
3. Molarity is moles per litre; halving the solution halves both, so the ratio is unchanged: intensive.
4. E°cell does not depend on how large the electrodes are — this is why cell potentials are not multiplied when an equation is doubled: intensive.
5. Gibbs free energy change scales with the amount reacting, so it is extensive; molar mass is a per-mole quantity and so intensive; and mole is a direct count of matter, hence extensive.
6. Counting the intensive entries — molar heat capacity, molarity, E°cell and molar mass — gives 4.
 Volume                 extensive
 Molar heat capacity    INTENSIVE
 Molarity               INTENSIVE
 E0(cell)               INTENSIVE
 Gibbs free energy dG   extensive
 Molar mass             INTENSIVE
 Mole                   extensive
 -----------------------------------
 count of intensive = 4
⚡ JEE Trick The word "molar" is an automatic marker for intensive. E°cell is the one that catches people out — it is intensive even though ΔG = −nFE° certainly is not.
Answer: 4 intensive properties
73For complete combustion of methanol, CH3OH(l) + (3/2)O2(g) → CO2(g) + 2H2O(l), the amount of heat produced as measured by bomb calorimeter is 726 kJ mol−1 at 27°C. The enthalpy of combustion for the reaction is −x kJ mol−1, where x is an integer. (R = 8.3 J K−1 mol−1) (JEE Main 2022, 26 Jun Shift 1, Level-6)ΔH from bomb-calorimeter ΔUthe amount of heat produced as measured by bomb calorimeter | 26 June 2022 |Jee Main PYQ🟢 Exactx = 727 kJ mol<sup>−1</sup>
Concept A bomb calorimeter works at constant volume and therefore measures ΔU. Converting to the enthalpy of combustion needs the gas-mole correction, and here the reaction consumes more gas than it makes.
Key relation ΔH = ΔU + ΔngRT Liquid methanol and liquid water contribute nothing to Δng.
Solution
1. Reaction: CH3OH(l) + (3/2)O2(g) → CO2(g) + 2H2O(l), with ΔU = −726 kJ mol−1 at 27 °C.
2. Count the gases: one mole of CO2 is produced against 1.5 moles of O2 consumed, so Δng = 1 − 1.5 = −0.5.
3. Correction term: ΔngRT = (−0.5)(8.3)(300) = −1245 J = −1.245 kJ.
4. Substitute: ΔH = −726 − 1.245 = −727.2 kJ mol−1.
5. Since the answer is written as −x kJ mol−1 with x an integer, x = 727.
6. The direction is right: the system contracts as gas is consumed, so the surroundings do work on it and slightly more energy appears as heat at constant pressure than at constant volume.
7. The correction is tiny here — 1.2 kJ against 726 kJ, about 0.17% — which is typical for combustion reactions where Δng is small compared with the energy released.
 CH3OH(l) + 3/2 O2 -> CO2(g) + 2H2O(l)

 gases : 1 out, 1.5 in  ->  dn_g = -0.5
 dn_g R T = -0.5(8.3)(300) = -1245 J

 dH = -726 - 1.245 = -727.2 kJ/mol
 x = 727
⚡ JEE Trick A negative Δng makes ΔH more exothermic than ΔU; a positive one makes it less. Decide the direction before substituting and the sign takes care of itself.
Answer: x = 727 (ΔH = −727.2 kJ mol−1)
74When 5 moles of He gas expand isothermally and reversibly at 300 K from 10 litre to 20 litre, the magnitude of the maximum work obtained is ______ J. [nearest integer] (R = 8.3 J K−1 mol−1, log 2 = 0.3010) (JEE Main 2022, 27 Jun Shift 2, Level-6)Maximum work on doubling volumeThermodynamics || Calculation of W, q, U, H for reversible isothermal expansion process🟡 Similar8630 J
Concept Maximum work from a gas expansion is obtained along the reversible isothermal path, given by the logarithmic formula. A doubling of the volume makes the logarithm ln 2, supplied here through log 2 = 0.3010.
Key relation |wmax| = nRT ln(V2/V1) = 2.303 nRT log(V2/V1) Only the reversible path attains this maximum; any irreversible route yields less.
Solution
1. Data: n = 5 mol of helium, T = 300 K, V1 = 10 L, V2 = 20 L, R = 8.3 J K−1 mol−1.
2. Volume ratio: V2/V1 = 2, so log(V2/V1) = 0.3010.
3. Build the prefactor: nRT = 5 × 8.3 × 300 = 12450 J.
4. Include the 2.303: 2.303 × 0.3010 = 0.693, which is ln 2 as expected.
5. Multiply: |w| = 12450 × 0.693 = 8628 J ≈ 8630 J.
6. Because the change is isothermal and helium is effectively ideal, ΔU = 0 and the same 8630 J must be absorbed as heat from the surroundings. Note that the monatomic nature of helium is irrelevant here — CV never enters an isothermal calculation.
7. For comparison, an irreversible expansion against the final pressure would give |w| = nRT(1 − V1/V2) = 12450 × 0.5 = 6225 J, about 28% less — a concrete measure of what irreversibility costs.
 n = 5 mol   T = 300 K   10 L -> 20 L

 nRT = 5(8.3)(300) = 12450 J
 ln2 = 2.303 x 0.3010 = 0.693

 |w| = 12450 x 0.693 = 8628 J  ~ 8630 J
 q = +8630 J  (dU = 0)
⚡ JEE Trick "Maximum work" is always the reversible value with the logarithm. If a question offers a constant external pressure instead, the answer is smaller and uses pΔV.
Answer: |wmax| = 8630 J
752.2 g of nitrous oxide (N2O) gas is cooled at a constant pressure of 1 atm from 310 K to 270 K causing the compression of the gas from 217.1 mL to 167.75 mL. The change in internal energy of the process, ΔU is '−x' J. The value of |x| is ______. [nearest integer] (Atomic mass of N = 14 g mol−1, O = 16 g mol−1; molar heat capacity of N2O is 100 J K−1 mol−1) (JEE Main 2022, 29 Jun Shift 2, Level-6)ΔU on cooling with compressionMeasurements of ∆U and ∆H || Calorimetery || Thermodynamics Part - 5 || Class-11 | Chemistry🟡 Similar|x| = 195 J (ΔU = −195 J)⚠
Concept Cooling at constant pressure gives the enthalpy change directly from nCPΔT. Because the gas also contracts, the surroundings do a little work on it, and that work must be added back to reach the internal energy change.
Key relation ΔH = nCP,mΔT; ΔU = ΔH − PΔV 1 L·atm = 101.3 J, so the small volume change converts to only a few joules.
Solution
1. Molar mass of N2O = 2(14) + 16 = 44 g mol−1, so n = 2.2/44 = 0.05 mol.
2. Temperature change: ΔT = 270 − 310 = −40 K.
3. Enthalpy change: ΔH = nCP,mΔT = 0.05 × 100 × (−40) = −200 J.
4. Volume change: ΔV = 167.75 − 217.1 = −49.35 mL = −0.04935 L, so PΔV = (1 atm)(−0.04935 L) = −0.04935 L·atm = −5.0 J.
5. Substitute: ΔU = ΔH − PΔV = −200 − (−5.0) = −195 J.
6. So |x| = 195. The sign pattern is right: the gas loses 200 J as heat but recovers 5 J because the surroundings compress it, leaving a net 195 J drop in internal energy.
7. The same figure follows from ΔngRT reasoning: nRΔT = 0.05 × 8.314 × (−40) = −16.6 J. The small mismatch with −5.0 J shows the quoted volumes are not exactly ideal-gas values, so the explicit PΔV is the safer route.
 M(N2O) = 44 ; n = 2.2/44 = 0.05 mol
 dT = 270 - 310 = -40 K

 dH = 0.05 x 100 x (-40) = -200 J

 dV = 167.75 - 217.1 = -49.35 mL
 P dV = -0.04935 L.atm = -5.0 J

 dU = -200 - (-5.0) = -195 J   ->  |x| = 195
⚡ JEE Trick When both volumes are given explicitly, use PΔV directly rather than ΔngRT. The two agree, but the explicit volumes avoid any assumption about ideality.
Answer: |x| = 195 (ΔU = −195 J)  —  key prints 72 ⚠
76While performing a thermodynamics experiment, a student made the following observations: HCl + NaOH → NaCl + H2O, ΔH = −57.3 kJ mol−1; CH3COOH + NaOH → CH3COONa + H2O, ΔH = −55.3 kJ mol−1. The enthalpy of ionization of CH3COOH as calculated by the student is ______ kJ mol−1. (JEE Main 2022, 25 Jul Shift 2, Level-6)Enthalpy of ionisation of a weak acidVinay Sir - Thermodynamics - Enthalpy of Ionisation of Weak Acid🟢 Exact+2 kJ mol<sup>−1</sup>
Concept A strong acid is already fully ionised, so its neutralisation releases the full −57.3 kJ mol−1. A weak acid must ionise first, and that step absorbs energy, so it releases less. The shortfall is exactly the enthalpy of ionisation.
Key relation ΔHionisation = ΔHneut(weak) − ΔHneut(strong) The weak-acid neutralisation is the sum of ionisation plus the standard H+ + OH− step.
Solution
1. Strong acid with strong base: HCl + NaOH gives ΔH = −57.3 kJ mol−1, the pure H+ + OH− → H2O step.
2. Weak acid with strong base: CH3COOH + NaOH gives only −55.3 kJ mol−1.
3. Break the weak-acid case into two steps: first CH3COOH → CH3COO− + H+ with ΔHion, then H+ + OH− → H2O with −57.3 kJ.
4. So −55.3 = ΔHion + (−57.3).
5. Solve: ΔHion = −55.3 + 57.3 = +2.0 kJ mol−1.
6. The positive sign is right: ionising a weak acid costs energy, which is why less heat emerges overall. The value is small because acetic acid, though weak, is not far from ionising freely in dilute solution.
 strong : H+ + OH- -> H2O        -57.3 kJ
 weak   : CH3COOH + NaOH         -55.3 kJ

 weak = ionisation + (H+ + OH-)
 -55.3 = dH_ion - 57.3

 dH_ion = +2.0 kJ/mol
⚡ JEE Trick The shortfall in heat is always the ionisation enthalpy. Subtract the weak-acid value from the strong-acid value with signs intact and the positive answer appears automatically.
Answer: ΔHionisation = +2 kJ mol−1
77For the reaction H2F2(g) → H2(g) + F2(g), ΔU = −59.6 kJ mol−1 at 27°C. The enthalpy change for the above reaction is (−)______ kJ mol−1. (nearest integer) (R = 8.314 J K−1 mol−1) (JEE Main 2022, 26 Jul Shift 2, Level-6)ΔH from ΔU with Δng = 1Measurements of ∆U and ∆H || Calorimetery || Thermodynamics Part - 5 || Class-11 | Chemistry🟡 Similar57 kJ mol<sup>−1</sup> (ΔH = −57.1)
Concept One mole of gas becomes two, so Δng is positive and the enthalpy change is less exothermic than the internal energy change. The system expands and spends some of the released energy pushing the surroundings back.
Key relation ΔH = ΔU + ΔngRT All species are gases here, so Δng is read straight off the coefficients.
Solution
1. Reaction: H2F2(g) → H2(g) + F2(g), with ΔU = −59.6 kJ mol−1 at 27 °C = 300 K.
2. Count the gases: two moles produced from one, so Δng = 2 − 1 = +1.
3. Correction term: ΔngRT = (1)(8.314)(300) = +2494 J = +2.494 kJ.
4. Substitute: ΔH = −59.6 + 2.494 = −57.1 kJ mol−1.
5. Written in the requested (−)______ form and rounded, the answer is 57.
6. Direction check: a positive Δng always makes ΔH less negative than ΔU, because the expanding gas does work that is not available as heat. The shift of 2.5 kJ is exactly RT at 300 K.
7. Note the reaction is written as a dissociation yet is exothermic, which is unusual; H2F2 is a hydrogen-bonded dimer, so splitting it releases rather than consumes energy.
 H2F2(g) -> H2(g) + F2(g)

 gases : 2 out, 1 in  ->  dn_g = +1
 dn_g R T = (1)(8.314)(300) = 2494 J

 dH = -59.6 + 2.494 = -57.1 kJ/mol
 answer to fill : 57
⚡ JEE Trick Positive Δng pushes ΔH upward, negative Δng pushes it downward. Deciding that direction first turns the sign of the correction into a check rather than a guess.
Answer: 57 (ΔH = −57.1 kJ mol−1)
78The molar heat capacity for an ideal gas at constant pressure is 20.785 J K−1 mol−1. The change in internal energy is 5000 J upon heating it from 300 K to 500 K. The number of moles of the gas at constant volume is ______. (R = 8.314 J K−1 mol−1) (JEE Main 2022, 27 July Shift 1, Level-6)Moles from ΔU = nCVΔTMeasurements of ∆U and ∆H || Calorimetery || Thermodynamics Part - 5 || Class-11 | Chemistry🟡 Similar2 mol
Concept The internal energy change of an ideal gas always follows nCVΔT, whatever the path. Since CP is supplied rather than CV, the Mayer relation must be applied first.
Key relation CV,m = CP,m − R; ΔU = nCV,mΔT The phrase "at constant volume" signals that CV, not CP, governs ΔU.
Solution
1. Data: CP,m = 20.785 J K−1 mol−1, ΔU = 5000 J, heating from 300 K to 500 K, R = 8.314 J K−1 mol−1.
2. Mayer relation: CV,m = 20.785 − 8.314 = 12.471 J K−1 mol−1.
3. Note that 12.471 is exactly (3/2)R, so the gas is monatomic — a useful confirmation that the data are self-consistent.
4. Temperature change: ΔT = 500 − 300 = 200 K.
5. Rearrange ΔU = nCV,mΔT: n = 5000/(12.471 × 200) = 5000/2494.2.
6. Evaluate: n = 2.005 ≈ 2 mol. Using CP instead of CV would have given 1.2 mol, so applying the Mayer relation is the whole substance of the question.
7. Cross-check the other way: 2 mol of a monatomic gas heated 200 K stores 2 × 12.471 × 200 = 4988 J, matching the quoted 5000 J to rounding.
 Cp,m = 20.785 J/K/mol
 Cv,m = 20.785 - 8.314 = 12.471 = (3/2)R

 dT = 500 - 300 = 200 K
 dU = n Cv,m dT
 5000 = n (12.471)(200) = n (2494.2)

 n = 2.005 mol  ->  2 mol
⚡ JEE Trick ΔU always takes CV and ΔH always takes CP, regardless of the actual path. If the question hands you the wrong one, CP − CV = R is the bridge.
Answer: n = 2 mol
79A gas (molar mass = 280 g mol−1) was burnt in excess O2 in a constant volume calorimeter and during combustion the temperature of calorimeter increased from 298.0 K to 298.45 K. If the heat capacity of calorimeter is 2.5 kJ K−1 and enthalpy of combustion of gas is 9 kJ mol−1, then amount of gas burnt is ______ g. (JEE Main 2022, 27 Jul Shift 2, Level-6)Mass burnt from a calorimeter riseIIT JEE Chemistry Bomb Calorimeter & Tricks to solve Bomb Calorimeter questions easily by NV sir🟡 Similar35 g
Concept A constant-volume calorimeter absorbs the heat released by the burning sample, and its temperature rise measures that heat. Dividing by the molar enthalpy of combustion gives the moles burnt, and the molar mass then gives the mass.
Key relation q = CcalΔT; n = q/|ΔHcomb|; mass = nM The calorimeter's heat capacity is quoted for the whole assembly, so no mass of water is needed.
Solution
1. Temperature rise: ΔT = 298.45 − 298.0 = 0.45 K.
2. Heat absorbed by the calorimeter: q = CcalΔT = 2.5 × 0.45 = 1.125 kJ. This is the heat released by the burning gas.
3. Moles burnt: n = 1.125/9 = 0.125 mol, using the enthalpy of combustion of 9 kJ mol−1.
4. Mass burnt: m = nM = 0.125 × 280 = 35 g.
5. Sense check: 0.125 mol is one eighth of a mole, and one eighth of 280 g is indeed 35 g.
6. Note the unusually small enthalpy of combustion, 9 kJ mol−1, which is why such a large mass produces so modest a temperature rise. In a real calorimetry experiment the figure would be hundreds of kilojoules per mole.
7. Note too that a bomb calorimeter measures at constant volume, so strictly the 9 kJ mol−1 quoted as an enthalpy is really ΔU; with no Δng given, the distinction cannot be pursued further.
8. The molar mass of 280 g mol−1 is large for a gas, but it plays no part until the final multiplication.
 dT = 298.45 - 298.0 = 0.45 K
 q = C_cal dT = 2.5 x 0.45 = 1.125 kJ

 n = 1.125 / 9 = 0.125 mol
 m = 0.125 x 280 = 35 g
⚡ JEE Trick When the calorimeter's heat capacity is given in kJ K−1 for the whole assembly, no water mass or specific heat is needed. Multiply by ΔT and the heat drops straight out.
Answer: 35 g of gas burnt
80Among the following the number of state variables is ______: Internal energy (U), Volume (V), Heat (q), Enthalpy (H). (JEE Main 2022, 28 July Shift 2, Level-6)Counting state functionsTHERMODYNAMICS - 03 || PATH FUNCTION & STATE FUNCTION.🟢 Exact3
Concept A state variable is fixed by the present condition of the system alone. Heat and work are the two classic exceptions: they describe energy in transit along a particular route, not a property the system possesses.
Key relation State functions: U, H, S, G, A, T, P, V  |  Path functions: q and w A system "has" internal energy but never "has" heat.
Solution
1. Internal energy (U) is fixed once the state is fixed, and ΔU is the same along every path between two states. State variable.
2. Volume (V) is a direct measurement on the system as it stands, independent of history. State variable.
3. Heat (q) is energy crossing the boundary during a process. Two different routes between the same endpoints exchange different amounts of heat, so it is a path function.
4. Enthalpy (H) is defined as U + PV, and since U, P and V are all state functions, so is H. State variable.
5. Counting the state variables — U, V and H — gives 3.
6. The isothermal expansion in earlier questions illustrates the point: reversible and irreversible routes between the same two states give the same ΔU and ΔH but quite different values of q and w.
7. A neat consequence: round any closed cycle every state function returns to zero, while q and w do not — which is precisely what makes a heat engine possible.
 Internal energy U   : STATE
 Volume V            : STATE
 Heat q              : path function
 Enthalpy H = U + PV : STATE
 ------------------------------------
 count of state variables = 3

 same endpoints -> same dU, dH ; q and w differ
⚡ JEE Trick Only q and w are path functions in this syllabus. Anything built out of state functions — H = U + PV, G = H − TS — inherits the property automatically.
Answer: 3 state variables (U, V and H)
811 mol of Hg(g) is combusted in a fixed volume bomb calorimeter with excess of O2 at 298 K and 1 atm into HgO(s). During the reaction, temperature increases from 298.0 K to 312.8 K. If heat capacity of the bomb calorimeter and enthalpy of formation of Hg(g) are 20.00 kJ K−1 and 61.32 kJ mol−1 at 298 K respectively, the calculated standard molar enthalpy of formation of HgO(s) at 298 K is X kJ mol−1. The value of |X| is ______. [R = 8.3 J K−1 mol−1] (JEE Adv. 2022, Paper-1, Level-6)Bomb calorimetry to ΔfH of HgOIIT JEE Chemistry Bomb Calorimeter & Tricks to solve Bomb Calorimeter questions easily by NV sir🟡 Similar|X| = 90.39 kJ mol<sup>−1</sup>
Concept The calorimeter gives the heat at constant volume for the whole sample, so it must first be reduced to a per-mole ΔU, then corrected to ΔH, and finally combined with the formation enthalpy of gaseous mercury.
Key relation q = CcalΔT; ΔH = ΔU + ΔngRT; ΔfH(HgO) = ΔrH + ΔfH(Hg, g) The combustion is Hg(g) + ½O2(g) → HgO(s), so Δng = −1.5.
Solution
1. Heat released: ΔT = 312.8 − 298.0 = 14.8 K, so q = 20.00 × 14.8 = 296 kJ for the 2 mol of mercury burnt.
2. Per mole at constant volume: ΔU = −296/2 = −148 kJ mol−1.
3. Gas correction: one mole of Hg(g) and half a mole of O2 are consumed to give a solid, so Δng = −1.5 and ΔngRT = (−1.5)(8.3)(298) = −3.71 kJ.
4. Reaction enthalpy: ΔrH = −148 − 3.71 = −151.71 kJ mol−1.
5. Relate to formation enthalpies: ΔrH = ΔfH(HgO) − ΔfH(Hg, g), so ΔfH(HgO) = −151.71 + 61.32 = −90.39 kJ mol−1.
6. Hence |X| = 90.39. Note the essential subtlety: the mercury starts as a gas, so its own positive formation enthalpy of 61.32 kJ mol−1 must be added back to reach the value referenced to liquid mercury.
 dT = 312.8 - 298.0 = 14.8 K
 q = 20.00 x 14.8 = 296 kJ  for 2 mol
 dU = -148 kJ/mol

 Hg(g) + 1/2 O2 -> HgO(s) : dn_g = -1.5
 dn_g R T = -1.5(8.3)(298) = -3.71 kJ
 drH = -148 - 3.71 = -151.71 kJ/mol

 dfH(HgO) = -151.71 + 61.32 = -90.39 kJ/mol
⚡ JEE Trick Divide by the number of moles burnt before applying the ΔngRT correction. The correction is already per mole, so mixing the two orders doubles it.
Answer: |X| = 90.39 kJ mol−1
82A fish swimming in a water body when taken out from the water body is covered with a film of water of weight 36 g. When it is subjected to cooking at 100°C, then the internal energy for vaporization in kJ mol−1 is ______. [integer] Assume steam to be an ideal gas; ΔvapH° for water at 373 K and 1 bar is 41.1 kJ mol−1; R = 8.31 J K−1 mol−1. (JEE Main 2022, 26 Jun Shift 2, Level-5)ΔU of vaporisationClass 11 Chap 6 | Thermodynamics 13 | Entropy Change in Phase Change in Equilibrium IIT JEE/NEET🟡 Similar38 kJ mol<sup>−1</sup>
Concept Vaporising water at its boiling point creates one mole of gas per mole of liquid, so ΔU falls short of ΔH by exactly RT. The mass of the water film is a decoy, since the answer is asked per mole.
Key relation ΔU = ΔH − ΔngRT with Δng = +1 At 373 K, RT = 8.31 × 373 = 3100 J mol−1.
Solution
1. Process: H2O(l) → H2O(g) at 373 K and 1 bar, with ΔvapH° = 41.1 kJ mol−1.
2. Gas change: one mole of steam appears from a liquid of negligible volume, so Δng = +1.
3. Correction term: ΔngRT = (1)(8.31)(373) = 3100 J = 3.1 kJ mol−1.
