4. Substitute: ΔU = 41.1 − 3.1 = 38.0 kJ mol−1.
5. The integer answer required is therefore 38.
6. The 36 g of water film is deliberately unused: it would give 2 mol and a total ΔU of 76 kJ, but the question asks for the value in kJ mol−1, which is independent of how much water the fish carries.
7. Sense check: the correction of 3.1 kJ is about 7.5% of the 41.1 kJ enthalpy, the usual size when one mole of gas is created at around 373 K.
8. Had the question asked for the total ΔU of the 36 g film, the answer would be 2 mol × 38 = 76 kJ; the per-mole phrasing is what makes the mass irrelevant.
 H2O(l) -> H2O(g)   at 373 K, 1 bar
 dvapH = 41.1 kJ/mol      dn_g = +1

 dn_g R T = (1)(8.31)(373) = 3100 J = 3.1 kJ
 dU = 41.1 - 3.1 = 38.0 kJ/mol

 the 36 g film is a decoy (answer is per mole)
⚡ JEE Trick Read the units of the answer before using every number in the stem. A quantity asked "in kJ mol−1" never needs the sample mass.
Answer: ΔU = 38 kJ mol−1
83For combustion of one mole of magnesium in an open container at 300 K and 1 bar pressure, ΔCH° = −601.70 kJ mol−1, the magnitude of change in internal energy for the reaction is ______ kJ. (Nearest integer) (R = 8.3 J K−1 mol−1) (JEE Main 2022, 28 Jun Shift 2, Level-5)ΔU for magnesium combustionMeasurements of ∆U and ∆H || Calorimetery || Thermodynamics Part - 5 || Class-11 | Chemistry🟡 Similar600 kJ (ΔU = −600.5)
Concept Burning magnesium consumes half a mole of oxygen and produces a solid, so the system contracts. The internal energy change is therefore slightly less exothermic than the enthalpy change.
Key relation ΔU = ΔH − ΔngRT Only gaseous species count, and the sole gas here is the oxygen consumed.
Solution
1. Reaction: Mg(s) + ½O2(g) → MgO(s), with ΔCH° = −601.70 kJ mol−1 at 300 K.
2. Count the gases: none produced, half a mole consumed, so Δng = −0.5.
3. Correction term: ΔngRT = (−0.5)(8.3)(300) = −1245 J = −1.245 kJ.
4. Substitute: ΔU = −601.70 − (−1.245) = −601.70 + 1.245 = −600.46 kJ mol−1.
5. The magnitude to the nearest integer is 600 kJ.
6. Direction check: the container contracts, so the atmosphere does work on the system. Some of the energy released therefore arrives as work rather than heat, making |ΔU| slightly smaller than |ΔH| — exactly the 1.2 kJ found.
7. The phrase "open container" matters: it fixes the pressure at 1 bar, which is what makes the measured heat an enthalpy in the first place. In a sealed bomb the measured value would already have been ΔU.
 Mg(s) + 1/2 O2(g) -> MgO(s)
 dcH0 = -601.70 kJ/mol    T = 300 K

 dn_g = 0 - 0.5 = -0.5
 dn_g R T = -0.5(8.3)(300) = -1245 J

 dU = -601.70 + 1.245 = -600.46 kJ/mol
 magnitude = 600 kJ
⚡ JEE Trick Going from ΔH to ΔU you subtract ΔngRT; going the other way you add it. Writing the equation in the direction you need before substituting prevents the double sign flip.
Answer: |ΔU| = 600 kJ
84The enthalpy of combustion of propane, graphite and dihydrogen at 298 K are −2220.0 kJ mol−1, −393.5 kJ mol−1 and −285.8 kJ mol−1 respectively. The magnitude of enthalpy of formation of propane C3H8 is ______ kJ mol−1. (Nearest Integer) (JEE Main 2022, 25 Jul Shift 1, Level-5)ΔfH of propaneEnthalpy of Formation Reaction & Heat of Combustion, Enthalpy Change Problems Chemistry🟡 Similar104 kJ mol<sup>−1</sup> (Δ<sub>f</sub>H = −103.7)
Concept Three combustion enthalpies give the formation enthalpy of propane by Hess's law. Burn the elements in the proportions the molecule needs, then reverse the combustion of propane itself.
Key relation ΔfH(C3H8) = 3ΔcH(C) + 4ΔcH(H2) − ΔcH(C3H8) Propane has three carbons and eight hydrogens, so four moles of H2 are needed.
Solution
1. Target: 3C(graphite) + 4H2(g) → C3H8(g).
2. Burn three carbons: 3 × (−393.5) = −1180.5 kJ.
3. Burn four hydrogens: 4 × (−285.8) = −1143.2 kJ.
4. Reverse the combustion of propane: −(−2220.0) = +2220.0 kJ.
5. Add the three: ΔfH = −1180.5 − 1143.2 + 2220.0 = −103.7 kJ mol−1, so the magnitude required is 104.
6. The accepted literature value for propane is −103.8 kJ mol−1, so the data reproduce it almost exactly. The negative sign is expected for a stable saturated hydrocarbon.
7. Note that the water is taken as liquid here, consistent with the −285.8 kJ mol−1 supplied; using the vapour value would shift the answer by 4 × 44 = 176 kJ mol−1.
 3C(gr) + 4H2(g) -> C3H8(g)

 3 x (-393.5)  = -1180.5
 4 x (-285.8)  = -1143.2
 -(-2220.0)    = +2220.0
 --------------------------
 dfH           =  -103.7 kJ/mol

 magnitude asked for : 104
⚡ JEE Trick Halve the hydrogen count to get the number of H2 molecules: eight hydrogens means four moles of H2, not eight. This is the single most common slip in formation-enthalpy cycles.
Answer: 104 kJ mol−1 (ΔfH = −103.7)
852.4 g coal is burnt in a bomb calorimeter in excess of oxygen at 298 K and 1 atm pressure. The temperature of the calorimeter rises from 298 K to 300 K. The enthalpy change during the combustion of coal is −x kJ mol−1. The value of x is ______. (Heat capacity of bomb calorimeter 20.0 kJ K−1; assume coal to be pure carbon) (JEE Main 2022, 26 Jul Shift 1, Level-5)Molar enthalpy from a coal burnIIT JEE Chemistry Bomb Calorimeter & Tricks to solve Bomb Calorimeter questions easily by NV sir🟡 Similarx = 200 kJ mol<sup>−1</sup>
Concept The calorimeter's temperature rise gives the heat released by the sample, and dividing by the moles of carbon burnt gives the molar value. Because the combustion of carbon involves no net change in gas moles, ΔH and ΔU coincide.
Key relation q = CcalΔT; Δng = 0 for C(s) + O2(g) → CO2(g), so ΔH = ΔU One mole of gas is consumed and one produced, so the correction term vanishes.
Solution
1. Temperature rise: ΔT = 300 − 298 = 2 K.
2. Heat absorbed by the calorimeter: q = 20.0 × 2 = 40 kJ, which is the heat released by the coal.
3. Moles of carbon: assuming pure carbon, n = 2.4/12 = 0.2 mol.
4. Molar internal energy change: ΔU = −40/0.2 = −200 kJ mol−1.
5. Gas balance: C(s) + O2(g) → CO2(g) has one mole of gas on each side, so Δng = 0 and ΔH = ΔU = −200 kJ mol−1.
6. Hence x = 200. Note that this is the one common combustion where no ΔngRT correction is needed at all — the bomb result is the enthalpy directly. The real value for graphite is −393.5 kJ mol−1, so this "coal" is far from pure carbon in practice.
 dT = 300 - 298 = 2 K
 q = 20.0 x 2 = 40 kJ

 n(C) = 2.4 / 12 = 0.2 mol
 dU = -40 / 0.2 = -200 kJ/mol

 C(s) + O2(g) -> CO2(g) : dn_g = 0
 dH = dU = -200 kJ/mol   ->  x = 200
⚡ JEE Trick Check Δng before reaching for the correction. Carbon combustion, and any reaction with equal gas moles on both sides, gives ΔH = ΔU exactly.
Answer: x = 200 (ΔH = −200 kJ mol−1)
86The correct option(s) about entropy (S) is(are) [R = gas constant, F = Faraday constant, T = Temperature]: [MULTI CORRECT]
(a) For the reaction M(s) + 2H+(aq) → H2(g) + M2+(aq), if dEcell/dT = R/F, then the entropy change of the reaction is R   (b) The cell reaction Pt(s)|H2(g, 1 bar)|H+(aq, 0.01 M)||H+(aq, 0.1 M)|H2(g, 1 bar)|Pt(s) is an entropy driven process   (c) For racemization of an optically active compound, ΔS > 0   (d) ΔS > 0 for [Ni(H2O)6]2+ + 3 en → [Ni(en)3]2+ + 6H2O   (JEE Adv. 2022, Paper-2, Level-5)		Entropy across four contextsThermodynamic Functions of Cell Reaction || Most Expected Topic of JEE Advanced to Know!!⚡💯🟡 Similar(b), (c), (d)
Concept Four unrelated entropy claims. The first is a quantitative test of the temperature-coefficient relation, the second turns on what drives a concentration cell, and the last two are counting arguments about disorder.
Key relation ΔS = nF(dEcell/dT)P; a concentration cell has ΔH = 0, so ΔG = −TΔS The number of electrons n is what makes statement (a) fail.
Solution
1. (a) For M(s) + 2H+ → H2 + M2+, two electrons transfer so n = 2. With dE/dT = R/F, ΔS = nF(R/F) = 2R, not R. The statement is false.
2. (b) In the given cell both electrodes are hydrogen at 1 bar; only the H+ concentrations differ, from 0.01 M to 0.1 M. Chemically nothing changes, so ΔH = 0.
3. With ΔH = 0, the entire driving force is ΔG = −TΔS, coming from the dilution of the concentrated solution. The cell is therefore entropy driven — true.
4. (c) Racemisation converts a single enantiomer into an equimolar mixture of two, and mixing two distinguishable species always raises the entropy by R ln 2 per mole. True.
5. (d) [Ni(H2O)6]2+ + 3en → [Ni(en)3]2+ + 6H2O turns four particles into seven, so the entropy rises. True — this is the chelate effect.
6. The correct set is therefore b, c and d, with (a) failing only on the factor of n.
 (a) n = 2, dS = nF(R/F) = 2R, not R    FALSE
 (b) concentration cell: dH = 0
     dG = -T dS  ->  entropy driven      TRUE
 (c) 1 enantiomer -> 2 : dS = R ln2 > 0  TRUE
 (d) 4 particles -> 7  : chelate effect  TRUE
⚡ JEE Trick The chelate effect is fundamentally an entropy effect: replacing several monodentate ligands with fewer polydentate ones always increases the particle count, and hence the disorder.
Answer: (b), (c) and (d)
87At 25°C and 1 atm pressure, the enthalpy of combustion of benzene(l) and acetylene(g) are −3268 kJ mol−1 and −1300 kJ mol−1, respectively. The change in enthalpy for the reaction 3C2H2(g) → C6H6(l) is:
(a) +324 kJ mol−1   (b) +632 kJ mol−1   (c) −632 kJ mol−1   (d) −732 kJ mol−1   (JEE Main 2022, 25 Jun Shift 2, Level-3)		Trimerisation of acetyleneHess's Law Problems & Enthalpy Change - Chemistry🟡 Similarc
Concept When only combustion enthalpies are available, the reaction enthalpy is reactants minus products — the reverse of the usual formation-enthalpy rule. Burning the acetylene and un-burning the benzene assembles the trimerisation.
Key relation ΔrH = ΣΔcH(reactants) − ΣΔcH(products) Both routes end at the same CO2 and H2O, so the difference is the reaction itself.
Solution
1. Reaction: 3C2H2(g) → C6H6(l). Both sides contain six carbons and six hydrogens, so the equation is balanced.
2. Reactants: burning three moles of acetylene releases 3 × (−1300) = −3900 kJ.
3. Products: burning one mole of benzene releases −3268 kJ.
4. Apply the combustion rule: ΔrH = −3900 − (−3268) = −632 kJ mol−1, which is option (c).
5. The sign is right: acetylene is far less stable than benzene, so combining three molecules into an aromatic ring releases a great deal of energy.
6. Note the reversed subtraction. With combustion enthalpies it is reactants minus products, the opposite of the products-minus-reactants rule used with formation enthalpies — option (b) is precisely the trap for anyone who forgets that.
 3C2H2(g) -> C6H6(l)

 reactants : 3(-1300) = -3900 kJ
 products  :            -3268 kJ
 ------------------------------------
 drH = -3900 + 3268 = -632 kJ/mol

 combustion data: REACTANTS minus PRODUCTS
⚡ JEE Trick Formation data subtract products from reactants one way, combustion data the other. Write the rule down before substituting, or the sign will come out inverted.
Answer: (c) −632 kJ mol−1
8817.0 g of NH3 completely vapourises at −33.42°C and 1 bar pressure and the enthalpy change in the process is 23.4 kJ mol−1. The enthalpy change for the vapourisation of 85 g of NH3 under the same conditions is ______ kJ. (JEE Main 2022, 29 Jun Shift 1, Level-3)Scaling ΔHvap to 5 molesClass 11 Chap 6 | Thermodynamics 13 | Entropy Change in Phase Change in Equilibrium IIT JEE/NEET🟡 Similar117 kJ
Concept Enthalpy of vaporisation is an extensive quantity quoted per mole, so scaling to a different mass is simply a matter of counting moles. The temperature and pressure are held fixed, so the molar value does not change.
Key relation ΔH = n × ΔvapH, with n = mass/M M(NH3) = 14 + 3 = 17 g mol−1, so 17.0 g is exactly one mole.
Solution
1. The stem states that 17.0 g of NH3 vaporises with an enthalpy change of 23.4 kJ mol−1.
2. Since the molar mass of ammonia is 17 g mol−1, that 17.0 g is exactly 1 mol — which is why the two numbers were given together.
3. Moles in 85 g: n = 85/17 = 5 mol.
4. Under the same conditions the molar enthalpy is unchanged, so ΔH = 5 × 23.4.
5. Evaluate: ΔH = 117 kJ.
6. The value is positive because vaporisation absorbs heat. Note also how small 23.4 kJ mol−1 is compared with water's 40.7 — ammonia's hydrogen bonding is much weaker, which is why it boils at −33 °C.
7. If ΔU were wanted instead, five moles of vapour appear, so ΔU = 117 − 5(8.314)(239.7)/1000 = 117 − 10.0 = 107 kJ at the boiling point of −33.42 °C.
8. The pressure and temperature are stated only to confirm that the same molar value applies to both samples; neither enters the arithmetic.
 M(NH3) = 14 + 3 = 17 g/mol
 17.0 g = 1 mol   ->  dH = 23.4 kJ

 85 g / 17 = 5 mol
 dH = 5 x 23.4 = 117 kJ

 endothermic, so the sign is positive
⚡ JEE Trick Spot when the quoted mass is exactly one mole — the question is then pure ratio work. Here 85/17 = 5 is the whole calculation.
Answer: ΔH = 117 kJ
89When 600 mL of 0.2 M HNO3 is mixed with 400 mL of 0.1 M NaOH solution in a flask, the rise in temperature of the flask is ______ × 10−2 °C. (Enthalpy of neutralisation = 57 kJ mol−1, specific heat of water = 4.2 J K−1 g−1; neglect heat capacity of flask) (JEE Main 2022, 29 Jul Shift 1, Level-3)Temperature rise on neutralisationTHERMO | 17 | ENTHALPY OF NEUTRALISATION🟡 Similar54 × 10<sup>−2</sup> °C
Concept Only the limiting reagent determines how much water forms and therefore how much heat is released. That heat then warms the entire mixed solution, so the total volume enters the denominator.
Key relation q = nlimiting × ΔHneut; ΔT = q/(m × c) Taking the density of the solution as 1 g mL−1, the total mass is just the total volume in millilitres.
Solution
1. Moles of acid: 600 mL of 0.2 M HNO3 gives 0.600 × 0.2 = 0.12 mol of H+.
2. Moles of base: 400 mL of 0.1 M NaOH gives 0.400 × 0.1 = 0.04 mol of OH−, so the base is limiting.
3. Heat released: q = 0.04 × 57 = 2.28 kJ = 2280 J. Only 0.04 mol of water can form, however much acid is left over.
4. Mass warmed: the total volume is 600 + 400 = 1000 mL, so m = 1000 g.
5. Temperature rise: ΔT = 2280/(1000 × 4.2) = 0.543 °C.
6. Expressed in the requested form, 0.543 °C = 54.3 × 10−2 °C, so the value to fill in is 54. The excess acid contributes nothing but extra mass to warm, which is why the rise is modest.
7. Had the acid been limiting instead, 0.12 mol of water would have formed and released 6.84 kJ, giving a rise of about 1.63 °C — three times larger. Identifying the limiting reagent is the whole question.
 HNO3 : 0.600 x 0.2 = 0.12 mol
 NaOH : 0.400 x 0.1 = 0.04 mol   <- limiting

 q = 0.04 x 57 kJ = 2280 J
 mass = 1000 mL x 1 g/mL = 1000 g

 dT = 2280 / (1000 x 4.2) = 0.543 C
    = 54.3 x 10^-2 C   ->   54
⚡ JEE Trick Identify the limiting reagent for the heat but use the total volume for the mass. Excess reagent adds thermal ballast without adding energy.
Answer: 54 (ΔT = 0.543 °C)
90At 25°C and 1 atm pressure, the enthalpies of combustion are: H2 = −286.0, C(graphite) = −394.0, C2H6(g) = −1560.0 kJ mol−1. The enthalpy of formation of ethane is:
(a) +54.0 kJ mol−1   (b) −68.0 kJ mol−1   (c) −86.0 kJ mol−1   (d) +97.0 kJ mol−1   (JEE Main 2022, 24 Jun Shift 2, Level-2)		ΔfH of ethane from combustionEnthalpy of Formation Reaction & Heat of Combustion, Enthalpy Change Problems Chemistry🟡 Similarc
Concept Combustion enthalpies of the elements and of the compound combine by Hess's law to give the formation enthalpy of ethane. Burn two carbons and three hydrogens, then reverse the combustion of the ethane.
Key relation ΔfH(C2H6) = 2ΔcH(C) + 3ΔcH(H2) − ΔcH(C2H6) Ethane needs two carbons and six hydrogens, hence three moles of H2.
Solution
1. Target: 2C(graphite) + 3H2(g) → C2H6(g).
2. Burn two carbons: 2 × (−394.0) = −788.0 kJ.
3. Burn three hydrogens: 3 × (−286.0) = −858.0 kJ.
4. Reverse the combustion of ethane: −(−1560.0) = +1560.0 kJ.
5. Add the three: ΔfH = −788.0 − 858.0 + 1560.0 = −86.0 kJ mol−1, which is option (c).
6. The negative sign is expected for a stable alkane, and the magnitude is close to the accepted −84 kJ mol−1. Options (a) and (d) are positive and can be dismissed on chemical grounds alone.
7. Compare with PYQ 20, where slightly different combustion data gave −95 kJ mol−1 for the same compound. The spread reflects the rounding in the quoted values rather than any real disagreement.
 2C(gr) + 3H2(g) -> C2H6(g)

 2 x (-394.0)  =  -788.0
 3 x (-286.0)  =  -858.0
 -(-1560.0)    = +1560.0
 --------------------------
 dfH           =   -86.0 kJ/mol
⚡ JEE Trick Saturated hydrocarbons always have negative formation enthalpies. Any positive option can be eliminated before the arithmetic begins, which halves the work here.
Answer: (c) −86.0 kJ mol−1
91The standard entropy change for the reaction 4Fe(s) + 3O2(g) → 2Fe2O3(s) is −550 J K−1 at 298 K. [The standard enthalpy change for the reaction is −165 kJ mol−1.] The temperature in K at which the reaction attains equilibrium is ______. (Nearest Integer) (JEE Main 2022, 25 Jun Shift 1, Level-2)Equilibrium temperature for rustingCalculating the Temperature at Which a Reaction Becomes Spontaneous🟡 Similar300 K
Concept Equilibrium means ΔG = 0, so the temperature is the plain ratio ΔH°/ΔS°. Both quantities are negative for the rusting of iron, so the ratio comes out positive.
Key relation ΔG = 0 ⇒ T = ΔH°/ΔS° ΔH° must be converted to joules to match ΔS° in J K−1.
Solution
1. Reaction: 4Fe(s) + 3O2(g) → 2Fe2O3(s), with ΔS° = −550 J K−1 and ΔH° = −165 kJ mol−1.
2. The negative entropy change is expected: three moles of gas disappear into a solid, so Δng = −3.
3. Convert the enthalpy: ΔH° = −165000 J.
4. Set ΔG = ΔH° − TΔS° = 0, so T = ΔH°/ΔS° = (−165000)/(−550).
5. Evaluate: T = 300 K.
6. Interpretation: with both quantities negative this is a low-temperature reaction, so iron rusts spontaneously below 300 K and the oxide becomes unstable above it. That is precisely why iron oxide can be reduced back to iron in a hot blast furnace.
7. Check either side: at 200 K, ΔG = −165000 + 110000 = −55 kJ, spontaneous; at 400 K, ΔG = −165000 + 220000 = +55 kJ, non-spontaneous. The switch sits at 300 K as found.
 4Fe(s) + 3O2(g) -> 2Fe2O3(s)
 dH0 = -165000 J    dS0 = -550 J/K

 dG = 0  ->  T = -165000 / -550 = 300 K

 both negative : spontaneous BELOW 300 K
⚡ JEE Trick Two negatives divide to a positive temperature. If your crossover comes out negative, one of the two signs has been dropped — usually the entropy's.
Answer: T = 300 K
924.0 L of an ideal gas is allowed to expand isothermally into vacuum until the total volume is 20 L. The amount of heat absorbed in this expansion is ______ L atm. (JEE Main 2022, 28 Jun Shift 1, Level-2)Heat in a free expansionExpansion of a gas in vacuum is called free expansion. Calculate the work done and the change in ...🟢 Exact0 L atm
Concept Expansion into a vacuum meets no opposing pressure, so no work is done however large the volume change. For an ideal gas held at constant temperature the internal energy is also unchanged, which forces the heat to be zero as well.
Key relation pex = 0 ⇒ w = 0; isothermal ideal gas ⇒ ΔU = 0; hence q = −w = 0 Free expansion is the one case where both q and w vanish together.
Solution
1. The gas expands from 4.0 L to a total of 20 L, so ΔV = 16 L — a large change.
2. But the expansion is into vacuum, so the external pressure is zero throughout: w = −pexΔV = 0.
3. The gas is ideal and the process isothermal, so its internal energy depends only on temperature and ΔU = 0.
4. First law: ΔU = q + w gives 0 = q + 0.
5. Hence the heat absorbed is q = 0 L atm.
6. This is worth contrasting with a reversible expansion over the same volumes, which would absorb nRT ln 5 of heat and do exactly that much work. The endpoints are identical, yet q and w differ completely — the clearest demonstration that both are path functions. Only the entropy still rises, by nR ln 5.
7. Note the word "isothermal" is doing real work here. For a real gas the free expansion would cool slightly through the Joule–Thomson effect, and a small heat flow would be needed to hold the temperature steady.
 4.0 L -> 20 L into vacuum

 pex = 0        ->  w = 0
 isothermal ideal ->  dU = 0
 dU = q + w     ->  q = 0 L atm

 reversible instead: q = nRT ln5, w = -nRT ln5
 either way dS = nR ln5 > 0
⚡ JEE Trick "Into vacuum" sets w = 0 immediately; adding "isothermal ideal gas" then sets q = 0 too. The large volume change is deliberately there to tempt a logarithm that has no place here.
Answer: q = 0 L atm
93Which of the following relation is not correct?
(a) ΔH = ΔU − PΔV   (b) ΔU = q + W   (c) ΔSsys + ΔSsurr ≥ 0   (d) ΔG = ΔH − TΔS   (JEE Main 2022, 28 July Shift 1, Level-1)		Spotting the sign error in ΔHTHERMODYNAMICS - 19 || GIBBS FREE ENERGY (∆G = ∆H - T∆S )🟡 Similara
Concept Three of the four are standard relations of the chapter and one has a sign inverted. Checking the definition H = U + PV settles the matter in a single line.
Key relation H = U + PV, so ΔH = ΔU + PΔV at constant pressure The printed option (a) subtracts where the definition adds.
Solution
1. (a) "ΔH = ΔU − PΔV". From H = U + PV, differencing at constant pressure gives ΔH = ΔU + PΔV. The printed sign is reversed, so this is not correct.
2. (b) "ΔU = q + W" is the first law in the modern sign convention, with W the work done on the system. Correct.
3. (c) "ΔSsys + ΔSsurr ≥ 0" is the second law, with equality for a reversible change and strict inequality for any real one. Correct.
4. (d) "ΔG = ΔH − TΔS" is the Gibbs–Helmholtz equation. Correct.
5. So the relation that is not correct is (a).
6. A quick physical test confirms it: when a gas expands at constant pressure it does work on the surroundings, so more heat must be supplied at constant pressure than at constant volume. ΔH must therefore exceed ΔU when ΔV is positive, which requires the plus sign.
 H = U + PV
 dH = dU + P dV        (constant P)

 (a) dH = dU - P dV        WRONG sign
 (b) dU = q + W            first law   OK
 (c) dS_sys + dS_surr >= 0 second law  OK
 (d) dG = dH - T dS        Gibbs       OK
⚡ JEE Trick Test any suspect ΔH–ΔU relation on an expanding gas. Expansion must make ΔH larger than ΔU, which fixes the sign of the PV term without any algebra.
Answer: (a) ΔH = ΔU − PΔV is the incorrect relation
94C(s) + O2(g) → CO2(g) + 400 kJ; C(s) + ½O2(g) → CO(g) + 100 kJ. When coal of purity 60% is allowed to burn in presence of insufficient oxygen, 60% of carbon is converted into 'CO' and the remaining is converted into 'CO2'. The heat generated when 0.6 kg of coal is burnt is:
(a) 1600 kJ   (b) 3200 kJ   (c) 4400 kJ   (d) 6600 kJ   (JEE Main 2022, 29 Jul Shift 2, Level-1)		Partial combustion of impure coalTricks to solve Thermochemistry problems easily | Enthalpy of formation combustion🟡 Similard
Concept Two successive percentages have to be applied — first to find how much carbon the coal actually contains, then to split that carbon between the two combustion products. Each portion is then multiplied by its own heat.
Key relation C → CO2 releases 400 kJ mol−1; C → CO releases only 100 kJ mol−1 Incomplete combustion wastes most of the available energy, which is the point of the question.
Solution
1. Carbon content: 0.6 kg of coal at 60% purity gives 600 × 0.60 = 360 g of carbon.
2. Moles of carbon: 360/12 = 30 mol.
3. Split it: 60% goes to CO, that is 18 mol, and the remaining 40% goes to CO2, that is 12 mol.
4. Heat from the CO portion: 18 × 100 = 1800 kJ.
5. Heat from the CO2 portion: 12 × 400 = 4800 kJ.
6. Total: 1800 + 4800 = 6600 kJ, which is option (d). Note how costly the incomplete burning is: had all 30 mol reached CO2, the yield would have been 12000 kJ, so nearly half the energy is lost to the carbon monoxide.
7. The 40% impurity contributes nothing at all — it is inert ballast, which is why the purity had to be applied before anything else.
8. Distractor check: option (b) 3200 kJ would follow from ignoring the purity split, and (c) 4400 kJ from reversing the 60/40 allocation between CO and CO2.
 0.6 kg x 60% = 360 g carbon = 30 mol

 60% -> CO  : 18 mol x 100 kJ = 1800 kJ
 40% -> CO2 : 12 mol x 400 kJ = 4800 kJ
 -----------------------------------------
 total                        = 6600 kJ

 all to CO2 would give 30 x 400 = 12000 kJ
⚡ JEE Trick Apply the percentages in order — purity first, then the product split. Combining them in one step is where the arithmetic usually goes astray.
Answer: (d) 6600 kJ
95Passage III. For X(s) ⇌ Y(s) + Z(g), the plot of ln(pZ/p°) versus 104/T is a straight line passing through (10, −3) and (12, −7), where pZ is the pressure in bar of gas Z and p° = 1 bar. Given d(ln K)/d(1/T) = −ΔH°/R with K = pZ/p° and R = 8.314 J K−1 mol−1. The value of standard enthalpy ΔH° (in kJ mol−1) for the given reaction is ______. (JEE Adv. 2021, Paper-1, Level-6)ΔH° from a van't Hoff slopeVan’t Hoff Equation Graph Trick | JEE Main 2026 Physical Chemistry🟡 SimilarΔH° = 166.28 kJ mol<sup>−1</sup>
Concept The plot is a van't Hoff line, so its gradient carries the standard enthalpy. Reading two points off the straight line gives the slope, and the supplied derivative relation converts it into ΔH°.
Key relation d(ln K)/d(1/T) = −ΔH°/R, with K = pZ/p° The x-axis is 104/T, so the raw gradient must be multiplied by 104.
Solution
1. Two points on the line: (104/T = 10, ln K = −3) and (104/T = 12, ln K = −7).
2. Gradient against the plotted variable: (−7 − (−3))/(12 − 10) = −4/2 = −2.
3. Convert to the gradient against 1/T: since the axis is 104/T, multiply by 104 to get d(ln K)/d(1/T) = −2 × 104 K.
4. Apply the supplied relation: −ΔH°/R = −2 × 104, so ΔH° = 2 × 104 × R.
5. Evaluate: ΔH° = 2 × 104 × 8.314 = 166280 J mol−1 = 166.28 kJ mol−1.
6. The positive sign is right: X(s) ⇌ Y(s) + Z(g) releases a gas, and a falling line on a ln K versus 1/T plot always signals an endothermic reaction whose K grows on heating.
 points : (10, -3) and (12, -7)

 gradient vs 10^4/T = (-7+3)/(12-10) = -2
 gradient vs 1/T    = -2 x 10^4 K

 -dH0/R = -2e4   ->  dH0 = 2e4 x 8.314
 dH0 = 166280 J/mol = 166.28 kJ/mol
⚡ JEE Trick Watch the axis scaling. Plotting against 104/T rather than 1/T hides a factor of ten thousand in the gradient — miss it and the answer is out by four orders of magnitude.
Answer: ΔH° = 166.28 kJ mol−1
96Passage III (continued). The value of ΔS° (in J K−1 mol−1) for the given reaction, at 1000 K is ______. (JEE Adv. 2021, Paper-1, Level-6)ΔS° from the interceptVan’t Hoff Equation Graph Trick | JEE Main 2026 Physical Chemistry🟡 SimilarΔS° = 141.34 J K<sup>−1</sup> mol<sup>−1</sup>
Concept Once ΔH° is known from the gradient, the entropy follows from any single point on the line. Substituting one pair of coordinates into ln K = −ΔH°/RT + ΔS°/R leaves ΔS° as the only unknown.
Key relation ln K = −ΔH°/(RT) + ΔS°/R The intercept of the van't Hoff line is ΔS°/R, but reading it off a truncated axis is unreliable, so use a known point instead.
Solution
1. Pick the point where 104/T = 10, which corresponds to T = 1000 K — exactly the temperature the question asks about. There ln K = −3.
2. From the previous part, ΔH° = 166280 J mol−1.
3. Enthalpy term: ΔH°/(RT) = 166280/(8.314 × 1000) = 20.0.
4. Substitute into the van't Hoff form: −3 = −20.0 + ΔS°/R.
5. Rearrange: ΔS°/R = 20.0 − 3 = 17.0, so ΔS° = 17.0 × 8.314 = 141.34 J K−1 mol−1.
6. The positive value is exactly what X(s) ⇌ Y(s) + Z(g) should give, since a gas is created from two solids. A figure of about 140 J K−1 mol−1 is typical for releasing one mole of gas.
7. Cross-check with the other plotted point: at 104/T = 12, T = 833 K and ΔH°/(RT) = 24.0, so ln K = −24.0 + 17.0 = −7 — exactly the value on the graph, confirming both ΔH° and ΔS°.
 at 10^4/T = 10  ->  T = 1000 K, lnK = -3
 dH0 = 166280 J/mol

 dH0/(RT) = 166280 / 8314 = 20.0

 -3 = -20.0 + dS0/R
 dS0/R = 17.0
 dS0 = 17.0 x 8.314 = 141.34 J/K/mol
⚡ JEE Trick Do not try to extrapolate to the intercept on a graph whose axis starts away from zero. Substituting one reliable point into the full equation is both quicker and safer.
Answer: ΔS° = 141.34 J K−1 mol−1
97One mole of an ideal gas at 900 K undergoes two reversible processes, I followed by II, as shown on a U/R versus S plot: process I runs from (p1, V1) at U/R = 2250 K down to (p2, V2) at U/R = 450 K at constant S, then process II runs at constant U/R = 450 K to (p3, V3). If the work done by the gas in the two processes are same, the value of ln(V3/V2) is ______. (CV,m = (5/2)R) (JEE Adv. 2021, Paper-2, Level-6)Equating adiabatic and isothermal work13 REVERSIBLE AND IRREVERSIBLE ADIABATIC PROCESS | IIT ADVANCED | JEE MAIN | CHEMISTRY CLASS 11🟡 Similarln(V<sub>3</sub>/V<sub>2</sub>) = 10
Concept The plot is drawn with U/R on the vertical axis and S on the horizontal, so a vertical line is a constant-entropy (adiabatic) step and a horizontal line is a constant-internal-energy (isothermal) step. Equating the two works gives the volume ratio.
Key relation U = nCV,mT = (5/2)RT for one mole, so U/R = (5/2)T; adiabatic w = −ΔU; isothermal w = −nRT ln(Vf/Vi) Reading the temperatures off the U/R axis is the key first step.
Solution
1. Convert the axis: with CV,m = (5/2)R, U/R = (5/2)T. So U/R = 2250 gives T1 = 900 K and U/R = 450 gives T2 = 180 K.
2. Process I runs at constant entropy, so it is reversible adiabatic with q = 0 and work done by the gas equal to −ΔU.
3. That work is (2250 − 450)R = 1800R joules.
4. Process II runs at constant U, which for an ideal gas means constant temperature — an isothermal expansion at 180 K. Its work is RT2 ln(V3/V2) = 180R ln(V3/V2).
5. The question states the two works are equal: 1800R = 180R ln(V3/V2).
6. Cancel R and divide: ln(V3/V2) = 10. The volume must expand by a factor of e10, roughly 22000, because the cold isothermal step is a very inefficient way to extract the same work.
 U/R = (5/2)T
   2250 -> T1 = 900 K
    450 -> T2 = 180 K

 I  : constant S, adiabatic
      w = -dU = (2250 - 450)R = 1800R
 II : constant U, isothermal at 180 K
      w = 180 R ln(V3/V2)

 1800R = 180R ln(V3/V2)  ->  ln(V3/V2) = 10
⚡ JEE Trick On a U–S diagram, constant U means constant temperature for an ideal gas and constant S means adiabatic. Translating the axes into familiar processes is most of the work.
Answer: ln(V3/V2) = 10
98An ideal gas undergoes a reversible isothermal expansion from state I to state II followed by a reversible adiabatic expansion from state II to state III. The correct plot(s) representing the changes from state I to state III is(are) (P: pressure, V: volume, T: temperature, H: enthalpy, S: entropy): [MULTI CORRECT] Options (a) P–V, (b) P–T, (c) H–S, (d) T–S. (JEE Adv. 2021, Paper-1, Level-5)Recognising the correct state-plots14- Graphical Understanding of Adiabatic Process | Reversible vs Irreversible | IIT Advanced| HP Sir🟡 Similar(a), (b), (d)
Concept Two expansions in sequence: an isothermal one, on which T and hence H and U stay fixed while S rises, and a reversible adiabatic one, on which S stays fixed while T, H and P all fall. Each plot must show both behaviours correctly.
Key relation Isothermal: ΔT = 0, ΔH = 0, ΔS > 0  |  Reversible adiabatic: ΔS = 0, T falls, H falls For an ideal gas H depends only on T, so H and T always move together.
Solution
1. (a) P–V. Both steps expand, so V rises throughout while P falls, with the adiabatic portion steeper than the isotherm. Correct.
2. (b) P–T. On I → II the temperature is fixed while the pressure falls, giving a vertical drop; on II → III both P and T fall together. Correct.
3. (d) T–S. On I → II the temperature holds constant while the entropy rises, a horizontal line to the right; on II → III the entropy is constant while the temperature falls, a vertical line downwards. Correct.
4. (c) H–S. The first leg is right: H is constant and S rises, giving a horizontal line. But the second leg must have S constant and H falling, since the gas cools.
5. The plot shows the second leg going vertically upward, which would mean the enthalpy rises during an adiabatic expansion — impossible when no heat enters and the gas does work. Incorrect.
6. The correct set is therefore a, b and d, with (c) failing only on the direction of the final arrow.
 I -> II  isothermal : T, H, U constant ; S rises
 II -> III adiabatic : S constant ; T, H, P fall

 (a) P-V  expansion, adiabat steeper     OK
 (b) P-T  vertical then falling curve    OK
 (c) H-S  second leg drawn UPWARD      WRONG
 (d) T-S  right then down                OK
⚡ JEE Trick On any H–S or T–S plot, a reversible adiabatic step is a vertical line and it must point downwards for an expansion. An upward vertical arrow is always wrong.
Answer: (a), (b) and (d)
99In thermodynamics, the P–V work done is given by w = −∫dV Pext. For a system undergoing a particular process, the work done is w = −∫dV (RT/(V − b) − a/V2). This equation is applicable to a: [MULTI CORRECT]
(a) system that satisfies the van der Waals equation of state   (b) process that is reversible and isothermal   (c) process that is reversible and adiabatic   (d) process that is irreversible and at constant pressure   (JEE Adv. 2020, Paper-1, Level-6)		Reading conditions out of a work integralCalculate Work for Reversible and Irreversible Expansion/Compression🟡 Similar(a), (b), (c)
Concept The integrand in the given work expression is the van der Waals pressure. Writing w = −∫PgasdV rather than −∫PextdV is only legitimate when the external pressure matches the gas pressure at every instant, that is when the process is reversible.
Key relation PvdW = RT/(V − b) − a/V2; reversible ⇒ Pext = Pgas For an irreversible change Pext is a separate constant and the integral takes a quite different form.
Solution
1. (a) The expression RT/(V − b) − a/V2 is precisely the pressure predicted by the van der Waals equation, so the system must obey that equation of state. Correct.
2. (b) Substituting the gas's own pressure into w = −∫PdV requires the external pressure to track it continuously, which is the definition of reversibility. The reversible isothermal case fits, and the integral can then be evaluated with T constant. Correct.
3. (c) A reversible adiabatic process equally satisfies Pext = Pgas at every instant, so the same integral applies — only now T varies with V along the path. Correct.
4. (d) For an irreversible change at constant pressure the work is simply w = −PextΔV, with the fixed external pressure outside the integral. The gas's internal pressure never enters. Incorrect.
5. So the expression applies to (a), (b) and (c).
6. The essential point is that the equation carries two separate pieces of information: the form of the integrand identifies the equation of state, while the very act of integrating the gas pressure identifies the process as reversible.
 w = -int dV [ RT/(V-b) - a/V^2 ]
              ^^^^^^^^^^^^^^^^^^
              van der Waals pressure

 (a) vdW equation of state          TRUE
 (b) reversible isothermal          TRUE
 (c) reversible adiabatic           TRUE
 (d) irreversible, constant P       FALSE
     there w = -Pext dV
⚡ JEE Trick Look at which pressure sits inside the integral. The gas's own pressure means reversible; a constant external pressure pulled outside means irreversible.
Answer: (a), (b) and (c)
100Tin is obtained from cassiterite by reduction with coke. Use the data below to determine the minimum temperature (in K) at which the reduction of cassiterite by coke would take place. At 298 K: ΔfH°(SnO2, s) = −581.0 kJ mol−1, ΔfH°(CO2, g) = −394.0 kJ mol−1, S°(SnO2, s) = 56.0, S°(Sn, s) = 52.0, S°(C, s) = 6.0, S°(CO2, g) = 210.0 J K−1 mol−1. Assume that the enthalpies and the entropies are temperature independent. (JEE Adv. 2020, Paper-2, Level-6)Minimum reduction temperature for tinAt what temperature does the reduction of lead oxide to lead by carbon becomes spontaneous? PbO ...🟡 Similar935 K
Concept Reducing cassiterite with coke is endothermic but releases a mole of gas, so it becomes spontaneous only above a crossover temperature. Both ΔH° and ΔS° must first be assembled from the tabulated data.
Key relation ΔH° and ΔS° from products minus reactants; Tmin = ΔH°/ΔS° Sn and C are elements, so their formation enthalpies are zero but their absolute entropies are not.
Solution
1. Balanced reaction: SnO2(s) + C(s) → Sn(s) + CO2(g), with one mole of gas produced.
2. Enthalpy. ΔH° = [0 + (−394.0)] − [(−581.0) + 0] = −394.0 + 581.0 = +187.0 kJ mol−1, endothermic as expected for a reduction.
3. Entropy, products. S°(Sn) + S°(CO2) = 52.0 + 210.0 = 262.0 J K−1 mol−1.
4. Entropy, reactants. S°(SnO2) + S°(C) = 56.0 + 6.0 = 62.0 J K−1 mol−1, so ΔS° = 262.0 − 62.0 = +200.0 J K−1 mol−1.
5. Both are positive, so the reaction needs heat. Setting ΔG° = 0 gives T = ΔH°/ΔS° = 187000/200.0.
6. Evaluate: Tmin = 935 K. Above this temperature carbon reduces tin oxide spontaneously — the thermodynamic basis of smelting tin with coke, and comfortably reached in a furnace.
 SnO2(s) + C(s) -> Sn(s) + CO2(g)

 dH0 = -394.0 + 581.0 = +187.0 kJ/mol
 dS0 = (52.0 + 210.0) - (56.0 + 6.0)
     = 262.0 - 62.0 = +200.0 J/K/mol

 T_min = 187000 / 200.0 = 935 K
⚡ JEE Trick Elements have zero formation enthalpy but non-zero absolute entropy. Dropping S°(C) = 6.0 or S°(Sn) = 52.0 because "they are elements" is the classic error in this calculation.
Answer: minimum temperature = 935 K
